Civil
Legal Family
Tunisia's legal system is predominantly civil law-based, derived from the French legal tradition with elements of Islamic law (particularly in personal status). The unified secular judiciary was established in 1956 upon abolition of Shari'a courts. The current constitution is the 2022 Constitution (adopted July 25, 2022 by referendum; published August 18, 2022), which supersedes the 1959 and 2014 constitutions. Official legal texts are published in the Journal Officiel de la République Tunisienne (JORT).

That bloc's boycott was cross-referenced to the previously-documented Development Plan 2026–2030 entry as the explanation for its July 10, 2026 vote drawing only 103 of 161 members. The rupture entry is accordingly upgraded from provisional/single-sourced to corroborated, and its "Reported" date line now distinguishes the French original from the English translation. Added Law No. 73-55 of August 3, 1973 establishing the Ordre des Pharmaciens' (CNOPT) modern professional-order structure (tracing to a 1947 Beylical decree, reorganized 1960, and amended six times through 2008) — distinct from the already-documented Pharmacy Law No. 85-91, which governs pharmaceutical activity rather than the profession's own governance; confirmed via CNOPT's own institutional-history pages and its published law text. Added Veterinary Profession Law No. 97-47 of July 14, 1997, which carved veterinary practice out of the 1958 joint physician/dentist/veterinarian statute (that statute's physician/dentist half was already documented as Law No. 91-21) into its own Order, implemented via Decree No. 98-1145 of 1998; confirmed via the Conseil National de l'Ordre des Médecins Vétérinaires de Tunisie's own published legal basis. Added Decree-Law No. 82-12 of October 21, 1982 establishing the Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens, plus a companion Pending Reforms entry — the OIT's May 4, 2026 call to replace it, following its May 1–3, 2026 Sousse council session — distinct from the already-documented July 2026 diaspora framework bill; confirmed via the OIT's institutional history and Business News/L'Économiste Maghrébin/webdo.tn/Gnet News coverage of the May 2026 session. Added Casino Games Decree-Law No. 74-21 of October 24, 1974 (ratified by Law No. 74-97), covering casino authorization and the Promosport sports-betting/lottery monopoly (Arts. 67–70) — a previously entirely uncovered legal area — plus a companion Pending Reforms entry, Minister Sadok Mourali's revised games-of-chance/sports-betting draft law targeting AML and match-fixing risks and unlicensed online operators; confirmed via CMS Expert Guides' Africa gambling-law survey, FocusGN, and African Manager, cross-checked against each other. What's new in v2.84 (August 20, 2026, same-day follow-up): The largest single expansion since this artifact's early versions: 24 new law-cards, raising documented items from 221 to 245. Where recent passes chased newly-published instruments day by day, this pass deliberately inverted the method — instead of asking "what was published this week?", it systematically audited the existing coverage for structural gaps: enacted, still-in-force codes and statutes that had never been given an entry, including several the artifact already referenced in passing without ever documenting. Three parallel research threads ran: a JORT/press sweep of August 14–20, 2026; a domain-by-domain gap audit against the 221 existing cards; and a status re-check of all 30 tracked pending bills.
Currency confirmed — nothing new enacted this window. Only one JORT issue appeared after No. 82 (August 14): JORT No. 83 of August 18, 2026 (27 pages), whose sole substantive normative act is BCT Circular No. 2026-7 — already documented in v2.82. Its remaining contents are appointments and honours (Presidential Decree No. 2026-208 of August 17, awarding the Ordre National du Mérite in education and science to 11 individuals; an Equipment Ministry order of August 10 appointing the head of the management unit for the El Menzah/Radès sports-infrastructure projects; an Industry Ministry order of August 6 naming a Presidency representative to the CPG and GCT boards). No law, organic law, or décret-loi was enacted between August 14 and 20, 2026 — the ARP closed its fourth ordinary session on July 29 and does not reconvene until October 7, 2026. One non-JORT regulatory act was noted and deliberately not carded as a standalone statute: a Ministry of Commerce price-ceiling decision on poultry (live chicken TND 5.300/kg; oven-ready TND 7.900/kg ex-abattoir, TND 8.950/kg retail), effective August 19–September 19, 2026, enforced under the existing Competition and Prices Law. A widely-shared report of "up to 3 years' prison for licence plates" was checked and excluded as enforcement communication on the existing Road Traffic Code Art. 88, not a new text.
24 structural gaps closed. Foundational codes: the Code of Real Rights (Law No. 65-5 of 1965) — the single most significant omission, Tunisia's substantive property code, previously conflated with the 1885 land-registration law inside that entry's date line; the Public Accounting Code (Law No. 73-81 of 1973), which governs actual budget execution beneath the Organic Budget Law; the Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Code (Law No. 76-18 of 1976), referenced twice elsewhere in this artifact but never carded despite underlying both the 2018 crypto prohibition and the pending Exchange Code reform; the National Business Register Law (2018-52), Tunisia's FATF beneficial-ownership instrument; and the Code for the Provision of Financial Services to Non-Residents (2009-64), the offshore-finance code. Professions: bailiffs (Organic Law 2018-9), completing the auxiliaires-de-justice trio alongside advocates and notaries; physicians and dentists (91-21); architects (74-46). Health & safety: communicable diseases (92-71), the statutory base of the entire COVID-19 response; tobacco control (98-17); ionizing radiation protection (81-51); disasters and civil protection (91-39), distinct from the state-of-emergency regime. Sectoral: physical education and sport (94-104) plus its companion Organic Law 95-11 on sports structures — the artifact had been tracking two sports reform bills without the law being reformed; anti-doping (2007-54); land transport organization (2004-33), source of the covoiturage/ride-hailing controversy; energy management (2004-72), the demand-side counterpart to Law 2015-12; livestock and animal products (2005-95), closing a total absence of veterinary/animal-origin food-safety coverage; vocational training (2008-10); travel agencies (Decree-Law 73-13), the operative text the three pending tourism bills would amend. Quality infrastructure & information governance: national standardization (2009-38) and legal metrology (99-40), both previously absent entirely; the national statistical system (99-32); the Archives Law (88-95), the records-retention companion without which access-to-information rights are hollow. Finally, Law No. 2016-68 on the National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad, whose amending bill this artifact had tracked in Pending Reforms since v2.18 without ever documenting the base statute.
Pending-bill status refreshed. All 30 tracked bills were re-checked; three carried genuine movement. The Exchange Code reform missed its pre-recess target: on July 23, 2026 Finance Committee chair Maher Kattari publicly rejected the Finance Minister's request to defer examination to January 2027 ("we cannot wait another year") and set an October 2026 chapter-by-chapter vote to proceed even without the Central Bank's input, the BCT having not replied since April 2026. The Labor Code / social-security reform advanced from ministerial intent to an active drafting mandate: an August 18, 2026 Conseil ministériel ordered immediate drafting of the legislative texts, with scope covering pension-regime revision and extension of coverage to informal, agricultural, self-employed and platform workers. The 2027 Finance Law file advanced to adoption of the budget économique 2027 on August 19, 2026 (H1-2026 growth 2.4%, unemployment 14.9%). No tracked bill was adopted in plenary, published in JORT, or formally withdrawn in July–August 2026 — the only plenary adoptions in that window were the 2026–2030 Development Plan and four loan-ratification bills.
Standing open items (unresolved, re-checked this pass): the cybersecurity bill n°2024-36 (no ARP committee movement located), the overseas-placement reform, the Redaoui/Hosni Gafsa appeal hearing date, the Soumoud case 2026/4010 trial date, and the May 2018 BCT crypto-directive's own citation number. Areas flagged for future expansion: deeper sub-decree and circular-level detail beneath primary statutes; the professional orders not yet carded (pharmacists, veterinarians, engineers); gambling, alcohol, and seed/biosafety regulation; and the full sommaire of JORT No. 83, whose routine arrêtés could not be read end-to-end because the official PDF is login-gated and the iort.gov.tn/pist.tn mirrors returned 503 throughout this pass.

v2.44 (August 7, 2026): A research pass roughly a day after v2.43, using the built-in web-search tool with direct article and WebFetch verification (Tavily remained unavailable — same HTTP 402 "account disabled" error persisting since v2.9). No new JORT text, floor vote, or promulgation was identified for August 7 itself (a routine Friday cycle issue could not be confirmed either way by this pass), and ARP remains in recess until the October 7, 2026 session. This pass finally closed the long-standing "no public accounting" frontier flagged in v2.42/v2.43 for the penal-reconciliation mechanism, and in doing so found a genuine, previously-undocumented gap: an entire amending law — Law No. 2024-3 of January 18, 2024 (adopted by the ARP 126–0, with 3 abstentions, published in JORT the same evening) — had gone completely uncaptured despite predating this artifact and substantially restructuring Decree-Law No. 2022-13 two years before the artifact's June 2026 discovery of the underlying mechanism. The amendment rewrote Article 30 to strip out the 2022 text's explicit Court of Accounts oversight of reconciliation receipts and replace it with a strengthened National Security Council role (the Commission president must now transmit each signed reconciliation report to the President of the Republic within 48 hours, and the National Security Council can approve, reject, or increase the sums claimed within three days); added two vice-presidents to the Commission (an administrative magistrate and a financial magistrate, each with 15+ years' seniority); set a formal valuation method (value of the misappropriated asset or advantage or damage caused, increased 10% per year elapsed since the underlying act); capped the Commission's examination period at four months; and distinguished "final" reconciliation (full payment, extinguishing public action under Art. 35) from "provisional" reconciliation (50% upfront, balance due within six months, with breach reviving prosecution). Confirmed via Le Temps News' contemporaneous January 19, 2024 report and independently corroborated via tuniscope.com's JORT-publication report and businessnews.com.tn's August 6, 2026 op-ed, which itself broke the news of Article 30's rewrite as its central transparency critique. Also added, with an explicit reliability caveat rather than as confirmed government data: Mondafrique's July 11, 2026 investigative report placing total funds recovered under the mechanism since 2022 at roughly TND 35 million — more than the Truth and Dignity Instance's own roughly TND 9 million recovered over its 2014–2018 mandate, but far short of President Saïed's original 2022 claim of TND 13 billion (a figure the same report traces to an oversimplified mechanical extrapolation from a 2011 Ben Ali-era asset-crimes commission) — and the fact, per the same and the lapresse.tn July 15, 2026 sources, that no official Tunisian institution has ever published a consolidated, verified figure, meaning the TND 35 million number is unofficial and un-cross-verified by a second independent outlet, unlike this artifact's usual two-source-minimum practice; it is flagged accordingly in the card rather than stated as fact. Also newly noted: the pre-2026 Commission was dissolved September 15, 2024, roughly eight months after the Law No. 2024-3 restructuring and about twenty months before the June 2026 relaunch under Ali Abbès — a gap in institutional continuity not previously documented here. Swept broadly for other August 6–7 developments (the Boughdiri "Maersk protest" appeal remains set for August 11, 2026, unchanged; the PLF 2027 project remains at the orientations stage per an August 4–5 news cycle already substantively documented, unchanged; the Exchange Code reform, Constitutional Court bill, and water/electricity-crisis censure motion are all unchanged from v2.43) and found no additional gaps. Total documented items remains 204 (this pass substantially enriched an existing card rather than adding a new one, consistent with this artifact's practice of keeping amendments to an already-tracked instrument within that instrument's card). Coverage remains comprehensive for enacted primary law; the main frontier for future expansion remains the 30 tracked pending bills (paused until October 7, 2026), continued circular/sub-decree-level detail beneath primary statutes, and independent verification of the TND 35 million penal-reconciliation recovery figure against a second outlet or an eventual official disclosure.

This pass tracked the Development Plan 2026–2030 approval bill — tracked as pending since v2.4 — to a genuine, significant milestone: the National Council of Regions and Districts (CNRD) held its own plenary vote on Friday, July 17, 2026 and adopted the bill 56 in favor, 6 against, 5 abstentions, one week after the ARP's July 10, 2026 plenary adoption (64–24–15). This closes the two-chamber legislative process required for plan-approval bills under the 2022 Constitution's Arts. 75 & 77 (first exercised for this type of bill under the new bicameral-adjacent architecture); confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie's July 17 and July 18, 2026 reports and cross-checked against Business News and L'Économiste Maghrébin coverage of the same vote. The entry's title, status line, and description are updated accordingly: with both required chambers now having voted, the sole remaining step is presidential promulgation and JORT publication, neither of which had been located as of this pass despite a dedicated JORT-listing search — so the entry is deliberately kept in Pending Reforms rather than moved to Administrative Law, consistent with the artifact's practice for the Artist Status Law and Finance Law entries at the equivalent pre-publication stage. ⚖️ Constitutional Law

Constitution of 2022Constitution of the Republic of Tunisia

Adopted: July 25, 2022 (referendum) | Published: August 18, 2022 | In force: August 2022
The current governing constitution, promulgated by Presidential Decree No. 2022-691 of August 17, 2022. Establishes a presidential system with a bicameral legislature (National Council of Regions and Districts + Assembly of People's Representatives). Supersedes the 1959 and 2014 constitutions as well as Decree-Law No. 2011-14 of March 23, 2011. Article 5 references Islam and "the aims of Pure Islam." Comprises 142 articles organized into 10 chapters.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1: Tunisia is a free, independent, sovereign state; Islam is its religion, Arabic its language, the republic its form of government
  • Art. 2: Tunisia is a civil state based on citizenship, the will of the people, and the supremacy of law
  • Art. 5: The state shall work to achieve the objectives of Pure Islam
  • Art. 29: Intellectual property is guaranteed
  • Art. 40: Freedom to form political parties, unions, and associations is guaranteed
  • Art. 42: Freedom of peaceful assembly and demonstration is guaranteed
  • Art. 86–90: President elected for 5-year term, limit of two terms
  • Repeals: 1959 Constitution, Decree-Law No. 2011-14, 2014 Constitution
Update (2026): The Constitutional Court mandated by Art. 118 et seq. has still not been formed, four years after promulgation. A prior organic-law proposal collapsed in April 2025 when sponsoring deputies withdrew signatures; a new proposal (draft law n°100/2025, filed by 21 deputies) was still under committee hearings at the Assembly as of February 2026, with no enacted implementing law or appointed judges. See Pending Reforms.
Constitutional

Parliament–Government Institutional RuptureNon-Implementation of Enacted Legislation & the Article 69 Dispute

Reported: July 29, 2026 (Nawaat, French original) / August 17, 2026 (English translation) | Independently corroborated: June 25, 2026 (Business News, parliamentary-bloc boycott) | Underlying pattern: ongoing through 2026
A Nawaat investigative feature — published in French on July 29, 2026 and in English translation on August 17, 2026 (this artifact previously cited only the later translation date) — documents a widening institutional rift between the Assembly of the Representatives of the People (ARP) and the executive: ministers routinely skip parliamentary plenary and committee sessions, leave written questions unanswered (some pending over a year), and — most consequentially — withhold the implementing decrees needed to operationalize laws the ARP has already adopted, effectively nullifying legislation after enactment. Concrete examples cited: Law No. 2025-9 (May 2025, banning labor subcontracting) is reported to remain without effective enforcement mechanisms in practice; Law No. 2025-18 (December 2025, exceptional graduate recruitment) had no implementing decree published as of the report despite this artifact's own June 2026 note on lagging rollout; and a February 2024 proposal to amend Decree-Law No. 2022-54 (cybercrime/disinformation) is reported to have stalled for over a year without action. The report quotes Finance Minister Michket Slama Khaldi's December 1–2, 2025 plenary-session warning to deputies over ARP-added amendments to the Finance Law 2026 — Article 27 (an interest-free microenterprise financing line) and Article 92 (health-assistant recruitment from waiting lists) — that "vous approuvez des propositions illégales. Si vous persistez dans cette voie, la loi de finances ne sera ni mise en œuvre ni appliquée" ("you are approving illegal proposals; if you persist, the finance law will be neither implemented nor applied"). The report also documents the Government Presidency's decision, in early May 2026, to suspend its members' and director-generals' attendance at Parliament for roughly one month, officially justified by preparation of the Development Plan 2026-2030. The government's stated legal basis for non-implementation is Article 69 of the 2022 Constitution, which bars legislative proposals threatening the state's financial equilibrium; Nawaat's analysis argues this narrow textual bar is being invoked as a general pretext for disregarding parliamentary legislation more broadly. Independent corroboration found this pass: Business News reported June 25, 2026 that the ARP parliamentary bloc "Pour que le peuple triomphe" formally announced it was suspending all cooperation with the government on every matter submitted to the Assembly, judging the government incapable of implementing the President's directives — a rupture from a second, independent primary source describing the same executive-legislative breakdown from the opposite side. That bloc's boycott is also the documented reason the Development Plan 2026-2030's July 10, 2026 plenary vote drew only 103 of 161 members (64-24-15), well under a full house. This entry is accordingly upgraded from provisional/single-sourced to corroborated.
ConstitutionalAdministrative2026

Constitution of 2014 [Superseded]Constitution of the Republic of Tunisia

Adopted: January 26, 2014 | In force: February 2014 | Superseded: August 2022
Post-Arab Spring constitution adopted by the National Constituent Assembly with 200 votes for, 12 against, 4 abstentions. Comprised 149 articles across 10 chapters. Established a semi-presidential system. Article 74 restricted the presidency to Muslims. Now superseded by the 2022 Constitution.
Key provisions (historical)
  • Art. 1: Tunisia is a free, independent, sovereign state; Islam its religion, Arabic its language
  • Art. 2: Tunisia is a civil state based on citizenship, the will of the people, and the primacy of law
  • Art. 20: Equality before the law guaranteed without discrimination
  • Art. 46: Enshrined women's rights and gender parity in elected bodies
  • Art. 74: Presidential candidates must be Muslim, Tunisian nationals, born of Tunisian parents
  • Art. 88: Mechanism for presidential impeachment
  • Art. 115: Prime minister removal required majority of assembly members
ConstitutionalSuperseded

Constitution of 1959 [Superseded]Constitution of the Tunisian Republic

Promulgated: June 1, 1959 | In force: June 1, 1959 | Superseded: August 2022
Tunisia's first post-independence constitution. Modeled on the French system, vested strong executive power in a president. Arabic declared national language. Presidents elected for 5-year terms. Amended multiple times — notably in 2002 (removed presidential term limits, opened candidacy to persons over 75). In force for 63 years until replaced by the 2022 Constitution.
Key provisions (historical)
  • Art. 1: Tunisia is a free, independent, sovereign state; Islam its religion, Arabic its language, the republic its form
  • Art. 39: Presidential term set at 5 years
  • Art. 40: President elected by universal direct suffrage
  • 2002 amendment: removed two-term limit, raised age ceiling to 75
ConstitutionalSuperseded

Decree-Law No. 2011-14 of March 23, 2011Provisional Organization of Public Authorities

Enacted: March 23, 2011 | In force: March 2011 | Superseded: August 2022
Established the transitional constitutional framework following the 2011 revolution. Organized interim executive and legislative powers pending the drafting and adoption of a permanent constitution. Superseded by the 2022 Constitution.
ConstitutionalSuperseded

Organic Law No. 2018-51 of October 29, 2018National Human Rights Institution (Instance des Droits de l'Homme)

Enacted: October 29, 2018 | In force: 2018 | Implements: 2014 Constitution Art. 128
Establishes Tunisia's National Human Rights Institution (INDH), designed to comply with the UN "Paris Principles" on independent national human rights bodies. Composed of 9 members elected by the Assembly of the People's Representatives (ARP) by a two-thirds majority, the institution is empowered to monitor human rights compliance, receive individual complaints, propose legislative reform, and publish an annual report in the JORT. As with the anti-corruption body INLUCC (see above), the 2022 Constitution removed the prior constitutional chapter dedicated to independent bodies, leaving unresolved questions about the INDH's continued constitutional footing and practical functioning as of 2026 — no verified decree has repealed or reconstituted it.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

📜 Civil Law & Law of Obligations

Code of Obligations and Contracts (COC) — 1906Majallat Al-Iltizamat wa-l-Uqud

Promulgated: December 15, 1906 (under French Protectorate) | In force: January 1, 1907
The foundational civil code governing contracts, torts, and obligations. Derived from the French civil law tradition, adapted for Tunisian context. Covers general theory of obligations, specific contracts (sale, lease, agency, loan, deposit, surety), and civil liability. Has been substantially amended multiple times since independence. Remains in force as the core private law instrument.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–106: General theory of obligations (formation, validity, effects, extinction)
  • Art. 13: Requirements for valid contract — capacity, consent, object, cause
  • Art. 62–80: Nullity of contracts (absolute vs. relative)
  • Art. 82–96: Effects of obligations; performance and non-performance
  • Art. 100: Damages for non-performance; principle of full reparation
  • Art. 222–262: Contract of sale (conditions, effects, warranty)
  • Art. 728–733: Civil liability for delict and quasi-delict
  • Art. 786–828: Mandate and agency
CivilAmended

Code of Real Rights (CDR) — Law No. 65-5 of February 12, 1965Code des Droits Réels

Enacted: February 12, 1965 | In force: 1965 | Amended: 1992, 1995, 1997, 2001, 2005, 2006, 2008
The substantive property-law code — the counterpart to the Code of Obligations and Contracts (COC) for real rights — distinct from the 1885 land-registration procedure law below, which it is often conflated with. 372 articles across five parts covering ownership and its acquisition, dismembered real rights (usufruct, use, habitation, servitudes), emphyteusis, and mortgages/real securities. Governs the registered-title (titre foncier) regime under which transfers of registered land take legal effect only upon inscription in the land register (effet constitutif). Post-1992 amendments introduced compulsory land registration (immatriculation obligatoire) and updated the state-owned agricultural immovables regime.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–26: General provisions on classification and nature of property
  • Art. 17–141: Acquisition and ownership, possession, and acquisitive prescription
  • Art. 142–190: Dismembered real rights — usufruct, use, habitation, servitudes
  • Art. 191 ff.: Emphyteusis (long-term real lease)
  • Art. 270–372: Mortgage (hypothèque) regime — constitution, rank, registration, enforcement
  • Registered-title system: transfer of registered land effective only upon inscription
CivilLandAmended

Code of Civil and Commercial Procedure — 1959Majallat Al-Murafa'at Al-Madaniyya wa-l-Tijariyya

Enacted: 1959 | Multiple amendments
Governs civil and commercial litigation procedures including jurisdiction, service of process, evidence, trials, judgments, and appeals. Regulates enforcement of judgments and provisional measures. Organizes appeals to Courts of First Instance, Courts of Appeal, and the Court of Cassation.
Key provisions
  • Rules on jurisdiction: territorial and subject-matter
  • Provisional and conservatory measures
  • Rules of evidence and burden of proof
  • Default judgments and opposition procedures
  • Appeals and cassation procedures
  • Enforcement of domestic and foreign judgments
CivilCommercialAmended

Law of August 1, 1957Civil Status (Registry of Births, Deaths, Marriages)

Enacted: August 1, 1957 | In force: 1957 | Amended: multiple times
Establishes the civil registration system for Tunisian nationals including registration of births, deaths, marriages, divorces, and changes of nationality. Mandates registration within specified time limits. Organizes the function of civil status officers (officiers d'état civil).
CivilAdministrative

Nationality Code — Law No. 63-6 of February 28, 1963

Enacted: February 28, 1963 | In force: 1963 | Amended: 1975, 2002, 2010
Defines Tunisian nationality by birth, descent, naturalization, and reacquisition. Originally limited transmission of nationality through the paternal line; amended in 2010 to allow Tunisian mothers married to foreign nationals to transmit nationality to their children. Governs loss, renunciation, and deprivation of nationality. A bill to tighten several of its provisions is under ARP committee review as of mid-2026 — see Pending Reforms below.
Key provisions
  • Art. 6: Nationality acquired by birth to a Tunisian father (jus sanguinis)
  • Art. 12: Nationality acquired through naturalization (5-year residence requirement)
  • 2010 amendment: children of Tunisian mother and foreign father may acquire nationality
  • Art. 30–35: Loss and deprivation of Tunisian nationality
CivilNationality

👪 Personal Status Law

The Code of Personal Status (CPS) is Tunisia's most internationally distinctive statute — it abolishes polygamy and replaced unilateral repudiation (talaq) with court-supervised divorce. It has been continuously in force since 1957.

Code of Personal Status (CPS) — Beylical Decree of August 13, 1956Majallat Al-Ahwal Al-Shakhsiyya

Enacted: August 13, 1956 (beylical decree under Lamine Bey) | In force: January 1, 1957 | Amended: 1959, 1966, 1981, 1993, 2017
Tunisia's landmark personal status code, principally drafted by President Habib Bourguiba. Abolished polygamy (criminal offense under Art. 18), replaced unilateral male repudiation (talaq) with court-supervised divorce, set minimum marriage age. The most progressive family law in the Arab world. Covers marriage, divorce, filiation, adoption (recognition of kafala), inheritance, guardianship, and wills. Never suspended or repealed despite political pressures (2011–2013). The 2022 Constitution has not altered its operation. August 13, 2026 marked the Code's 70th anniversary (also Tunisia's National Women's Day), observed with a UNFT conference on the post-2011 marriage contract (August 5, 2026) and an ATFD international seminar, "70 ans de CSP: où en sommes-nous de l'égalité?", at Tunis's Hôtel El Mechtel (August 12, 2026); coverage in La Presse de Tunisie and Kapitalis (both August 12, 2026) noted continued civil-society advocacy for inheritance-equality reform (the inheritance rules at Art. 85–199 below have not been amended toward equal shares since the 2018 COLIBE report, and no such bill was before the ARP as of this pass) alongside renewed commentary on informal/customary ("orfi") marriage enforcement — advocacy and commemorative context only, not a change to the Code's text.
Key provisions
  • Art. 3: Marriage requires consent of both spouses; minimum age 18 (with exceptions)
  • Art. 5: Marriage must be registered and conducted before a civil status officer
  • Art. 18: Polygamy prohibited and criminalized — up to 1 year imprisonment + fine for marrying while already married
  • Art. 29–37: Divorce only by court order; grounds include mutual consent, harm, abandonment
  • Art. 31: Either spouse may initiate divorce; court-ordered alimony mandatory
  • Art. 46–67: Filiation and parentage; paternity presumption
  • Art. 68–78: Legal guardianship (tutelle) of minors
  • Art. 85–199: Inheritance rules (miras) — blends civil and Islamic principles
  • Art. 154–165: Guardianship over persons under disability
  • 1993 amendment: mother gains equal custody rights; divorced mothers may become guardians
  • 2017 amendment: allowed Muslim women to marry non-Muslim men (circular, not codified)
Personal StatusCivilAmended

Child Protection Code — Law No. 95-92 of November 9, 1995Majallat Himayat Al-Tifl

Enacted: November 9, 1995 | In force: 1995 | Amended: 2002, 2008
Comprehensive statute protecting minors from abuse, neglect, exploitation, and trafficking. Establishes the Children's Judge (juge des enfants) with jurisdiction over child protection cases. Covers child welfare, delinquency, protective placement, and guardianship. Implements Tunisia's obligations under the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child (ratified 1992).
Key provisions
  • Creates specialized Children's Judges and children's courts
  • Art. 24–30: Protective measures for children in danger
  • Art. 70–80: Juvenile delinquency proceedings; preference for rehabilitation over punishment
  • Art. 101: Prohibition of child labor below age 16 in principle
  • Art. 105–110: International child abduction; return mechanisms
Personal StatusCivil

Law No. 58-27 of March 4, 1958Kafala (Legal Sponsorship of Abandoned Children)

Enacted: March 4, 1958 | In force: 1958 | Amended: 1994
Regulates the kafala system — the legal guardianship of abandoned children (Tunisia does not recognize formal adoption in the Islamic sense). Allows Tunisian families to take in abandoned children as legal dependents. Creates rights and obligations equivalent to adoption without severing the child's original lineage.
Personal Status

Law No. 2017-58 of August 11, 2017Elimination of Violence Against Women

Enacted: August 11, 2017 | In force: February 2018
Landmark statute criminalizing all forms of violence against women — physical, psychological, sexual, and economic. Abolished the provision allowing rapists to escape prosecution by marrying their victim. Created specialized police units and prosecution procedures for gender-based violence. Established protective orders and victim shelters. Considered one of the most progressive anti-GBV laws in the Arab world.
Key provisions
  • Removed Art. 227 bis of the Penal Code (marriage escaping rape prosecution)
  • Defines and criminalizes economic, psychological, and sexual violence
  • Establishes specialized prosecution units for violence against women
  • Provides emergency protective orders for victims
  • Mandatory reporting obligations for health professionals
  • National Observatory on Violence Against Women created
Personal StatusCriminal

⚔️ Criminal Law & Procedure

Penal Code — Law of July 9, 1913 (as amended)Majallat Al-Qanun Al-Jaza'i

Originally enacted: July 9, 1913 (French Protectorate) | Revised: 1964, 1968, 1987, 1993, 1999, 2004, 2010, 2017
Tunisia's criminal code. Enacted under the French Protectorate in 1913, substantially revised after independence. Classifies offenses into crimes (felonies), délits (misdemeanors), and contraventions (infractions). Organized in two books: general provisions and specific offenses. Notable provisions include criminalization of same-sex relations (Art. 230), blasphemy provisions, and offenses against state security. Amended significantly in 2017 following the Violence Against Women Law.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–59: General principles — criminal liability, complicity, recidivism, penalties
  • Art. 52: Capital punishment available for certain crimes (murder, rape causing death, terrorism)
  • Art. 72–85: Offenses against state security (treason, espionage)
  • Art. 86: Attacks against the head of state or government
  • Art. 121: Prohibited associations
  • Art. 214: Abortion prohibited except to save mother's life
  • Art. 218: Homicide and serious assault
  • Art. 226: Offenses against public morals
  • Art. 226 bis: Defamation of public officials via new communication technologies
  • Art. 227: Rape — minimum 6 years imprisonment
  • Art. 230: Consensual same-sex relations criminalized — up to 3 years imprisonment
  • Art. 231–245: Theft, fraud, embezzlement
  • Art. 305: Receiving stolen goods
CriminalAmended

Code of Criminal Procedure — Law No. 68-23 of July 24, 1968

Enacted: July 24, 1968 | Published: JORT No. 32 (August 2–6, 1968) | Amended: Law No. 87-70 (Nov. 26, 1987), Law No. 93-114 (Nov. 22, 1993)
Governs all stages of criminal proceedings in Tunisia. Organized in five books covering: public action and preliminary investigation, trial court jurisdictions, extraordinary remedies (appeals, cassation), special procedures, and execution of sentences. Article 1 establishes that any infraction gives rise to public action aimed at applying penalties.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1: Any infraction triggers public action ("Toute infraction donne ouverture à une action publique")
  • Art. 13–14 (as amended by Law No. 87-70): Limits on garde à vue detention
  • Art. 47: Preliminary judicial investigation mandatory for crimes (felonies)
  • Art. 57: Rights of suspects during investigation
  • Art. 85–87 (as amended by Law No. 93-114): Preventive detention — initial 6-month maximum, extendable
  • Art. 106–107: Extended detention provisions
  • Book II: Jurisdiction of cantonal courts, courts of first instance, courts of appeal
  • Book III: Opposition, appeal, and cassation remedies
  • Book IV: Military courts, terrorism courts, special jurisdictions
  • Book V: Execution of criminal sentences
Extradition: Tunisia has no standalone extradition statute — extradition and related international judicial cooperation are governed by the CPP's Book IV title on extradition (Arts. 308–335), most recently amended by Law No. 93-113 of November 22, 1993, covering conditions for extradition requests, procedure before the Indictment Chamber, and effects of a grant or refusal.
CriminalAmended

Anti-Terrorism Law — Law No. 2015-26 of July 7, 2015

Enacted: July 7, 2015 | In force: July 2015
Comprehensive counter-terrorism statute enacted following the Bardo Museum and Sousse beach attacks (2015). Replaced the 2003 anti-terrorism law. Defines terrorism broadly, criminalizes financing, propaganda, recruitment, and travel for terrorist purposes. Establishes specialized anti-terrorism courts and a national anti-terrorism agency (CTAF). Criticized by human rights groups for overly broad definitions.
Key provisions
  • Broad definition of "terrorist act" including threats to public order
  • Criminalization of glorification of terrorism and incitement
  • Criminalizes financing of terrorism and related financial flows
  • Prohibition on travel to conflict zones for terrorist purposes
  • Specialized Terrorism Chamber at the Court of First Instance of Tunis
  • Extended detention periods for terrorism suspects
  • Asset freezing without prior judicial authorization
CriminalSecurity

Organic Law No. 2017-10 of March 7, 2017Reporting of Corruption and Protection of Whistleblowers

Enacted: March 7, 2017 | Published: JORT 2017-020 | In force: 2017
Establishes the legal procedure for reporting corruption in the public and private sectors and creates protections for whistleblowers (lanceurs d'alerte) who report in good faith, including identity confidentiality and — per Government Decrees No. 2019-1123 and 2019-1124 — security protection and financial rewards. Reports were historically channeled through INLUCC, the independent anti-corruption body created by Framework Decree-Law No. 2011-120 of November 14, 2011. INLUCC was shut down by security forces in July–August 2021 as part of President Saied's emergency measures; a temporary head was installed by presidential decree in October 2022, but INLUCC's case files and independent functioning remain unresolved. The 2022 Constitution removed the constitutional chapter on independent bodies altogether, leaving Tunisia without a fully functioning, independent anti-corruption authority as of 2026 — no verified decree has formally re-established or renamed INLUCC. As of December 2024, the Interior Ministry reported extending security protection to over 50 whistleblowers under this law.
CriminalAdministrative

Organic Law No. 2017-62 of October 24, 2017Reconciliation in the Administrative Domain

Filed: March 20, 2015 (President Béji Caïd Essebsi) | Adopted after contentious debate: September 2017 | Enacted: October 24, 2017
A scaled-back survivor of Essebsi's original 2015 "Economic and Financial Reconciliation" bill, which had also proposed amnesty for businessmen accused of Ben Ali-era corruption in exchange for repatriating capital. Sustained civil-society opposition — the "Manich Msamah" ("I won't forgive") campaign, ICTJ, and repeated mass protests through 2015–2017 — forced removal of the businessmen provisions before the roughly two-years-delayed final passage. The enacted 7-article text is narrower: it ends criminal liability and pending prosecutions/proceedings against public officials and equivalent personnel for regulatory violations or harm caused to the administration in the course of their duties, provided they acted on a superior's orders and did not personally benefit (no bribes received). Distinct from, and predating, the broader Penal Reconciliation Decree-Law No. 2022-13 below, which revived and substantially extended the reconciliation concept to businesspeople and economic/financial offenses via presidential decree-law power after 2021.
CriminalAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-13 of March 20, 2022Penal Reconciliation and Allocation of Its Resources

Enacted: March 20, 2022 | Published: JORT, March 21, 2022 | 50 articles | Amended: Law No. 2024-3 of January 18, 2024 | First commission dissolved: September 15, 2024 | Relaunched: Decree No. 2026-109 of June 8, 2026 (chair) and Decree No. 131/2026, JORT June 26, 2026 (full membership)
Issued under President Saïed's post-July 25, 2021 exceptional decree-law powers, this 50-article text revives and broadens the reconciliation concept that civil-society pressure had stripped from the 2017 administrative-reconciliation law above, this time reaching businesspeople and economic/financial offenses directly. Article 6 covers misappropriation of public funds, violations involving state property, bribery, money laundering, and tax, customs, and exchange-control and financial-market offenses; any natural or legal person facing criminal charges or judicial/administrative proceedings for such acts committed before 2011 and up to the decree-law's JORT publication is eligible (terrorism offenses excluded). Reconciliation substitutes prosecution and trial with a monetary payment or the financing of a national, regional, or local development project, examined by a National Commission for Penal Reconciliation (CNRP) chaired by a third-rank judicial magistrate. A completed reconciliation ends the prosecution and cancels penalties, certified by the Prosecutor General (Art. 35); failure to complete it within the set timeframe resumes criminal proceedings (Art. 37). Recovered resources are earmarked for national, regional, and local development projects on equity grounds.
Amendment — Law No. 2024-3 of January 18, 2024 (adopted by the ARP 126 votes to 0, 3 abstentions; published in JORT the same evening): restructured the CNRP by adding two vice-presidents (an administrative magistrate and a financial magistrate, each with 15+ years' seniority); fixed a formal valuation method for reconciliation sums (value of the misappropriated asset, advantage obtained, or damage caused, increased by 10% per year elapsed since the underlying act); capped the Commission's case-examination period at four months; and distinguished "final" reconciliation (full payment upfront) from "provisional" reconciliation (50% upfront, balance due within six months, breach reviving prosecution). Most consequentially, it rewrote Article 30: the 2022 original had made the Commission's handling of reconciliation receipts subject to Court of Accounts (Cour des Comptes) oversight; the 2024 rewrite deleted that oversight reference and instead requires the Commission president to transmit each signed reconciliation report to the President of the Republic within 48 hours, with the National Security Council empowered to approve, reject, or increase the sums claimed within three days — a shift civil-society commentary (businessnews.com.tn, August 6, 2026) frames as removing the mechanism's one external financial-audit checkpoint in favor of presidency/security-apparatus control. The pre-2026 Commission was dissolved September 15, 2024, roughly eight months after this restructuring, before being relaunched in 2026.
The mechanism produced few concrete results in its first years, prompting President Saïed to relaunch it: he installed magistrate Ali Abbès — previously Procureur Général près la Cour de Cassation and head of the state's Contentieux de l'État — as the Commission's new president on June 10, 2026 (formalized by Decree No. 2026-109), with the Commission's full membership completed via Decree No. 131/2026 (JORT June 26, 2026). Saïed met Abbès again on August 5, 2026 to press for accelerated case processing, stating "the state's right to recover public funds does not expire" and warning against "procedural delays" being used to stall the process, while framing it as compensation and regional development rather than a general amnesty. No official Tunisian institution has ever published a consolidated, verified accounting of funds or projects recovered under the mechanism since 2022 (businessnews.com.tn and lapresse.tn both make this absence their central criticism as of mid-2026). One unofficial, single-source estimate exists: Mondafrique's July 11, 2026 investigative report puts total recoveries at roughly TND 35 million against Saïed's original 2022 claim of TND 13 billion (a figure the same report attributes to an oversimplified extrapolation from a 2011 Ben Ali-era asset-crimes commission) — a total the report says exceeds, but falls well short of, the roughly TND 9 million recovered by the separate Truth and Dignity Instance (IVD) over its 2014–2018 transitional-justice mandate. This TND 35 million figure has not been independently corroborated by a second outlet or official source as of this pass and is noted here as unverified, not as confirmed fact.
CriminalFinancialAdministrative

Law No. 2018-46 of August 1, 2018Declaration of Assets and Interests; Illicit Enrichment and Conflicts of Interest

Adopted by ARP: July 2018 (126 votes in favor) | Enacted: August 1, 2018
Requires elected officials, senior public servants, magistrates, and other listed categories of public office-holders to declare their assets and interests — in Tunisia and abroad, including those of spouses and minor children — within 60 days of taking office or of final election results. Declarations have two parts: an asset declaration and a declaration of interests, aimed at preventing conflicts of interest and detecting illicit enrichment. Over 350,000 individuals were estimated to fall within scope at adoption. Distinct from, but complementary to, the whistleblower-protection statute above (Organic Law 2017-10); both were historically administered through INLUCC.
CriminalAdministrative

Organic Law No. 2018-50 of October 23, 2018Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination

Adopted by ARP: October 9, 2018 | Published: October 23, 2018 | In force: 2018
The first law in the Middle East and North Africa to criminalize racial discrimination. Defines racial discrimination as any distinction, exclusion, restriction, or preference based on race, color, descent, or national or ethnic origin that impairs equal enjoyment of rights and freedoms. Drafted with civil-society groups ADAM for Equality and Development and Mnemty following high-profile incidents of racial violence in 2016–2018 (on Mnemty's president's own prosecution, unrelated to this statute, see the Saadia Mosbah case below). Criminalizes racist statements/hate speech (1 month–1 year imprisonment, fine up to ~1,000 TND) and incitement or organized racist acts (1–3 years imprisonment, fine up to ~3,000 TND). Implementation has been uneven — civil-society reviews describe "mixed results" — but the law has been actively used: 14 cases involving 18 defendants were heard in 2024. In November 2025, the UN Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination commended Tunisia's removal of restrictions on registering Amazigh names while raising continued concerns over protections for migrants and hate speech enforcement.
CriminalCivil

Law No. 2015-26 / AML — Law No. 2019-23 of March 6, 2019Anti-Money Laundering and Financing of Terrorism

Enacted: March 6, 2019 | Replaced: Law No. 2003-75
Brings Tunisia into compliance with FATF recommendations. Criminalizes money laundering and terrorist financing. Imposes KYC (know-your-customer) obligations on financial institutions, notaries, accountants, and real estate agents. Establishes the Financial Intelligence Unit (CTAF — Commission Tunisienne des Analyses Financières).
CriminalFinancial

Law No. 92-52 of May 18, 1992Narcotics and Psychotropic Substances

Enacted: May 18, 1992 | In force: 1992 | Amended: 1999, 2017
Governs narcotics and psychotropic substances including cultivation, production, trafficking, possession, and use. Imposes severe penalties — mandatory minimum 1 year for consumption (Art. 4), up to life imprisonment for trafficking. Article 4 on personal drug use attracted international criticism; the 2017 amendment introduced some discretion for judges in consumption cases.
Key provisions
  • Art. 4: Personal consumption of narcotics — 1 to 5 years imprisonment + fine (mandatory)
  • Art. 5: Trafficking — 6 years to life imprisonment
  • Art. 12: Confiscation of proceeds and instruments of offenses
  • 2017 amendment: judges given discretion on sentencing for first-time consumption offenses
Criminal

Organic Law No. 2016-61 of August 3, 2016Prevention and Combating of Trafficking in Persons

Enacted: August 3, 2016 | Published: JORT No. 66, August 12, 2016 | In force: 2016
Tunisia's first comprehensive anti-trafficking statute, enacted in the wake of the 2011 revolution and grounded in the UN Palermo Protocol (ratified 2003). Criminalizes all forms of human trafficking — sexual and labor exploitation, forced begging, organ removal, and servitude — with aggravated penalties where victims are children or otherwise vulnerable. Establishes the National Authority Against Trafficking in Persons (INLTP), formally constituted February 2017, which published Tunisia's first National Anti-Trafficking Strategy (2018–2023) and a National Report on Trafficking in Persons for 2021 (with UNICEF, released September 2022, recording roughly 1,100 identified victims that year). Distinct from the Irregular Migration Law below, which criminalizes unauthorized emigration by Tunisian nationals rather than exploitation of trafficking victims. The US State Department's Trafficking in Persons Report has tracked Tunisia annually through 2025 (Tier 2 / Tier 2 Watch List in recent years); no legislative amendment to the 2016-61 text itself was identified as of July 2026. See the content-creators trafficking case below for a July 2026 prosecution applying this statute alongside Decree-Law 2022-54.
Key provisions
  • Art. 2: Defines trafficking (recruitment, transport, harboring by force, fraud, deception, or abuse of vulnerability, for exploitation) and defines "vulnerability" (minors, irregular migrants, pregnant women, persons with disability, extreme necessity)
  • Art. 8: Base penalty — 10 years' imprisonment + TND 50,000 fine
  • Art. 23: Aggravated circumstances (child victim, pregnant woman, 3+ offenders) — 15 years + TND 50,000–100,000
  • Art. 24: Organized/transnational criminal group involvement — 15–20 years + TND 50,000–100,000
  • Art. 25: Trafficking resulting in death or suicide of the victim — life imprisonment + TND 100,000–200,000
  • Art. 44–46: Establish INLTP, chaired by a judicial magistrate, with representatives from Interior, Defense, Foreign Affairs, Human Rights, Social Affairs, Health, and Education plus two NGO seats
  • Art. 50–58: Victim protections — anonymous testimony, closed-door hearings, penalties for breaching victim confidentiality, and a one-month renewable reflection/recovery period with temporary residence rights for cooperating victims
CriminalNationality

Decree-Law No. 2022-54 of September 13, 2022Combating Offenses Related to Information and Communication Systems

Enacted: September 13, 2022 | In force: September 2022 | No formal amendment as of 2026
Criminalizes use of telecom networks to produce or spread "false news" or rumors that harm third parties, public order, or national defense (Art. 24: up to 5 years' imprisonment and a TND 50,000 fine, doubled where the target is a public official). Criticized by UN Special Rapporteurs (January 2023), Amnesty International, ICJ, ARTICLE 19, and the National Syndicate of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT) for vague language enabling prosecution of journalists, lawyers, and activists. Parliamentary attempts to revise it (2023, 2025) were both blocked; a third attempt has been under review in the ARP's General Legislation Committee since around January 2026, but as of March 2026 press reporting described the executive as employing delay tactics ("joue la montre") to stall the revision, and no amendment had passed as of July 2026. Human Rights Watch documented at least 22 prosecutions of journalists/activists by December 2023; by 2024–2026 the Committee to Protect Journalists recorded the highest count of jailed journalists in Tunisia since it began tracking in 1992, including Borhen Bsaies, Mourad Zeghidi, Sonia Dahmani, Ramlah Dahmani, Ghassen Ben Khelifa, and Zied el-Heni. From 2026, authorities have increasingly paired Decree-54 charges with financial-crime allegations and administrative measures (asset freezes, dissolution proceedings) against independent outlets. Illustrating the pattern of repeat prosecutions of the same individuals, lawyer Sonia Dahmani — arrested May 2024, on conditional parole since November 2025 — was convicted a fifth time under this decree on May 25, 2026 (2 years, over a 2023 radio interview criticizing prison conditions, brought by the General Administration of Prisons), following earlier 18-month sentences for a 2024 remark about migrants and an April 2026 remark about racially segregated cemeteries and buses. On July 7, 2026, this decree was applied in a very different context — jointly with the Anti-Trafficking and Anti-Terrorism/AML Laws — against 18 social-media content creators in the content-creators trafficking case below.
CriminalDigitalPress

🏪 Commercial Law

Commercial Code — Law No. 59-129 of October 5, 1959Majallat Al-Tijara

Enacted: October 5, 1959 | In force: January 1, 1960 | Amended: multiple times (through 2007+)
Core statute governing commercial activities, traders, and commercial acts in Tunisia. Defines the status of traders (commerçants), regulates commercial registers, commercial agencies, and trade names. Covers bills of exchange, promissory notes, cheques, and commercial contracts. Has been substantially amended since 1960 to modernize commercial law.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–15: Definition of commercial acts and traders
  • Art. 16–30: Commercial register obligations
  • Art. 57–90: Agency and commercial representation
  • Art. 192–260: Bills of exchange (lettres de change)
  • Art. 261–290: Promissory notes (billets à ordre)
  • Art. 291–325: Cheques — form, endorsement, payment, protest
  • Art. 400+: Commercial leases (fonds de commerce)
CommercialAmended

Companies Code — Law No. 2000-93 of November 3, 2000Majallat Al-Sharikaat

Enacted: November 3, 2000 | In force: 2001 | Amended: 2002, 2005, 2009, 2016, 2019
Comprehensive statute regulating all forms of companies in Tunisia. Governs formation, management, operation, auditing, transformation, merger, and dissolution of companies. Covers: SARL (private limited company), SA (joint-stock company), SNC (general partnership), SCS (limited partnership), and cooperative societies.
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–50: General provisions applicable to all companies
  • Art. 51–128: Joint-stock companies (Sociétés Anonymes — SA): board of directors, general meetings, auditors
  • Art. 129–165: Private limited companies (SARL): capital minimum, management
  • Art. 166–200: General partnerships (SNC)
  • Art. 201–230: Limited partnerships (SCS, SCA)
  • Mandatory: external statutory auditor (commissaire aux comptes) for SA
  • Minority shareholder protection provisions
  • 2019 amendment: introduced single-member SARL (SUARL)
CommercialAmended

Collective Proceedings Code — Law No. 1995-34 of April 17, 1995Insolvency and Business Recovery

Enacted: April 17, 1995 | In force: 1995 | Amended: 1999, 2003, 2016
Governs insolvency, bankruptcy, judicial reorganization (redressement judiciaire), and liquidation of insolvent businesses. Establishes procedures for appointing judicial administrators and liquidators, protecting creditors, and rehabilitating viable enterprises. Amended in 2016 to expand preventive settlement mechanisms.
Key provisions
  • Preventive settlement (règlement amiable) for pre-insolvency situations
  • Judicial recovery (redressement judiciaire): 6-month observation period
  • Liquidation (faillite): asset realization and creditor distribution
  • Ranking of creditors: secured, preferred, unsecured
  • Art. 36: Suspension of creditor actions during reorganization
CommercialAmended

Consumer Protection Law — Law No. 92-117 of December 7, 1992

Enacted: December 7, 1992 | In force: 1993 | Amended: 2009, 2015
Protects consumers against defective goods, unfair commercial practices, misleading advertising, and price manipulation. Establishes the right to information, right to safety, and right to redress. Regulates consumer contracts including distance contracts and credit. Creates the National Council for Consumer Protection.
CommercialCivil

Competition and Prices Law — Law No. 2015-36 of September 15, 2015

Enacted: September 15, 2015 | In force: 2015 | Replaced: Law No. 91-64
Governs competition policy, prohibits anti-competitive agreements, abuse of dominant position, and anti-competitive mergers. Establishes the Competition Council (Conseil de la Concurrence) as independent regulatory authority. Regulates prices for essential goods and services.
Commercial

National Business Register Law — Law No. 2018-52 of October 29, 2018Registre National des Entreprises (RNE)

Enacted: October 29, 2018 | In force: February 6, 2019 | Replaced: Law No. 95-44 (commercial register)
Creates the centralized electronic National Business Register, replacing the court-held commercial register. Requires universal registration of all natural persons in commerce or a profession, Tunisian and foreign companies, legal arrangements, and associations. Establishes Tunisia's core FATF-compliance instrument: the mandatory declaration of ultimate beneficial owners (bénéficiaires effectifs). Art. 4 creates the Centre National du Registre des Entreprises, a public establishment with administrative and financial autonomy.
Key provisions
  • Universal registration duty covering commercial actors, companies, and associations
  • Mandatory beneficial-ownership declaration for AML/CFT compliance
  • Art. 64: repeals Law No. 95-44 of May 2, 1995 on the commercial register
  • Art. 55 & 57: up to 15 years' imprisonment and fines up to TND 100,000 for falsified filings or false beneficial-ownership declarations
  • Administrative fines of TND 1,000–50,000 for late filing or non-registration
CommercialFinancial

National Standardization System Law — Law No. 2009-38 of June 30, 2009

Enacted: June 30, 2009 | In force: 2009
Establishes the national standardization system administered by INNORPI (Institut National de la Normalisation et de la Propriété Industrielle — also responsible for industrial property under Law No. 2000-84). The minister responsible for industry sets the system's general orientation in consultation with stakeholders. Distinguishes voluntary Tunisian standards from mandatory technical regulations, and organizes certification and conformity marking, aiming to strengthen the national economy, facilitate trade, and protect consumer health and safety.
Commercial

Legal Metrology Law — Law No. 99-40 of May 10, 1999

Enacted: May 10, 1999 | Amended: 2008 (Law No. 2008-12)
Governs legal units of measurement and metrological control (type approval, initial and periodic verification of measuring instruments) to prevent commercial fraud and ensure measurement reliability. Creates the Conseil National de la Métrologie Légale and regulates the sale, holding, manufacture, repair, and import of measuring instruments. Administered by the Agence Nationale de Métrologie (ANM).
Commercial

Decree-Law No. 73-13 of October 17, 1973Travel Agencies Regulation (ratified by Law No. 73-68 of November 19, 1973)

Enacted: October 17, 1973 | Amended: Law No. 2006-33 of May 22, 2006
The operative licensing statute for Tunisia's travel-agency sector, administered through ONTT (Office National du Tourisme Tunisien) cahiers des charges — the base text that three tourism-sector reform bills currently before the ARP (see Pending Reforms) would amend, since no unified Code du Tourisme yet exists. Makes travel-agency activity subject to prior ministerial licence (agrément), with Category A agencies authorized to book tourist stays, sell transport tickets, organize trips and circuits, and assist tourists, and Category B agencies limited to a narrower scope. Requires financial guarantees and professional-competence conditions.
CommercialAdministrative

Electronic Commerce Law — Law No. 2000-83 of August 9, 2000

Enacted: August 9, 2000 | In force: 2000 | Amended: 2004
First statute to legally recognize electronic contracts and digital signatures in Tunisia. Establishes the legal equivalence of electronic documents and handwritten signatures. Governs online commercial transactions, electronic payment, and online offers. Establishes the legal framework for certification authorities issuing digital certificates.
CommercialDigital

Law No. 2024-41 of August 2, 2024Reform of Bounced/Dishonored Cheques

Adopted by ARP: July 30, 2024 | Published: JORT No. 94, August 2, 2024 | Entry into force: February 3, 2025
Major reform of Commercial Code provisions on cheques, enacted after sustained civil-society pressure (Human Rights Watch's June 2024 "No Way Out" report documented hundreds imprisoned over unpaid debts). Amends Articles 410, 410 bis–sexies, 411, 411 quinquies, 411 septies, 412, 412 ter–quater, and 732 of the Commercial Code. Introduced the "TuniChèque" digital verification platform. Central Bank data show a roughly 94% drop in daily cheque transactions in the year following the reform, with a corresponding surge in cash circulation.
Key provisions
  • Cheques ≤ 5,000 TND: prosecution for insufficient funds now requires a complaint from the beneficiary rather than automatic ex officio prosecution
  • Art. 411 (new): for cheques > 5,000 TND, penalty reduced to 2 years' imprisonment + fine of 20% of the shortfall (down from up to 5 years)
  • Bearer cheques abolished — all cheques must name a payee; endorsement limited to bank deposit/collection
  • New standardized cheque with QR code; per-cheque cap of 30,000 TND above which certified cheques are required
  • Banks must verify client solvency and set per-client ceilings before issuing chequebooks (BCT Circular No. 2024-14)
  • Art. 412 ter: caps interest rates on long-term loans (>7 years, after 3 years repaid) where the rate exceeds 8% of remaining capital
  • Transitional amnesty/regularization mechanisms for persons already prosecuted or imprisoned over dishonored cheques issued before publication
CommercialCriminal2024

BCT Circular No. 2026-05 of April 24, 2026Unified Electronic Cheque Platform — Adherent Institutions List

Entry into force: April 24, 2026
Implementing circular updating the roster of financial institutions integrated into the "TuniChèque" unified electronic cheque platform created by the 2024 cheque reform (Law No. 2024-41). Formally lists the 25 banks now adhering to the platform, which centralizes real-time solvency verification, QR-code cheque validation, and fraud/duplicate-issuance detection across participating institutions. Illustrates the ongoing circular-level implementation layer beneath the 2024 primary-statute reform, alongside BCT Circular No. 2024-14 (chequebook-issuance solvency checks, already cited in that entry).
CommercialFinancial2026

Cooperatives Law — Law No. 2005-94 of October 18, 2005

Enacted: October 18, 2005 | Replaced: Law No. 84-28 of 1984 | Complemented by: Social and Solidarity Economy Law No. 2020-30
General statute governing the formation, governance, and dissolution of cooperative societies, plus category-specific rules for six types of cooperatives (consumer, housing, artisanal, services, etc.); agricultural cooperation is separately rooted in 1963 legislation. The Social and Solidarity Economy Law No. 2020-30 of June 30, 2020 later created an overlay framework recognizing cooperatives, mutual societies, and associations engaged in economic activity as part of a formal "social and solidarity economy" sector eligible for dedicated state support. See also the distinct Community Companies framework below, a separate presidential-decree-law vehicle for collective regional enterprises.
Commercial

Social and Solidarity Economy Law — Law No. 2020-30 of June 30, 2020

Adopted by ARP: June 17, 2020 | Published: JORT No. 63, July 3, 2020 (p. 1399) | Implementing decrees: still substantially outstanding as of the most recent reporting found
Tunisia's first framework statute formally recognizing "social and solidarity economy" (économie sociale et solidaire, ESS) as a distinct third sector alongside the public and private sectors. Art. 2 sets its objectives: balancing economic growth with social equity, ensuring fair coexistence of public, private, and third-sector actors, formalizing informal-economy activity, and improving the economic and social inclusion of disadvantaged and marginalized populations (isolated rural areas, unemployed youth) through cooperatives, mutual societies, and development groups. The law rests on four principles: priority of people and social purpose over capital (within sustainable-development limits), democratic one-person-one-vote governance, voluntary inter-enterprise cooperation, and statutorily limited profit distribution. It brings nine categories of entities within the ESS umbrella: cooperatives (including agricultural mutual societies), agricultural/maritime development groups, mutual associations, microfinance associations, mutual insurance companies, economically-active associations, commercial companies (excluding sole proprietorships), economic interest groupings, and future entities the legislature designates. It creates a three-tier institutional architecture — a Higher Council of the ESS (Conseil supérieur de l'ESS) at the level of the Presidency of Government, a Tunisian Instance of the ESS (Instance tunisienne de l'ESS) under the sector ministry to run a public national database/registry of ESS enterprises, and an Observatory ("Dar-ESS") to produce studies, surveys, and a satellite national account for the sector. Implementation has lagged badly: Tunisian legal commentary (Leaders.com.tn, Tunelyz) documented the core institutions as still lacking their implementing decrees more than two years after enactment, into 2023, drawing criticism for the law's heavy reliance on regulatory texts that were never issued. The related but legally distinct Community Companies framework (Decree-Law No. 2022-15, later restructured by Decree-Law No. 2025-3) has since become the more operational vehicle for collective/regional economic activity, while the ESS law's own institutions remain largely dormant on the record reviewed.
CommercialAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-15 of March 20, 2022Community Companies (Sociétés Communautaires), as amended by Decree-Law No. 2025-3

Original enactment: March 20, 2022 (in force March 21, 2022) | Amended/supplemented: Decree-Law No. 2025-3 of October 2, 2025, published JORT October 3, 2025
Creates "community companies" — a distinct legal form from cooperatives, framed by the presidency as a "third pillar" of the economy alongside the private and public sectors. Under Art. 2, a community company is formed by a group of residents of a region to pursue collective economic activity for equitable local wealth distribution and regional development. Profit distribution is fixed by statute: 15% to mandatory reserves (until reaching 50% of capital), 20% to social/cultural/environmental activities, up to 35% distributable to participants, and 30% reinvested in the company. The 2025-3 amendment restructured the regime into two tiers — local companies (minimum 10 participants from one delegation, TND 5,000 minimum capital) and regional companies (minimum 15 participants across multiple delegations of a governorate, TND 10,000 minimum capital) — created a National Register of Community Companies (registration constitutive of legal personality, administered via an electronic platform), reformed governance around elected boards (3–5 members local / 5–10 regional, renewable 3-year terms), and added incentives: ten years of tax and duty exemption, VAT suspension, preferential bank financing via the National Guarantee Fund, and priority access to state agricultural land on 40-year leases with a 5-year rent exemption. Adoption has been gradual and is debated as more symbolic than transformative to date.
CommercialAdministrative2025

Chartered Accountants Order Law — Law No. 88-108 of August 18, 1988

Enacted: August 18, 1988 | Published: JORT No. 56, August 26, 1988 | Implementing decree: 89-541 of May 25, 1989
Establishes the Order of Chartered Accountants of Tunisia (Ordre des Experts Comptables de Tunisie — OECT), a civil professional body under Finance Ministry oversight regulating statutory auditors (commissaires aux comptes required for SA companies under the Companies Code) and accounting professionals. Sets membership conditions (Tunisian nationality, minimum 5-year standing, clean disciplinary record, recognized diploma), bans salaried employment and commercial activity or advertising by members, and creates a professional disciplinary chamber.
CommercialFinancial

Decree-Law No. 82-12 of October 21, 1982Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens (OIT)

Enacted: October 21, 1982 | Ratified by Parliament: Law No. 82-85 of December 2, 1982 | Revised: Law No. 97-41 of June 9, 1997
Establishes the Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens (OIT), the professional, administrative, and jurisdictional body regulating engineering practice nationwide, structured around a National Council and regional councils. Sets the conditions for the protected title "ingénieur," registration on the Order's roll as a precondition for practice, professional-ethics obligations, and disciplinary jurisdiction, alongside the Order's advisory role in estimating the country's engineering workforce needs within national economic and social development plans. Now over four decades old and, per the Order's own National Council, "obsolete": at its May 1–3, 2026 Sousse council session ("The Engineer, Pillar of Sovereignty and Development"), the OIT formally called on the legislative and executive authorities to accelerate adoption of a replacement law — see the pending reform bill below, distinct from the separate, already-enacted-track diaspora framework bill the Order also sponsored in July 2026. Confirmed via the OIT's own published history (Wikipedia-hosted, cross-checked against the Order's institutional presidency timeline) and Business News/L'Économiste Maghrébin/webdo.tn coverage of the May 2026 Sousse session, cross-checked against each other.
CommercialAdministrativeAmended2026

Casino Games Decree-Law No. 74-21 of October 24, 1974Ratified by Law No. 74-97 of December 11, 1974 — Jeux de Hasard, Promosport

Enacted: October 24, 1974 | Ratified: December 11, 1974
The foundational framework for licensed games of chance in Tunisia. Subjects casino-game operation to prior authorization granted by joint order of the Ministers of the Interior and National Economy — in practice restricting land-based casinos (e.g. Sousse's Casino Veneziano and Grand Casino Kantaoui, and venues in Djerba) to foreign-passport holders, with citizen access barred. Articles 67–70 separately created Promosport, a state-owned public establishment under Ministry of Youth and Sports oversight holding a legal monopoly over sports-prediction contests, lotteries, and related betting operations (including the Loterie Nationale and online sports betting), framed as promoting physical and sporting activity; gambling and online-gambling winnings are subject to a 25% withholding tax under Tunisian tax law. A comprehensive legislative overhaul has been underway: Minister of Youth and Sports Sadok Mourali presented a revised draft law covering games of chance, money games, and sports betting — see the pending reform below — adapting the regime to international anti-money-laundering and match-fixing/sports-integrity standards and aiming to criminalize unlicensed online gambling outright. Confirmed via CMS Expert Guides' Africa gambling-law survey and African Manager's coverage of the Promosport-centered regulatory framework, cross-checked against each other.
CommercialTaxAdministrative2026

🚢 Transport, Maritime & Aviation Law

Maritime Commerce Code — Law No. 62-13 of April 24, 1962Code de Commerce Maritime

Enacted: April 24, 1962 | In force: 1962 | Amended: multiple times
Governs ship registration and nationality, shipowner and carrier liability, and maritime contracts. Incorporates rules derived from the 1924 Brussels Convention on bills of lading. Covers charter parties, maritime liens, collision, salvage, and general average. Complemented by the Maritime Ports Code for port-specific matters.
CommercialTransport

Maritime Ports Code — Law No. 2009-48 of July 8, 2009Code des Ports Maritimes

Enacted: July 8, 2009 | In force: 2009
Governs the creation, classification, and management of Tunisian maritime ports and the port public domain. Regulates port concessions, dockage and handling operations, and safety and environmental rules applicable to port activity. Applies to major commercial ports (Rades, Sfax, Sousse, Bizerte, Gabès) and fishing/pleasure ports.
CommercialTransport

Road Traffic Code — Law No. 99-71 of July 26, 1999Code de la Route

Enacted: July 26, 1999 | In force: 2000 | Amended: Law No. 2009-66
Comprehensive traffic code governing driver licensing, vehicle registration and technical standards, road signage, traffic rules, and enforcement of violations. The 2025 Finance Law (Art. 45) reclassified Road Traffic Code violations from five categories to three, with simplified flat fines of 20/40/60 TND effective January 2025.
AdministrativeTransport

Civil Aviation Code — Law No. 99-58 of June 29, 1999Code de l'Aéronautique Civile

Enacted: June 29, 1999 | In force: 6 months after JORT publication | Replaced: 1935 decree and Law No. 59-76
Governs airspace, airworthiness, airline licensing, airport operations, and aviation safety/security standards in line with ICAO conventions (Tunisia is party to the 1944 Chicago Convention and subsequent annexes). Administered by the Ministry of Transport and the Office of Civil Aviation and Airports (OACA) — a distinct regulator created earlier by Decree No. 98-1374 of June 30, 1998, replacing the former OPAT (Office des Ports Aériens de Tunisie).
AdministrativeTransport

Land Transport Organization Law — Law No. 2004-33 of April 19, 2004Organisation des Transports Terrestres

Enacted: April 19, 2004 | Amended: Law No. 2006-55 | Reform under discussion since 2023
Governs the organization of land transport services — buses, taxis, louages, road freight, and rail passenger service — as distinct from the Road Traffic Code's rules on driving and vehicles. Affirms citizens' right to transport and freedom to choose the mode, while requiring priority for public transport and, for mass and certain special cargo, for rail. Opens public-transport provision to private companies under licence, with public land-transport companies operating regular collective services under a State–company contrat-programme. Title III (Art. 28 ff.) governs freight transport, distinguishing carriage for hire or reward from own-account transport. Notably, it authorizes only non-profit ridesharing (covoiturage à titre gratuit) — the provision at the center of Tunisia's continuing ride-hailing legal controversy — and establishes regional transport authorities and the taxi, louage, and rural-transport licensing framework.
TransportAdministrativeAmended

👷 Labor Law

Labor Code — Law No. 66-27 of April 30, 1966Majallat Al-Shughl

Enacted: April 30, 1966 | In force: May 1966 | Amended: 1994, 1996, 2006, 2007, 2011, 2017
The primary statute governing employment relationships in Tunisia. Covers individual employment contracts, conditions of work, wages, termination, workplace safety, collective bargaining, and trade unions. Applies to the private sector; public servants are governed by the General Statute of Civil Servants. Implements ILO core labor conventions (Tunisia has ratified 62 ILO conventions).
Key provisions
  • Art. 6: Employment contract — types (fixed-term, indefinite-term, project-based)
  • Art. 14–22: Trial period (période d'essai)
  • Art. 23–35: Obligations of employer and employee
  • Art. 40: Minimum wage (SMIG/SMAG) — set by decree
  • Art. 73–83: Annual paid leave — minimum 1 day per month (18 days/year)
  • Art. 90–100: Work hours — 48 hours/week maximum (40 hours in industry)
  • Art. 120: Dismissal — requires just cause; procedural requirements for economic dismissal
  • Art. 123: Severance pay for unfair dismissal
  • Art. 152: Trade union rights; freedom of association
  • Art. 155: Right to strike (with pre-notice requirements)
  • Art. 193–200: Labor inspectorate powers
  • Art. 230–240: Occupational health and safety requirements
LaborAmended

Law No. 2025-9 of May 21, 2025Regulation of Employment Contracts and Prohibition of Labor Subcontracting

Enacted: May 21, 2025 | Published: JORT No. 61, May 23, 2025 | In force: May 23, 2025
A major reform of the Labor Code's Art. 6 contract-type regime and a flat prohibition of labor-supply subcontracting ("sous-traitance de main-d'œuvre" — one company supplying workers to another, including for guarding and cleaning services), framed as an anti-precarity measure. Enforced via CNSS and labor-inspectorate cross-checks; the leading edge of a broader post-2023 push to formalize employment.
Key provisions
  • Open-ended contracts (CDI) become the presumed default form of employment relationship
  • Fixed-term contracts (CDD) restricted to enumerated exceptional cases: seasonal work, temporary replacement of an absent employee, or a temporary surge in activity
  • Trial (probation) periods capped at six months, renewable once only; no further probationary period may follow
  • Labor subcontracting prohibited outright; only narrowly-defined service/works contracts survive, and only where they do not cover the client company's core, permanent activity, require specialized technical skills, and are not used to circumvent the ban
  • Workers previously supplied via now-banned subcontracting arrangements are automatically integrated into the beneficiary company's own workforce, retroactive to the law's entry into force, with seniority preserved
  • Penalties: fine of TND 10,000 per violation (doubled for legal entities); repeat offenses punishable by 3–6 months' imprisonment
  • Three-month compliance window for employers and service providers to regularize existing contracts and reinstate affected workers
Update (August 2026): A Nawaat investigative report of August 17, 2026 describes this law as lacking effective enforcement mechanisms in practice, part of a broader pattern of executive non-implementation of ARP-enacted legislation — see the new Parliament–Government Institutional Rupture entry in the Constitutional Law section above.
Labor2025

General Statute of Civil Servants — Law No. 83-112 of December 12, 1983

Enacted: December 12, 1983 | In force: 1984 | Amended: 2007, 2011, 2017
Governs the employment conditions, rights, and obligations of Tunisian civil servants (fonctionnaires). Covers recruitment, grades, promotions, disciplinary procedures, leave, retirement, and termination. Complemented by specific statutes for particular sectors (teachers, judges, military, etc.).
LaborAdministrative

Law No. 2025-18 of December 22, 2025Exceptional Public-Sector Recruitment of Long-Term Unemployed Graduates

Enacted/signed by President Kaïs Saïed: December 22, 2025 | Published: JORT No. 153, December 23, 2025 | In force: December 23, 2025
