A time-limited statute creating an exceptional, file-based (rather than competitive-exam) recruitment channel into the public sector and civil service for higher-education graduates in long-term unemployment, supervising by the Ministry of Employment and Vocational Training via a dedicated digital platform centralizing applications and applicant data. Complements, without amending, the General Statute of Civil Servants's ordinary recruitment rules.
Key provisions
  • Priority given to graduates over 40 years old, those whose degree was obtained more than 10 years ago, one member per family regardless of age, and persons in fragile social situations
  • Recruitment window runs for a maximum of three years from the law's publication (i.e., through December 2028)
  • Criteria and procedures further specified by implementing decree following enactment
Update (2026): Press reporting through June 2026 (Le Temps News) noted implementation lagging the law's stated ambitions, with the digital platform and first recruitment tranches still ramping up six months after enactment. A Nawaat investigative report of August 17, 2026 went further, stating no implementing decree had been published as of that date — see the new Parliament–Government Institutional Rupture entry in the Constitutional Law section above.
LaborAdministrative2025

Social Security Law — Law No. 60-30 of December 14, 1960

Enacted: December 14, 1960 | In force: 1961 | Amended: extensively
Establishes the compulsory social security system. Organizes the National Social Security Fund (CNSS for private sector) and the National Retirement and Social Welfare Fund (CNRPS for public sector). Covers retirement pensions, invalidity, survivors' benefits, occupational accidents, and maternity allowances.
LaborSocial

Occupational Accidents and Diseases Law — Law No. 94-28 of February 21, 1994

Enacted: February 21, 1994 | In force: 1994
Defines occupational accidents and diseases, establishes mandatory employer liability, and sets compensation levels for temporary or permanent disability and death. Requires employers to report occupational accidents within 2 days. Organizes compulsory insurance through CNSS.
Labor

Decrees No. 2024-419 & 2024-420 of July 4, 2024Minimum Wage (SMIG/SMAG) Increase

Published: JORT No. 85, July 9, 2024 | Effect: May 1, 2024 (retroactive) and January 1, 2025
Two-step increase to the guaranteed minimum wage under Art. 40 of the Labor Code: SMIG (non-agricultural) rose 7% effective May 1, 2024 and a further 7.5% effective January 1, 2025, reaching TND 495.284/month (48-hour regime) and TND 444.886/month (40-hour regime), plus transport and attendance allowances. SMAG (agricultural minimum wage) rose to TND 20.320/day by January 2025. Private-sector retirement pensions were automatically adjusted on the same dates.
Labor2024

Decrees No. 2026-66 to 2026-69Minimum Wage (SMIG/SMAG) Increases 2026–2028

Published: JORT, April 30, 2026 | Effect: staged January 2026, 2027, and 2028
Sets a three-year schedule of minimum-wage increases: SMAG rises from TND 21.336/day (2026) to TND 23.520/day (2028); SMIG (48-hour regime) rises from TND 554.736/month (2026) to TND 611.520/month (2028); SMIG (40-hour regime) rises from TND 470.251/month (2026) to TND 517.571/month (2028). A companion decree sets graduated pay increases for supervisory, management, and executive categories under collective bargaining agreements for the same period.
Labor2026

Decrees No. 2026-63 & 2026-64 of April 30, 2026Civil Service & Public Enterprise Salary Increases 2026–2028

Published: JORT, April 30, 2026 | Effect: staged January 1, 2026, 2027, and 2028
The public-sector counterpart to the private-sector SMIG/SMAG decrees (No. 2026-66 to 2026-69) published the same day. Decree No. 2026-63 revalorizes a broad range of allowances and premiums (management/execution, teaching, research, engineering, health, judicial services, administrative oversight, and social action) for agents of the civil service (fonction publique), local authorities, and administrative public establishments, in a graduated monthly schedule by administrative category: TND 120/month for superior categories A1–A2, TND 100–105/month for intermediate categories A3–B, and TND 90/month for categories C–D, phased in from January 1, 2026, 2027, and 2028. Decree No. 2026-64 sets comparable graduated increases by professional category for employees of public establishments and enterprises (établissements et entreprises publics) under special statutes or collective agreements. Separately, judicial magistrates' function premium (prime de fonction) was raised by a reported TND 120/month over the same three annual tranches. Press reporting (Le Temps News, WebManagerCenter, La Presse de Tunisie, African Manager) confirms the increases were also repercussed onto CNRPS (public-sector) and CNSS (private-sector) retirement pensions, with retirees affiliated to both funds receiving a 5% pension increase from January 2026, paid with arrears; CNSS retirees' 2026 increase and arrears were disbursed exceptionally from May 21, 2026. Implements Article 15 of the Finance Law 2026 (Law No. 2025-17), which had authorized the government to fix the multi-year salary and pension increases by decree.
LaborAdministrativeSocial2026

Law No. 2010-49 of November 1, 2010Private Overseas Employment Placement

Enacted: November 1, 2010 | Implementing decrees: No. 2010-2948 (November 9, 2010) and No. 2011-456 (April 30, 2011)
Completes Law No. 85-75 of July 20, 1985 (regime applicable to technical-cooperation personnel) by regulating private establishments (bureaux/agences de placement) that place Tunisians in jobs abroad. Requires prior ministerial authorization from the Ministry of Employment and Vocational Training (MEFP) to operate, with Decrees No. 2010-2948 and No. 2011-456 fixing the conditions, procedures, and documentation for granting that authorization. As of 2025 press reporting, 82 offices had been authorized since the framework's entry into force, of which 62 remained active, having placed a cumulative 17,000 Tunisians abroad between 2010 and 2024 (mainly France, Italy, and Gulf states, with the ministry also citing efforts to open new markets such as Australia, Canada, Germany, Austria, and Belgium). The Agence Nationale pour l'Emploi et le Travail Indépendant (ANETI) operates a parallel public placement channel; the ministry publishes and periodically updates a list of authorized private establishments and warns applicants against unauthorized operators.
Enforcement gap (documented since 2024–2025): Ministry data cited by La Presse de Tunisie showed 71 fraud complaints filed against unauthorized/fictitious placement structures by end-March 2025, following an earlier November 2024 ministry warning about the same phenomenon — the backdrop for the pending reform bill below.
LaborNationality

🏛️ Administrative Law

Administrative Court Law — Law No. 72-40 of June 1, 1972

Enacted: June 1, 1972 | In force: 1972 | Amended: multiple times
Establishes the Administrative Court (Tribunal Administratif) as the supreme administrative jurisdiction. Has jurisdiction over disputes between citizens and public bodies, annulment of illegal administrative acts, and administrative contracts. Organized into chambers; appeals go to Administrative Court plenary. Public-account auditing is a separate function of the Cour des Comptes (financial jurisdiction) below, not the Administrative Court.
Administrative

Court of Accounts (Cour des Comptes) Organic Law — Organic Law No. 2019-41 of April 30, 2019

Adopted by ARP: April 16, 2019 (143 votes for, 1 abstention) | Enacted: April 30, 2019 | Replaces: Law No. 68-8 of March 8, 1968
Establishes the Cour des Comptes as Tunisia's third jurisdictional order alongside the judicial courts (Cour de Cassation) and the administrative order (Tribunal Administratif) — the "financial justice" (justice financière) and supreme institution for control of public finances. Grants full administrative and financial independence, with its own state-funded budget. Judges the accounts of public accountants (comptables publics) and exercises jurisdictional and non-jurisdictional control over the State, public establishments whose budgets are attached to the state budget, local authorities, independent constitutional bodies, and other public bodies — including budget-execution review, management audits, and sanctioning of budgetary and financial discipline offenses. Distinct from, and should not be conflated with, the Administrative Court's separate role reviewing the legality of administrative acts.
AdministrativeConstitutional

Local Government Code — Law No. 2018-29 of May 9, 2018Majallat Al-Jama'aat Al-Mahaliyya

Enacted: May 9, 2018 | In force: May 2018 | Implements: 2014 Constitution Chapter VII
Comprehensive decentralization legislation replacing the previous municipalities law. Organizes three levels of local government: municipalities (balladiyyat), regions (wilayat), and districts (aqalim). Establishes directly-elected municipal councils with legislative powers in local matters. Introduces participatory budgeting and citizens' right to petition. Implemented after 2018 municipal elections. Its regional- and district-council provisions were superseded in 2025 by Organic Law No. 2025-4 below; the municipal-council provisions remain in force.
Key provisions
  • Three levels: municipality, region (governorate), district
  • Directly elected councils at all three levels
  • Fiscal autonomy — own revenue sources and transfers from central budget
  • Participatory democracy mechanisms (citizen petitions, public consultation)
  • Gender parity requirement for municipal council lists
  • Youth quota (minimum 1/3 of each list under 35)
Administrative

Organic Law No. 2025-4 of March 12, 2025Local, Regional & District Councils

Adopted by ARP: February 27, 2025 (109 for, 2 against, 4 abstentions) | Enacted: March 12, 2025 | Published: JORT No. 30, March 13, 2025
A 10-article organic law implementing the 2022 Constitution's local-power architecture by establishing local councils, regional councils, and district councils as local authorities with legal personality and administrative/financial autonomy, tasked with deliberating on local, regional, and district development plans within the framework of state unity. Requires each council to meet at least monthly, with convocations issued at least 7 days in advance. Repeals contrary regional- and district-level provisions of the 2018 Local Government Code (Organic Law No. 2018-29), the 1989 regional councils law (Organic Law No. 89-11), and the 1994 local development councils law (Law No. 94-87). Regional and district council members are indirectly elected from among directly-elected local (municipal/delegation-level) council members, consistent with President Saied's "bottom-up" ("bina qa'edi") governance model.
AdministrativeConstitutional

Law No. 2026-16 of July 20, 2026Approval of the Development Plan 2026–2030

ARP plenary adoption: July 10, 2026 (64–24–15) | CNRD plenary adoption: July 17, 2026 (56–6–5) | Enacted: July 20, 2026 | Published: JORT, July 21, 2026
A single-article law ("Est approuvé le plan de développement 2026-2030, annexé à la présente loi") approving Tunisia's next five-year Development Plan, prepared under Circular No. 10 of April 22, 2025 by the Ministry of Economy and Planning using a "bottom-up" methodology compiling priorities from over 3,696 working sessions of local, regional, and district councils (see the Organic Law No. 2025-4 entry above), yielding roughly 50,000 project proposals and a pipeline of 40,748 proposed projects. Targets average annual growth of 4.2% (rising to 5.1% by 2030, versus 2.4% in the prior plan period), raising the investment rate from 15.5% to 20% of GDP via a public-investment portfolio of roughly TND 102 billion and an estimated USD 6 billion in targeted foreign direct investment, with emphasis on reducing regional disparities. Adopted in ARP plenary session on July 10, 2026 (64–24–15) and, per the 2022 Constitution's two-chamber architecture for plan-approval bills, by the National Council of Regions and Districts (CNRD) in plenary session on July 17, 2026 (56–6–5); promulgated by President Kaïs Saïed and published in the JORT of July 21, 2026, closing out the JORT-citation gap this artifact had tracked in Pending Reforms since v2.20. Confirmed via L'Économiste Maghrébin and Business News reporting the specific law number, date, and JORT publication date, cross-checked against each other.
Update (August 2026): Per Business News (June 25, 2026), the ARP bloc "Pour que le peuple triomphe" announced it was suspending all cooperation with the government and would not examine government-submitted texts, and subsequently boycotted this bill's July 10, 2026 plenary vote — the documented reason the 64-24-15 tally reflects only 103 of 161 members rather than a full house. See the Parliament–Government Institutional Rupture entry above.
Administrative2026

Public Enterprises Law — Law No. 89-9 of February 1, 1989Participations, Entreprises et Établissements Publics

Enacted: February 1, 1989 | Amended: Laws No. 94-102, 96-74, 99-38, 2001-33, 2006-36; further amending decree reported 2023
Foundational governance statute for Tunisia's state-owned sector. Art. 8 defines a "public enterprise" as a wholly state-owned non-administrative public establishment, a wholly state-owned company, or a mixed-capital company in which state or public-sector holdings exceed 50%. Governs board representation proportional to shareholding and requires public enterprises to publish audited financial statements in the JORT by August 31 each year. Repeatedly amended to adjust oversight and privatization procedures.
AdministrativeCommercial

Public Procurement Decree — Decree No. 2014-1039 of March 13, 2014

Enacted: March 13, 2014 | In force: 2014 | Amended: 2022
Governs all public procurement by state entities, public establishments, and local authorities. Establishes competitive bidding requirements, transparency obligations, evaluation criteria, and remedies for tenderers. Creates the National Commission for Public Procurement Investigations (HAICOP). Sets thresholds for direct award, restricted tendering, and open tendering.
Update (2026): Following a November 22, 2025 restricted ministerial council, the Prime Minister designated public-procurement reform a "state priority," with a broader legislative overhaul reported as an active 2026 work item; no enacted amendment identified as of July 2026.
Administrative

Access to Information Decree — Decree-Law No. 2011-41 of May 26, 2011

Enacted: May 26, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Strengthened by 2016 Organic Law
Grants citizens the right to access information held by public bodies. Establishes a 20-day response deadline for requests. Replaced in part by the more comprehensive Organic Law No. 2016-22 of March 24, 2016, which created an independent Access to Information Commission (IVD predecessor).
AdministrativePress

Law No. 2016-46 of July 11, 2016Electronic Procedures in Administrative Transactions

Enacted: July 11, 2016 | In force: 2017
Authorizes and regulates e-government services. Allows administrative procedures to be conducted electronically. Establishes standards for electronic authentication, digital archives, and online payment to the state. Part of Tunisia's e-government reform strategy.
AdministrativeDigital

Public Accounting Code — Law No. 73-81 of December 31, 1973Code de la Comptabilité Publique

Enacted: December 31, 1973 | Published: JORT No. 51, December 31, 1973 | Amended: Law No. 96-86, Law No. 99-29, Law No. 2003-43, and successive finance laws
The operative rulebook governing execution of the state budget — the counterpart, at the transaction level, to the Organic Budget Law and to the Cour des Comptes's oversight function. Establishes the cardinal principle of séparation des ordonnateurs et des comptables publics (incompatibility between the officials who authorize expenditure and those who execute payment). Defines the ordonnateur's powers (committing, liquidating, and authorizing expenditure) and the comptable public's duties (collection, payment, custody of funds, and regularity control), with personal and pecuniary liability for comptables. Governs recovery of public receivables and the Treasury's single-account and public-deposit rules.
AdministrativeTaxAmended

National Statistical System Law — Law No. 99-32 of April 13, 1999

Enacted: April 13, 1999 | Implementing decrees: three decrees of December 13, 1999
Defines the fundamental principles and structure of Tunisia's national statistical system. Makes the Institut National de la Statistique (INS) the central executive organ and establishes the Conseil National de la Statistique as the coordination and programming body. Institutes statistical confidentiality, the mandatory response duty for official surveys, and the authorization procedure for conducting surveys and censuses — the underlying legal basis for the credibility of official Tunisian economic and demographic data, an active governance topic.
Administrative

Archives Law — Law No. 88-95 of August 2, 1988

Enacted: August 2, 1988 | Implementing decrees: Decrees No. 88-1980, 88-1981 of December 13, 1988; Decree No. 97-389 of February 21, 1997
The necessary companion to the Access to Information framework — access rights are only as meaningful as the underlying records-retention regime this law creates. Defines "archives" broadly as all documents, whatever their date, form or medium, produced or received by any person or public/private body in the exercise of its activities. Arts. 7–8 make it mandatory for public administrations to develop and implement a records-management programme with the National Archives, governing appraisal, retention, destruction (tri et élimination), and mandatory transfer (versement) of public archives.
AdministrativeHeritage

💰 Tax & Fiscal Law

Income Tax Code — Law No. 89-114 of December 30, 1989Code de l'Impôt sur le Revenu des Personnes Physiques et l'Impôt sur les Sociétés

Enacted: December 30, 1989 | In force: January 1, 1990 | Amended: annually in Finance Laws
Governs personal income tax (IRPP) and corporate income tax (IS) in Tunisia. Amended annually through the Finance Law — see the 2024, 2025 and 2026 Finance Law entries below for the latest bracket and rate changes.
Key rates (as amended by the 2025 Finance Law, effective January 1, 2025)
  • Personal income tax (IRPP): 0% up to 5,000 TND; 15% (5,000–10,000); 25% (10,000–20,000); 30% (20,000–30,000); 33% (30,000–40,000); 36% (40,000–50,000); 38% (50,000–70,000); 40% above 70,000 TND
  • Corporate income tax (IS): standard rate raised from 15% to 20% (applied retroactively to FY2024 profits)
  • Banks, financial institutions, insurers: raised from 35% to 40%
  • SMEs: reduced rate of 10% (conditions apply)
  • Withholding tax on dividends: 10%
  • Capital gains tax: included in income tax
TaxAmended

Organic Budget Law — Organic Law No. 2019-15 of February 13, 2019Loi Organique du Budget

Enacted: February 13, 2019 | In force: 2019 | Replaced: Organic Law No. 67-53 of 1967
The framework statute governing how the annual Finance Law (see the 2024–2026 entries below) is prepared, presented, adopted, executed, and controlled — distinct from the Finance Laws themselves. Introduces a rolling three-year medium-term budget framework (cadre budgétaire à moyen terme) and a results-based "program manager" (responsable de programme) accountability model, replacing the line-item budgeting approach of the 1967 organic budget law. Implementing Government Decree No. 2019-1067 of November 14, 2019 sets out program-manager responsibilities and reporting obligations. A 2020 amendment proposal (n°9/2020) was filed; its enactment status could not be independently confirmed as of this research pass.
TaxAdministrative

Finance Law 2024 — Law No. 2023-13 of December 11, 2023Loi de Finances 2024

Enacted: December 11, 2023 | Published: JORT No. 144, December 12, 2023 | In force: January 1, 2024
Annual budget law: state budget of TND 77.868 billion (+9.3% vs. 2023), revenues TND 49.160 billion, deficit TND 10.645 billion. Grants a 4-year IRPP/IS exemption for newly created companies (2024–2025 investment declarations, excluding finance, insurance, mining, real estate promotion, and telecom); introduces a temporary 4% tax on banks, financial institutions, and insurers (2024–2025); caps late-payment tax and customs penalties at 100% of principal; moves the monthly e-filing deadline from the 28th to the 20th.
Tax2023

Finance Law 2025 — Law No. 2024-48 of December 9, 2024Loi de Finances 2025

Enacted: December 9, 2024 | Published: JORT No. 149, December 10, 2024 | In force: January 1, 2025
Annual budget law: state budget revenues TND 50.028 billion, expenditures TND 59.828 billion, deficit TND 9.800 billion. Article 36 restructures the IRPP into eight brackets (0–40%, see Income Tax Code entry) and raises the standard corporate tax rate from 15% to 20% (40% for banks/insurers). Abolishes the 13% VAT rate for real estate, introducing a 7% reduced rate on residential sales up to TND 400,000; cuts VAT on low-voltage household electricity (≤300 kWh/month) from 13% to 7%. Also reclassified Road Traffic Code offenses from 5 categories to 3, with simplified fines of 20/40/60 TND (Article 45).
Tax2024

Finance Law 2026 — Law No. 2025-17 of December 12, 2025Loi de Finances 2026

Enacted: December 12, 2025 (ARP approval Dec 4; transmitted to President absent joint-committee consensus) | Published: JORT No. 148, December 12, 2025 | In force: January 1, 2026
Annual budget law: total expenditure credits TND 63.575 billion; financing needs of TND 27 billion including an 11 billion TND interest-free Central Bank facility and Tunisia's first planned sukuk issuance (TND 7 billion). Article 88 replaces the prior flat 0.5% wealth tax with a progressive "Impôt sur la Fortune" covering both real estate and movable assets: 0.5% on wealth between TND 3–5 million, 1% above TND 5 million. Introduces a new non-deductible 4% sectoral contribution on banks, insurers, telecom operators, and car dealerships (min. TND 10,000/year), earmarked for social security funding, and extends the Contribution Sociale de Solidarité (CSS) through 2026 (3% for standard-rate companies, phasing individuals/employees toward 1% by 2027). A supplementary article — reported as Article 81 by Tunisie Tribune, adopted December 2, 2025 by a 69–17–17 vote and in force since January 1, 2026 — liberalizes foreign-currency accounts for Tunisian residents: such accounts may now be opened with licensed financial intermediaries without prior Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT) authorization, funded from convertible-dinar or foreign-currency balances, tourist currency allowances, or other approved sources, may not be overdrawn, and their holders may make international payments, withdraw cash for travel, and transfer between currency accounts without further prior authorization; interest earned is taxed at a symbolic 0.01% rate. It amends Articles 16 and 18 of the 1976 Exchange and Foreign Trade Code (Law No. 76-18) and Article 16 of the 1986 supplementary finance law. Confirmed via two independent sources (Tunisie Tribune and Managers.tn) that agree on the December 2, 2025 adoption date, the 69–17–17 vote, and the 0.01% rate, though only Tunisie Tribune supplies the "Article 81" numbering, so that specific citation is attributed rather than independently corroborated. This already-enacted, narrower measure is distinct from — and adopted well ahead of — the broader, still-pending Exchange Code Reform bill below. Article 53 amends Art. 18 of the VAT Code to extend mandatory electronic invoicing — previously limited to B2G transactions and B2B trade in pharmaceuticals and fuels — to all service-sector operations regardless of amount, company size, or tax regime, affecting an estimated 380,000+ professionals and businesses; effective January 1, 2026 via the Tunisie TradeNet "El Fatoora" platform (TEIF format, ANCE-issued qualified electronic signature), with penalties of TND 100–500 per paper invoice (capped at TND 50,000/year) and TND 250–10,000 for non-compliant e-invoices (doubled on repeat offense). Article 135, adopted December 4, 2025 by a 60–29–14 vote, lifts Tunisia's long-standing civilian drone ban for agricultural use, authorizing farmers to import, own, and operate drones for pesticide/phytosanitary spraying and crop monitoring — previously drone use was prohibited outright except for exceptional military authorization; actual use remains subject to authorizations issued by the competent authorities, and Finance Minister Méchket Salama indicated a separate, standalone bill regulating civilian drone use generally is being prepared (see the Pending Reforms entry below). Article 138 separately creates a restructuring fund for state-owned agricultural land plots exceeding 1,000 hectares under the Office of State Lands (Sfax, Siliana, Béja, and Jendouba governorates), aimed at improving productivity and creating opportunities for young agricultural engineers and entrepreneurs. Among the additional chapters adopted during the December 2025 floor debate (89 in favor, 12 abstentions, 13 against, on the package of amendments), Article 65 creates a dedicated "Fonds de l'eau" (Water Fund), financed by raising the per-cubic-meter fee charged to bottled-water companies from 50 to 100 millimes, to finance interventions by irrigated-land user associations amid the ongoing water-supply crisis; confirmed via two independent sources (Tustex/AWT commentary and juridoc.tn's law-text citation) that agree on Article 65 against an earlier, less-corroborated Article 71 attribution. The law separately creates a Fund for Environmental Justice and Sustainable Development (Article 95), financed by a 1% levy on the profits of extractive, industrial-transformation, and quarrying companies, donations, and resources the state budget may allocate, intervening in environmentally damaged regions — the Article 77-vs-95 numbering dispute standing since v2.68 is now resolved: the official JORT No. 148 Arabic text (December 12, 2025) confirms the fund at Article 95 verbatim ("الفصل 95 ـ يُحدث صندوق خاص يسمى صندوق العدالة البيئية والتنمية المستدامة…"), while the same text's actual Article 77 concerns unrelated tax exemptions for new/SME business promoters under the Industrial Development and Decentralization Fund, confirming the CKF/general-press "Article 77" attribution was erroneous. Secondary sourcing indicates the fund "is not applicable in the absence of implementing-modality provisions," so it is not yet operative. Article 43 further amends Article 13 of the 2024 Finance Law to exempt state services, local authorities, and public administrative establishments (but not commercial public enterprises) from the TND 10 court fee that funds the justice-reform support special treasury account, effective January 1, 2026 per General Note No. 12/2026 of the Directorate General of Tax Studies and Legislation, covering authorization-to-prosecute requests, payment orders, and appeals.
Tax2025

VAT Code — Law No. 88-61 of June 2, 1988Code de la Taxe sur la Valeur Ajoutée

Enacted: June 2, 1988 | In force: January 1, 1989 | Amended: annually
Establishes value-added tax in Tunisia. Standard rate 19%; reduced rates of 13% (certain services) and 7% (basic goods, agricultural products). Exports are zero-rated. Registration required for businesses with turnover exceeding 100,000 TND/year (services) or 200,000 TND (goods).
TaxAmended

Tax Procedures Code — Law No. 2000-82 of August 9, 2000Code des Droits et Procédures Fiscaux

Enacted: August 9, 2000 | In force: 2001 | Amended: annually
Unifies and governs all tax administration procedures including filing obligations, tax audits, assessment, payment, penalties, and dispute resolution. Establishes taxpayers' rights during tax audits. Provides for administrative appeals before tax committees and judicial appeals before administrative courts.
Tax

Mandatory Digital Cash Registers — Government Decree No. 2019-1126 & Minister of Finance Order of October 14, 2025Caisse Enregistreuse Numérique

Framework: Finance Law 2020 (amending Art. 59 ter, Income Tax Code) | Implementation decree: November 26, 2019 | Phase-in order: October 14, 2025 | Rollout: November 1, 2025 – July 1, 2028
Requires "on-site consumption" food and beverage establishments (restaurants, tea rooms, cafés, fast-food outlets) to install a homologated digital fiscal cash register recording each transaction in real time, as an anti-tax-evasion measure. The obligation traces to the Finance Law 2020, which amended Article 59 ter of the Income Tax Code to authorize the regime and delegate its scope and modalities to a Minister of Finance order; Government Decree No. 2019-1126 of November 26, 2019 sets the technical homologation procedure for approved devices/suppliers (published via the Ministry of Finance's jibaya.tn portal). A Minister of Finance order dated October 14, 2025 fixed the phased rollout: legal entities among classified tourist restaurants, tea rooms, and second/third-category cafés from November 1, 2025; all remaining legal entities in on-site food/drink services from July 1, 2026; individuals under the real tax regime (monthly filers) from July 1, 2027; and all other individuals in the sector from July 1, 2028. Non-compliance — including manipulating, deleting, or falsifying recorded data, failing to issue invoices, or issuing understated invoices — is punishable under Article 94 of the Tax Procedures Code by imprisonment of 13 days to 3 years and a fine of TND 1,000–50,000. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie (May 21, 2026), Tunisie Numérique (June 2026), African Manager, and Managers.tn (June 30, 2026), cross-checked against each other for the decree/order dates and phase-in schedule.
Tax2026

Customs Code — Law No. 2008-77 of December 22, 2008

Enacted: December 22, 2008 | In force: 2009 | Replaced: 1955 Customs Code
Governs importation, exportation, transit, and customs duties. Establishes the legal framework for customs declarations, inspections, duty rates, and customs offenses. Aligns Tunisia's customs procedures with international standards (WCO Revised Kyoto Convention). Governs free zones and customs warehouses.
TaxCommercial

Local Taxes Law — Law No. 97-11 of February 3, 1997

Enacted: February 3, 1997 | In force: 1997 | Amended: multiple times
Regulates taxes and fees collected by municipalities including property taxes (TFP — taxe foncière sur les propriétés bâties et non bâties), tourism taxes, advertising taxes, and local business taxes (TCL — taxe sur les établissements à caractère industriel, commercial ou professionnel). Chapter VII (Arts. 61–63) separately levies an annual licensing fee (droit de licence) on operators of cafés, bars, tea salons, and other establishments selling beverages for on-premises consumption — implemented via Décret No. 97-434 of March 3, 1997 (JORT No. 19, March 7, 1997), which fixes a three-tier fee schedule (25, 150, and 300 dinars by category); see the companion alcohol taxation and licensing entry below for the broader regulatory regime.
TaxAdministrative

Alcohol Taxation and Licensing Regime — Law No. 88-62 of June 2, 1988 & Décret No. 97-1368 of July 24, 1997

Consumption-tax framework enacted: June 2, 1988 | Alcohol-specific implementing decree: July 24, 1997 | Municipal licensing: Décret No. 97-434 of March 3, 1997, under the Local Taxes Law | Amended: 2016 Finance Law (rate cut); 2023 Finance Law, Art. 62 (advance-payment requirement)
Tunisia has no single consolidated alcohol code; the regime is assembled from tax law, an implementing decree, and municipal licensing rules layered atop a colonial-era criminal-law restriction still nominally on the books. Law No. 88-62 of June 2, 1988 reorganized the "droit de consommation" (excise/consumption duty), a tax levied at the import or manufacturing stage on a defined, annexed list of products including alcohols, spirits, wine, and beer. Décret No. 97-1368 of July 24, 1997 applies this framework specifically to beers, wines, alcoholic beverages, and spirits (customs tariff headings 22.03–22.08): Art. 2 designates manufacturers, bottlers, and packagers as taxpayers; Art. 3 sets differentiated rates for "classified" versus "unclassified" wine; and the decree requires pre-activity declarations, bonded warehousing, ten-year accounting-register retention, and denaturation procedures for industrial/cosmetic/pharmaceutical alcohol (rates reported elsewhere at 24 millimes/centiliter for beer and 2.4 dinars/liter for wine). Separately, operators of on-premises drinking establishments pay the annual municipal licensing fee under the Local Taxes Law's Arts. 61–63, whose implementing Décret No. 97-434 references a predecessor law establishing the underlying establishment-category system; a v2.88 search-engine sweep surfaced a candidate citation, Loi n°59-147 du 7 novembre 1959, but jort.tn and iort.gov.tn were unreachable (HTTP 503) throughout that pass, so this number is carried forward as plausible but still not independently confirmed against primary JORT text. Production and commercialization of ethyl alcohol is separately assigned to the state-owned Régie Nationale des Alcools, reported (via three independently-phrased but not primary-source-verified accounts, corroborated again in the v2.88 pass by additional independent search-engine summaries all ultimately citing the entity's own site) to have been created by Loi No. 74-101 of December 25, 1974 (the 1975 Finance Law); the entity's own site (regiedesalcools.com.tn) and fr.tunisie.gov.tn both again returned server errors (HTTP 503) during the v2.88 pass and this citation still awaits direct primary-source confirmation. Rates have shifted substantially over time: the tax on strong-alcohol imports/manufacture was cut from 650% to 50% by the 2016 Finance Law, and Article 62 of the 2023 Finance Law (promulgated December 22, 2022) added a 5% advance-payment obligation on sales by alcohol manufacturers/bottlers. Market production is dominated by SFBT (Société Frigorifique et Brasserie de Tunis), maker of Celtia beer, holding roughly 85% of the domestic beer market. Press commentary cites a colonial-era Penal Code provision (commonly identified as Art. 317) as nominally restricting alcohol sales to Muslims and banning sales on Fridays, during Ramadan, and on religious holidays, though enforcement is described as inconsistent; this article number rests on secondary press sourcing only and was not independently checked against the Penal Code text during this pass.
TaxCommercialAdministrative2026

🏦 Financial & Banking Law

Banking Law — Law No. 2016-48 of July 11, 2016

Enacted: July 11, 2016 | In force: 2016 | Replaced: Law No. 2001-65
Regulates credit institutions, banking activities, and financial services in Tunisia. Establishes licensing requirements for banks and microfinance institutions. Strengthens the supervisory powers of the Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT). Introduces new categories of banking licenses and regulates Islamic banking activities.
Key provisions
  • Licensing: exclusive reserve of banking activities for licensed credit institutions
  • Capital requirements: minimum TND 25 million for universal banks
  • Supervision: BCT has powers to inspect, sanction, and revoke licenses
  • Islamic banking: participatory banks regulated under specific provisions
  • Deposit guarantee: Fund for Deposit Guarantee created
  • Resolution: framework for failing banks established
Financial

Central Bank Law — Law No. 2016-35 of April 25, 2016

Enacted: April 25, 2016 | In force: 2016 | Replaced: Law No. 58-90 (1958)
Establishes the Banque Centrale de Tunisie (BCT) as an independent institution responsible for monetary policy, exchange rate management, payment systems oversight, and banking supervision. Grants operational independence to the BCT Governor. Prohibits direct central bank financing of the state budget deficit.
Financial

BCT Circular No. 2026-4 of March 26, 2026Financing Conditions for Non-Priority Product Imports

Issued: March 26, 2026 | Effective: immediately upon publication
A circular addressed to approved intermediaries (banks) under the BCT's exchange-regulation powers, restricting bank financing for imports of goods the circular classifies as "non-priority" — including passenger vehicles, household appliances, televisions, air-conditioning equipment, cosmetics, clothing, alcoholic beverages, and select non-essential foodstuffs. Importers of listed products must cover the full value of the planned import with their own funds (no letter-of-credit, transfer, or other bank financing/advance facility permitted) before the transaction proceeds. Framed by the BCT as an inflation- and import-bill containment measure amid sustained current-account pressure; UTICA and CONECT publicly raised concerns in early April 2026 about knock-on effects for importers and distributors. Illustrates the circular/sub-decree layer of financial regulation beneath the primary Exchange Code and Central Bank Law above.
Financial2026

BCT Circular No. 2026-7 of August 18, 2026Cereal-Crop Financing Ceilings & Repayment Deadlines

Issued/published in JORT: August 18, 2026 | Effective: immediately upon publication
A circular addressed to banks updating the financing terms for cereal and seasonal-crop agricultural credit, revising the framework set by BCT Circular No. 47 of December 23, 1987 on the conditions for granting, monitoring, and financing agricultural credit. Sets differentiated per-hectare bank-financing ceilings by crop and zone: hard wheat, soft wheat, and legumes at TND 1,415/ha (rain-fed zone 1), TND 1,175/ha (rain-fed zone 2), and TND 1,930/ha (irrigated); barley at TND 940/ha (rain-fed zone 1), TND 890/ha (rain-fed zone 2), and TND 350/ha (rain-fed zone 3); and forage crops at TND 1,030/ha (winter, rain-fed) and TND 1,260/ha (summer, irrigated). Sets repayment deadlines of August 31 for cereals, legumes, and barley loans and September 30 for summer forage loans. Framed as an adjustment ahead of the 2026–2027 agricultural campaign, alongside a restricted ministerial council session on grain-harvest monitoring and campaign preparation. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie, Tustex, and Tunisie Tribune (all August 18–19, 2026), cross-checked for the circular number and financing scales. Illustrates the circular/sub-decree layer of financial regulation beneath the primary Central Bank Law and Exchange Code above, alongside BCT Circular No. 2026-4 (non-priority-import financing) and BCT Circular No. 2026-05 (electronic-cheque platform).
Financial2026

BCT Directive of May 2018Cryptocurrency Prohibition (Current Regime)

Issued: May 2018 | Status: still in force as of August 2026, pending replacement
The Banque Centrale de Tunisie's (BCT) operative position, in force since May 2018, treats cryptocurrencies (Bitcoin, Ethereum, USDT, and similar) as "illegal goods" under Tunisia's exchange-control regime (the same 1976 Exchange Code the pending Exchange Code reform below would replace): public trading and exchange services, crypto-denominated payments, and mining activity that converts coins to dinars are all prohibited without state authorization, banks are instructed to deny and block related transfers, and violators face up to five years' imprisonment plus substantial fines, with funds, accounts, and mining equipment subject to seizure. Enforcement is coordinated across the BCT, the Financial Market Council (CMF), and the Tunisian Financial Analysis Commission (CTAF, the anti-money-laundering unit). No permanent licenses are granted for domestic crypto exchanges or custodians, though the BCT operates a limited regulatory sandbox for controlled fintech experimentation. This is the current, still-binding prohibition that the pending Exchange Code reform's crypto-asset declaration regime would supersede once enacted; despite the reform being described in press coverage since at least 2024, the underlying 2018 ban itself had no standalone entry in this artifact until now. Multiple secondary sources describing the ban were checked, but none cited a specific circular or directive number or JORT reference for the May 2018 text — recorded as an identified open item rather than guessed at. One candidate was checked and ruled out as of August 2026: BCT Circular n°2018-16 (December 31, 2018) was fetched and confirmed to concern payment-institution account tiers ("comptes de niveau 1/2/3"), an unrelated fintech-licensing matter, not the crypto prohibition.
FinancialDigital

Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Code — Law No. 76-18 of January 21, 1976Code des Changes et du Commerce Extérieur

Adopted by National Assembly: January 13, 1976 | Published JORT: January 20–23, 1976 | Amended: Art. 16 & 18 by Law No. 2024-41 (2024 currency-account liberalization, see Finance Law 2026 above)
The base exchange-control code underlying both the 2018 cryptocurrency prohibition and the still-pending Exchange Code reform bill, previously referenced only in passing elsewhere in this artifact. Establishes the general non-convertibility principle for the dinar (Art. 1: transfers abroad require authorization) and reserves exchange operations to the Central Bank or authorized intermediary banks (Art. 3), with a BCT monopoly over monetary gold (Art. 4). Residents must deposit foreign banknotes and securities with authorized institutions (Arts. 6–7) and generally may not settle transactions with other residents outside Tunisia without BCT authorization (Art. 21). Exchange infractions (Arts. 35–36) are punishable by 1 month to 5 years' imprisonment plus fines, with mandatory confiscation of illicitly held currency and securities — the offense category underlying the pending Foreign-Exchange Violations Regularization Bill.
FinancialCriminalAmended

Code for the Provision of Financial Services to Non-Residents — Law No. 2009-64 of August 12, 2009Code de Prestation des Services Financiers aux Non-Résidents

Enacted: August 12, 2009 | Amended: 2010, 2012, 2013, 2018
Tunisia's offshore-finance code, creating a unified non-resident status covering non-resident banks, insurance/reinsurance providers, investment-service providers, and lightly-regulated collective investment vehicles (OPCVM à règles d'investissement allégées). Sets licensing, organization, and mandatory professional-association membership rules, with prudential and dual internal/external control allocated among the BCT, the CMF, and the insurance regulator. Grants import-duty and tax exemptions on operating equipment and vehicles, plus a combined exchange, social-security, tax, and customs regime for non-resident providers and their foreign staff — complementing the Free Zones Law and Banking Law in structuring Tunisia's offshore financial sector.
FinancialCommercial

Insurance Code — Law No. 92-24 of February 9, 1992

Enacted: February 9, 1992 | In force: 1992 | Amended: 1997, 2002, 2008, 2014
Governs all insurance and reinsurance activities in Tunisia. Mandatory insurance categories include third-party motor liability, employer liability, and professional liability for certain professions. Regulates insurance contract terms, claims procedures, and policyholder protections. Insurance companies supervised by the Insurance General Committee (CGA).
Financial

Microfinance Law — Law No. 2011-117 of November 5, 2011

Enacted: November 5, 2011 | In force: 2011
Liberalizes and regulates the microfinance sector, allowing private and associative organizations to offer microcredit services beyond the former state monopoly (Banque Tunisienne de Solidarité). Establishes the Supervisory Authority for Microfinance (ACM). Maximum loan amounts and interest rate caps specified.
FinancialSocial

Leasing Law — Law No. 94-89 of July 26, 1994Crédit-Bail (Financial Leasing)

Enacted: July 26, 1994 | In force: 1994
Establishes the legal regime for crédit-bail (financial leasing) of movable and immovable property, later intersecting with the credit-institutions framework under Law No. 2001-65 of July 10, 2001 and the Banking Law No. 2016-48. The leasing sector remains bank-dominated — eight licensed institutions, six bank-affiliated, seven listed on the Bourse de Tunis. Applicable interest-rate ceilings are fixed semi-annually by the Central Bank (BCT); the H1 2026 ceiling for movable/immovable leasing was set at 16.12%, the highest of the BCT's rate-cap categories.
FinancialCommercial

Financial Market Law — Law No. 94-117 of November 14, 1994

Enacted: November 14, 1994 | In force: 1995 | Amended: 1999, 2005, 2016
Governs the Tunisian stock exchange (Bourse des Valeurs Mobilières de Tunis — BVMT), securities regulation, and capital markets. Creates the Financial Market Council (CMF — Conseil du Marché Financier) as the securities regulator. Regulates public offers, disclosure requirements, insider trading, and market manipulation.
FinancialCommercial

Law No. 2026-5 of March 11, 2026Regularization of Distressed Agricultural Debts

Enacted/Promulgated: March 11, 2026 | Adopted by ARP: March 10, 2026 | Applicable through: December 31, 2026
Requires public and private banks to regularize classified/distressed agricultural debts owed by farmers and agricultural companies, addressing an accumulation of non-performing loans in the sector. Over 30,000 farmers and roughly 4,785 agricultural enterprises are estimated to qualify. The Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT) issued an implementing circular (June 2026) directing banks on application. Cross-references the Banking Law and Central Bank Law frameworks the BCT circular operates under.
Key provisions
  • Covers bank-classified agricultural debts (BCT classification category 4 or worse) outstanding before September 30, 2025
  • Rescheduling of the full principal and originally-contracted interest over a maximum seven-year period, including a one-year grace period
  • BCT must automatically clear negative bank-classification records for farmers/entities once a rescheduling or settlement agreement is approved
  • Excludes debts under judicial proceedings involving corruption or money laundering, unless a final acquittal judgment is issued
  • Sunset clause: provisions apply only through December 31, 2026
Financial2026

Decree No. 2026-148 of July 23, 2026Interest-Free "Crédit d'Honneur" Financing Scheme

Published in JORT: July 23, 2026 | Legal basis: Article 412 ter of the Commercial Code
Creates an interest-free, collateral-free small-financing scheme ("crédit d'honneur") that banks must fund from a mandatory allocation of their own fiscal-year 2025 profits, aimed at widening access to credit for individuals, micro-project holders, and small and medium enterprises historically underserved by traditional bank lending. Each bank must open a dedicated line-of-financing account on its books for the scheme. Complements, but is distinct from, the existing Banking Law licensing/supervision framework and the agricultural-debt regularization law's separate debt-rescheduling mechanism.
Key provisions
  • Mandatory funding: each bank must dedicate at least 8% of its FY2025 profits to the scheme, credited to a dedicated on-books financing-line account (banks with bylaws restricting their field of intervention may be exempt)
  • Minimum 50% of each bank's honor-financing-line allocation must be earmarked for SMEs and community societies (sociétés communautaires)
  • Loan ceilings: TND 25,000 for SMEs/community societies, TND 10,000 for micro/small-project holders, TND 5,000 for individuals (consumption needs)
  • Terms: no interest, no collateral/guarantee requirement, no study fees or commissions; optional grace period of up to 6 months
  • Processing: banks must decide within 10 business days of application (first-come, first-served); refusals must be written and justified
  • Oversight: annual bank-auditor compliance reports go to the Finance Ministry and the Central Bank of Tunisia (BCT), which may sanction non-compliant banks under the Commercial Code
Correction (July 30, 2026, follow-up pass): The decree's Article 3 sets a maximum repayment term of 6 years, not the 2-year cap this entry previously (and incorrectly) stated. This is a genuine legal tension worth flagging: the underlying Article 412 ter of the Commercial Code, inserted by Law No. 2024-41 of August 2, 2024, contemplates short-term "crédit d'honneur" microfinancing not exceeding 2 years, so a regulatory decree tripling that ceiling arguably exceeds its implementing authority and is exposed to an illegality challenge before the administrative court — a risk multiple outlets flagged on publication (African Manager: two independent analyses reached the same Article 3 reading). Separately, fiscal advisor and university lecturer Mohamed Salah Ayari publicly clarified on July 30, 2026 (Jawhara FM) that a mere signed "declaration of honor" is not sufficient to obtain financing — applicants must still submit a full file for bank review under the decree's ordinary lending-decision procedure; the "on your honor" branding refers to the absence of collateral, not to the absence of underwriting.
Financial2026

📈 Investment & Business Environment

Investment Law — Law No. 2016-71 of September 30, 2016

Enacted: September 30, 2016 | In force: April 2017 | Replaced: Investment Code of 1993
Tunisia's primary investment statute, replacing the 1993 Investment Code. Applies to both domestic and foreign investment. Guarantees freedom of investment, equal treatment of foreign and domestic investors, protection against expropriation (with compensation), and transfer of profits and capital. Establishes the Investment Authority (FIPA — Foreign Investment Promotion Agency) and the Investment Development Agency (IDI). Creates investment incentives including tax benefits for priority sectors and regions.
Key provisions
  • Freedom of establishment for foreign investors (with exceptions in certain sectors)
  • Art. 7: Equal treatment of foreign investors
  • Art. 21–25: Protection against nationalization and expropriation
  • Art. 26: Freedom to transfer capital, profits, and salaries of foreign staff
  • Incentive framework: preferential tax rates for investment in priority sectors
  • Regional development incentives for investments in interior regions
  • Foreign ownership: up to 100% in most sectors; limited in certain strategic sectors
  • Single window (guichet unique) for investment approvals
CommercialFinancial

Free Zones Law — Law No. 92-81 of August 3, 1992

Enacted: August 3, 1992 | In force: 1993 | Amended: 2007, 2009, 2019
Creates special economic zones (free zones) offering customs and tax advantages for export-oriented businesses. Free zone companies are exempt from import duties, VAT on local purchases, and income tax for initial periods. Currently active free zones include Bizerte Economic Activity Park. Requires minimum 70% export of production.
CommercialTax

Startup Act — Law No. 2018-20 of April 17, 2018

Enacted: April 17, 2018 | In force: August 2018
Innovative legislation creating a special legal and fiscal framework for innovative startups. Eligible startups receive a "Startup" label granting advantages including: salary coverage for founders for 1 year, exemption from employer social security contributions for 3 years, preferential tax treatment, facilitated foreign currency access, and streamlined administrative procedures. One of Africa's first dedicated startup laws.
Key provisions
  • "Startup" label granted by the Startup Tunisia Committee
  • Founder salary covered by the state for up to 1 year
  • 3-year exemption from employer social security contributions
  • 5-year corporate tax holiday
  • Foreign currency account allowed for startups
  • Fast-track work permits for foreign talent
  • Crowdfunding and convertible notes facilitated
CommercialDigital

Arbitration Code — Law No. 93-42 of April 26, 1993

Enacted: April 26, 1993 | In force: 1993
Governs domestic and international commercial arbitration in Tunisia. Based on the UNCITRAL Model Law. Provides for arbitration agreements, constitution of tribunals, conduct of proceedings, and recognition and enforcement of awards. Tunisia ratified the New York Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards in 1967.
Commercial

Public-Private Partnership Law — Law No. 2015-49 of November 27, 2015

Enacted: November 27, 2015 | In force: 2015 | Implementing decrees: 2016-1104, 2016-1185
Establishes the general legal framework for PPP contracts between public entities and private partners for infrastructure and public-service projects. Defines a PPP contract as a written, fixed-term agreement entrusting a private partner with a global mission covering design, construction, financing, and maintenance, remunerated by the public entity over the contract term. Implementing Decree No. 2016-1185 (October 14, 2016) created the General Authority for PPP (IGPPP) responsible for oversight and technical support. No substantive amendment identified as of 2026, though broader public-procurement reform is under parliamentary discussion.
CommercialAdministrative

💡 Intellectual Property Law

Literary and Artistic Property Law — Law No. 2009-33 of June 23, 2009

Enacted: June 23, 2009 | In force: 2009 | Replaced: Law No. 94-36
Governs copyright and related rights in Tunisia. Protects literary, artistic, and musical works, software, databases, and audiovisual productions. Protection term: author's life + 70 years. Covers moral rights (inalienable) and economic rights (transferable). Aligns with WIPO Copyright Treaty and TRIPS Agreement obligations. Enforced by the Tunisian Copyright Protection Office (OTPDA).
Key provisions
  • Art. 1–10: Scope — literary, artistic, musical, audiovisual, photographic works; software
  • Art. 15: Moral rights (right of integrity, right of attribution) — inalienable and perpetual
  • Art. 16–22: Economic rights — reproduction, distribution, public performance
  • Art. 24: Duration — life of author + 70 years
  • Art. 45–55: Related rights for performers, producers, broadcasters
  • Art. 60–75: Exceptions and limitations (private use, education, libraries)
  • Art. 87–100: Civil and criminal remedies for infringement
IP

Industrial Property Law — Law No. 2000-84 of August 24, 2000

Enacted: August 24, 2000 | In force: 2001 | Amended: 2007
Governs patents, trademarks, geographical indications, industrial designs, and utility models in Tunisia. Administered by the Tunisian Intellectual Property Office (INNORPI). Tunisia is a member of WIPO, the Paris Convention, the Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT), and the Madrid Protocol on trademarks.
Key provisions
  • Patents: 20-year protection from filing date; requires novelty, inventive step, industrial application
  • Trademarks: 10-year protection, renewable; registration at INNORPI required
  • Industrial designs: 5-year protection, renewable up to 4 times (25 years total)
  • Geographical indications: protection for Tunisian products with geographic origin
  • Compulsory licenses: available for public health or national emergency
IP

🌿 Environmental Law

Framework Environmental Law — Law No. 88-91 of August 2, 1988

Enacted: August 2, 1988 | In force: 1988 | Amended: 1992, 2001
Tunisia's foundational environmental statute, creating the National Environment Protection Agency (ANPE). Establishes environmental impact assessment (EIA) requirements for major projects. Governs pollution prevention, waste management, and natural resource protection. Establishes the principle of "polluter pays."
Environmental

Water Code — Law No. 75-16 of March 31, 1975

Enacted: March 31, 1975 | In force: 1975 | Amended: 1987, 2001
Classifies all water resources (surface and groundwater) as public domain. Governs water use permits, irrigation rights, water conservation, and protection from pollution. Establishes the National Water Authority and regional water committees.
Environmental

Forestry Code — Law No. 88-20 of March 13, 1988

Enacted: March 13, 1988 | In force: 1988
Classifies and protects Tunisian forests and natural parks. Regulates logging permits, forest fires (criminalized), afforestation, and pastoral use of forest land. Creates the Directorate General of Forests under the Ministry of Agriculture. Articles 218–219 authorize creation of terrestrial national parks and nature reserves by decree; marine and coastal protected areas are governed separately — see the Marine & Coastal Protected Areas Law below.
Environmental

Waste Management Law — Law No. 96-41 of June 10, 1996

Enacted: June 10, 1996 | In force: 1996 | Amended: 2007
Governs waste prevention, collection, transport, treatment, and disposal. Creates the National Agency for Waste Management (ANGed). Establishes the waste hierarchy (prevention, reuse, recycling, recovery, disposal). Special provisions for hazardous waste, medical waste, and industrial effluents.
Environmental

Law No. 2015-12 of May 11, 2015Energy Transition and Green Economy

Enacted: May 11, 2015 | In force: 2015
Framework law for energy transition and promotion of renewable energy. Sets national targets for renewable energy share (30% by 2030, updated to 35%). Regulates self-generation of electricity from renewables, feed-in tariffs, and investment in the energy sector. Establishes the National Agency for Energy Conservation (ANME).
Environmental

Law No. 2019-47 of May 29, 2019Improvement of the Investment Climate — Renewable Energy Self-Production

Enacted: May 29, 2019 | In force: 2019 | Amends: ~15 existing laws including the 2015-12 energy transition framework
Cross-cutting investment-climate reform whose energy provisions allow industrial, agricultural, and service-sector operators and local authorities to self-produce electricity from renewable sources, form dedicated production companies, and sell surplus output to the state utility STEG under standardized power-purchase contracts — significantly liberalizing the electricity self-generation regime beyond the 2015 framework law.
EnvironmentalCommercial

Decrees No. 2026-138 & No. 2026-139 of July 20, 2026Presidential Guarantees for STEG Energy-Sector Financing (TEREG Program)

Ratified: July 20, 2026 | Published in JORT: July 21, 2026 | Underlying financing agreements concluded: November 3, 2025
Two decrees, both ratified by President Kaïs Saïed on July 20, 2026 and published in the JORT the following day, by which the Tunisian state guarantees international financing extended to the state electricity and gas utility STEG under the World Bank-backed TEREG program (Tunisia Energy Reliability, Efficiency and Governance Improvement Program), aimed at improving the reliability, efficiency, and governance of the energy sector and accelerating renewable-energy deployment. Decree No. 2026-138 guarantees a €384.8 million loan from the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD/World Bank); Decree No. 2026-139 guarantees a US$30 million loan from the Clean Technology Fund, disbursed with IBRD acting as implementing entity. Enacted against the backdrop of Tunisia's summer 2026 electricity crisis — rolling STEG-imposed load-shedding began July 7, 2026 amid a heatwave, record peak demand approaching 6,000 MW against roughly 4,630 MW of generation capacity, and a reported 87% collapse in STEG's own capital investment (from TND 1.947 billion in 2016–2020 to TND 248 million in 2021–2025) — though the guaranteed financing itself predates the crisis, having been agreed in November 2025. Confirmed via Echos Plus (July 22, 2026) and corroborated by World Bank press materials (November 11, 2025 project approval) and Tunisian financial press reporting on the JORT publication.
EnvironmentalFinancialAdministrative2026

Mining Code — Law No. 2003-30 of April 28, 2003Code Minier

Enacted: April 28, 2003 | Replaced: 1948 and 1985 mining codes
Governs prospection, research permits, and exploitation concessions for mineral resources (excluding hydrocarbons). Requires landowner notification/consent procedures for research activity and establishes the Mining Consultative Committee for permit review. A revision aligning the code with the 2022 Constitution's requirement that natural-resource agreements receive parliamentary approval was still under ministerial preparation as of mid-2026 — see Pending Reforms.
Environmental

Hydrocarbons Code — Law No. 99-93 of August 17, 1999Code des Hydrocarbures

Enacted: August 17, 1999 | Amended: Laws No. 2002-23, 2004-61, 2008-15
Governs prospection, research, and exploitation of oil and gas through a system of "hydrocarbon titles" (permits and concessions), production-sharing terms, and state participation via the national hydrocarbons company (ETAP). On June 25–26, 2026, the productive-sectors committee of the National Council of Regions and Districts held hearings with Energy and Mines Ministry officials calling for urgent revision of the code amid declining domestic production (hydrocarbons remain roughly 3% of GDP and 20% of FDI) and renewed focus on reviving the offshore "Sabah" and "Aziza" concessions — see Pending Reforms.
Environmental

Marine and Coastal Protected Areas Law — Law No. 2009-49 of July 20, 2009

Enacted: July 20, 2009 | In force: 2009
Establishes the legal regime for marine and coastal protected areas, distinct from the terrestrial national parks and reserves created under the Forestry Code (Law No. 88-20, Arts. 218–219). Governs the classification, management, and conservation of marine ecosystems and coastal zones of ecological significance, including permitted and prohibited activities within designated areas. Administered jointly with the Ministry of Agriculture's environmental and coastal-protection bodies.
Environmental

Fisheries Legislation — Law No. 75-17 (1975) & Law No. 94-13 of January 31, 1994Code du Pêcheur; Exercise of Fishing

Fisherman's Code: 1975 | Exercise of Fishing: January 31, 1994 (JORT No. 11, Feb. 8, 1994) | Amended: Law No. 2018-30 of May 23, 2018
Tunisian marine-fishing regulation is split across two texts rather than a unified code. Law No. 75-17 (the "Code du Pêcheur") governs fisher registration/documentation, vessel papers, and employer obligations toward crew. Law No. 94-13 regulates the exercise of fishing itself — licensing, permitted gear and methods, and seasonal bans (e.g., clam fishing prohibited May 15–September 30) — and was amended by Law No. 2018-30 of May 23, 2018.
EnvironmentalLabor

Energy Management Law — Law No. 2004-72 of August 2, 2004Maîtrise de l'Énergie

Enacted: August 2, 2004 | Amended: Law No. 2009-7 of February 9, 2009; Decree-Law No. 2022-12 of February 21, 2022 (JORT No. 19, February 22, 2022)
The demand-side energy-efficiency pillar, distinct from the supply-side renewable-electricity framework of Law No. 2015-12 above. Establishes the national maîtrise de l'énergie framework covering energy efficiency, substitution toward renewables, and rational energy use. Creates the Agence Nationale pour la Maîtrise de l'Énergie (ANME) and the Fonds National de Maîtrise de l'Énergie (FNME), an extra-budgetary mechanism financing efficiency and renewable investment. Makes periodic energy audits compulsory for large energy-consuming establishments in industry, services, and transport, requires prior energy-consumption assessment for new projects above threshold consumption, and introduces minimum energy-performance requirements and labelling for appliances and buildings. The 2009 amendment opened self-generation from renewables and cogeneration for own consumption; Decree-Law No. 2022-12 revised and supplemented the law more recently.
EnvironmentalAdministrativeAmended

Livestock and Animal Products Law — Law No. 2005-95 of October 18, 2005Élevage et Produits Animaux

Enacted: October 18, 2005 | Published: JORT No. 83, October 18, 2005
Tunisia's principal animal-health and veterinary instrument, closing what had been a complete absence of veterinary and animal-origin food-safety coverage in this reference. Its 55 articles run across seven titles: general provisions and definitions; genetic improvement of livestock; organization of pasture and animal feed; animal health protection; animals and animal products; detection of offenses and sanctions; and miscellaneous provisions. Stated objectives are to organize the breeding sector, secure genetic improvement and herd productivity, preserve local genetic resources, diversify feed sources, protect animal health, and develop animal products while ensuring their quality and safety. Underpins animal-health certification, livestock transport rules, and food-safety standards for products of animal origin.
EnvironmentalHealthCommercial

Seed, Seedling and Plant Variety Law — Law No. 99-42 of May 10, 1999Loi relative aux Semences, Plants et Obtentions Végétales

Enacted: May 10, 1999 | Implementing decree: No. 2000-102 of January 18, 2000, in force February 1, 2000 (repealing Décret No. 78-479 of May 2, 1978)
Tunisia's framework statute for seed and seedling production, certification, and plant-variety protection, administered by the Ministry of Agriculture's Directorate General for Plant Production. Requires registration in an official catalogue as a precondition for the legal sale of seeds and plants, and grants breeders exclusive rights over new plant varieties (obtentions végétales) — a plant-specific counterpart to the general intellectual-property regime. Import and commercialization of seed/plant material fall under this law and its implementing texts. Décret No. 2000-102 establishes the National Technical Commission for Seeds, Seedlings and Plant Varieties (chaired by the Director General for Plant Production, with research-institution, producer, distributor, and union representation, meeting at least twice yearly) and authorizes specialized sectoral sub-commissions per plant group for certification review; it repealed an earlier 1978 decree, giving Tunisia's seed-regulatory apparatus continuity back to at least that year. The law is criticized in agrarian-rights literature (Arab Reform Initiative; the Tunisian Association of Permaculture) for recognizing only officially catalogued varieties and offering no protection for uncatalogued farmer/heirloom ("paysannes") seeds; an advocacy proposal for a parallel farmer-seed register remains just that — a proposal, not adopted law. Separately and distinctly: Tunisia has no adopted biosafety/GMO law despite ratifying the Cartagena Protocol on Biosafety in June 2002 (following 1993 ratification of the Convention on Biological Diversity) — the field is governed only by a Ministry of Environment National Biosafety Strategy and a still-unadopted draft biosafety bill (covering confined use, deliberate release, and market placement of GMOs) pending since at least 2017; GMO-detection laboratory infrastructure exists across the agriculture, environment, health, and industry sectors but operates without statutory backing. Confirmed via FAOLEX's official English translation and WIPO Lex's decree text, cross-checked against Ministry of Agriculture secondary reporting; the biosafety gap is confirmed via multiple independent secondary sources including Tunisia's own Ministry of Environment materials.
EnvironmentalIPCommercial2026

📰 Press, Media & Freedom of Expression

Press Decree — Decree-Law No. 2011-115 of November 2, 2011

Enacted: November 2, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Replaced: Press Code of 1975
Post-revolution statute guaranteeing freedom of the press and abolishing prior government control over media. Abolished the requirement for prior authorization to publish newspapers. Criminalized defamation but with strong protections for press freedom. Created the High Authority for Audio-Visual Communication (HAICA — Haute Autorité Indépendante de la Communication Audiovisuelle).
Press

HAICA Organic Law — Decree-Law No. 2011-116 of November 2, 2011

Enacted: November 2, 2011 | In force: 2011
Establishes the High Authority for Audio-Visual Communication (HAICA) as an independent regulatory body for radio and television broadcasting. HAICA grants broadcasting licenses, sets content standards, and monitors compliance. Replaced the previous state-controlled broadcasting authority.
Press

Access to Information Organic Law — Law No. 2016-22 of March 24, 2016

Enacted: March 24, 2016 | In force: 2017
Comprehensive right-to-information law creating the independent National Access to Information Authority (INAI). Any person may request information from public bodies; response within 20 days required. Narrow exceptions for national security, privacy, and commercial confidentiality. Considered one of the strongest RTI laws in the MENA region. The statute itself has not been formally amended or repealed, but INAI was quietly dismantled in practice: its interim director was suspended around 2024 and its offices were shuttered in spring 2025 without any published decree, prompting condemnation from ARTICLE 19, iWatch Tunisia, and the SNJT, who called it "the last bastion of transparency" in the country. Compliance rates had already fallen from 82% (2018) to 68% (through August 2024) before the closure.
PressAdministrative

Zied El Heni ProsecutionTelecommunications Code Art. 86

Arrested: April 2026 | Convicted: May 7, 2026 | Appeal upheld: June 26, 2026
Veteran journalist and press-freedom advocate Zied El Heni was arrested in April 2026 and convicted on May 7, 2026 to one year's imprisonment over Facebook posts made in defense of a fellow journalist — prosecuted under Article 86 of the Telecommunications Code (Law No. 2001-1) rather than Decree-Law No. 2022-54. The Tunis Court of Appeal upheld the conviction on June 26, 2026; El Heni declined to pursue a cassation appeal, rejecting the trial's legitimacy. Documented by CPJ, RSF, and Amnesty International as part of the broader escalation in prosecutions of journalists described under Decree-Law 2022-54 below, illustrating that Art. 86 of the Telecommunications Code is used as an alternative charge for online speech offenses. El Heni also faces a second, unrelated prosecution arising from his prior tenure on the Carthage municipal council, for alleged abuse of office, harm to the public administration, and forgery; the trial has been repeatedly postponed (through at least June 30, 2026, when a release request was also denied) with no verdict yet reached.
PressDigitalCriminal2026

Haythem El Mekki ProsecutionTelecommunications Code Art. 86

First-instance: non-lieu (no case to answer) | Sfax Court of Appeal conviction: July 15, 2026
Radio commentator and journalist Haythem El Mekki was convicted on appeal on July 15, 2026 by the Sfax Court of Appeal to one year's imprisonment, reversing a first-instance finding of no case to answer. The prosecution arose from a January 2024 social-media post discussing conditions at Sfax's Habib Bourguiba University Hospital (CHU), which filed the original complaint. Proceedings were initially brought under Decree-Law No. 2022-54, but the appellate court convicted instead under Article 86 of the Telecommunications Code (Law No. 2001-1) — the same alternative charge already documented in the Zied El Heni case above. The National Syndicate of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT) condemned the ruling as an attack on freedom of expression and called for its annulment. Confirmed via Business News, L'Économiste Maghrébin, webdo.tn, Réalités Magazine, Kapitalis, and Nawaat, cross-checked against each other.
PressDigitalCriminal2026

Hamza Belloumi / Insaf Boughdiri CaseMarzouki "Bloodbath" Video Prosecution

Complaint filed: 2015 | First-instance default judgment: fine | Appeal default judgment: 1 year + fine (June 2026) | Opposition accepted, default judgment annulled: July 21, 2026 | New trial date: December 2, 2026
Broadcast journalist Hamza Belloumi and his former program editor-in-chief Insaf Boughdiri were sentenced by default in June 2026 to one year's imprisonment plus a fine by the Tunis Court of Appeal, in a case dating to a 2015 complaint by former President Moncef Marzouki over a broadcast video that purported to show him calling for a "bloodbath" in Tunisia — a clip both defendants say was a fabricated montage, and for which the program had already issued a public apology. Both maintain they received no proper notice of either the first-instance or appellate proceedings, preventing them from mounting a defense, and note the case is now over a decade old. At the July 21, 2026 hearing, the Tunis Court of Appeal accepted their opposition to the default appellate judgment, annulling that one-year sentence and setting a new trial on the merits for December 2, 2026 — meaning neither defendant currently stands convicted pending that retrial. The National Syndicate of Tunisian Journalists (SNJT) issued a statement of solidarity with both defendants. Confirmed via La Presse, Business News, WebManagerCenter, webdo.tn, Kapitalis, and Réalités Magazine, cross-checked against each other.
PressCriminal2026

Khaoula Boukrim ProsecutionDecree-Law No. 2022-54, In Absentia

Press accreditation revoked: August 2024 | Departed Tunisia for France: December 2025 | Verdicts rendered: February 26, 2026 | Verdicts discovered by defendant: June 8–9, 2026
Khaoula Boukrim, founder of the independent news website TUMEDIA, was convicted in absentia in two separate cases under the cybercrime Decree-Law No. 2022-54, each carrying a two-year sentence, for a combined four years' imprisonment. Both verdicts were rendered on February 26, 2026, but neither Boukrim nor a lawyer was notified of the proceedings or the verdicts themselves; she only learned of the convictions on June 8–9, 2026, after security officers visited her mother's home on June 2 seeking her whereabouts. As of reporting, she and press-freedom monitors had still not been able to identify the specific Facebook posts or statements underlying the charges. She had left Tunisia for France in December 2025 after her press accreditation was revoked in August 2024 (ahead of the presidential election) and amid mounting police questioning. The SNJT, CPJ, and the International Federation of Journalists condemned the convictions as emblematic of Decree-Law 2022-54's use against critical online journalism, in the same pattern already documented in the Zied El Heni and Haythem El Mekki entries above. Confirmed via CPJ, IFJ, Business News, WebManagerCenter/Directinfo, Kapitalis, and Réalités Magazine, cross-checked against each other.
PressDigitalCriminal2026

Hedi Redaoui / Makram Hosni ProsecutionGafsa Trade-Union Reporting Case

Video report published: September 2025 | First prosecutor's-office appearance: February 2026 | First-instance non-lieu (Gafsa Court of First Instance): July 14, 2026 | Prosecution's appeal filed: August 6, 2026 | Appeal hearing opened and postponed: reported early-to-mid August 2026
Journalist Hedi Redaoui produced a video report for Kashf Media and Echaab News in September 2025 documenting a protest by employees of Tunisia's National Institution for Food Safety and Health Control (INSSPA) in Gafsa over unpaid entitlements and unadopted implementing regulations governing their professional status. The INSSPA's regional branch filed a complaint against both Redaoui and Makram Hosni, secretary-general of the local trade union representing the agency's employees, reported in press coverage as a defamation-type charge rather than a specifically identified Decree-Law No. 2022-54 count. The two men first appeared before the Gafsa Public Prosecutor's Office in February 2026 and remained free pending investigation. On July 14, 2026, the Gafsa Court of First Instance issued a non-lieu (no case to answer) ruling dismissing the charges, but the Public Prosecutor's Office appealed that acquittal on August 6, 2026, returning both men to jeopardy before the Gafsa Court of Appeal; a hearing opened shortly after and was reported postponed. Press-freedom commentary framed the case as testing whether routine, public-interest coverage of a labor protest can be criminalized and as a companion concern to this artifact's other Decree-Law 54/Telecommunications Code Art. 86 press-prosecution entries (Zied El Heni, Haythem El Mekki, Khaoula Boukrim), though the precise statutory charge here was not consistently specified across sources. Confirmed via independent press-freedom reporting on the September 2025 report, the February 2026 prosecutor's-office appearance, the July 14, 2026 acquittal, and the August 6, 2026 appeal; the exact appeal-hearing outcome and the specific charging statute remain open items for a future pass.
PressLaborCriminal2026

Seifeddine Arfaoui ProsecutionTelecommunications Code Art. 86

Anti-government demonstration referenced: August 20, 2026 | Arrested: ~August 21–22, 2026 | Pretrial detention ordered (mandat de dépôt): August 24, 2026 | Hearing set: August 27, 2026
Seifeddine Arfaoui, an activist with the opposition Nafas movement, was arrested around August 21–22, 2026 after a Facebook post reproducing slogans from an August 20, 2026 anti-government demonstration. The Tunis public prosecutor ordered his pretrial detention (mandat de dépôt) on August 24, 2026 under Article 86 of the Telecommunications Code (Law No. 2001-1) — the same alternative-to-Decree-Law-2022-54 charge already documented in the Zied El Heni, Haythem El Mekki, and Khaoula Boukrim entries above — with a hearing scheduled for August 27, 2026. Press coverage frames this as the second Nafas-linked Article 86 prosecution after Zied El Heni's April 2026 arrest. As of this pass (August 26, 2026), Arfaoui remains in pretrial detention pending the August 27 hearing; no verdict has been reached. Confirmed via Kapitalis (August 25, 2026) and Business News (August 24, 2026), cross-checked against each other; the hearing outcome is an open item for a future pass.
