💻 Digital Law & ICT

Personal Data Protection Law — Law No. 2004-63 of July 27, 2004

Enacted: July 27, 2004 | In force: 2004 | Amended: 2013
Tunisia's data protection statute — one of the first in Africa. Creates the National Authority for Personal Data Protection (INPDP — Instance Nationale de Protection des Données Personnelles). Requires prior authorization from INPDP before processing sensitive personal data. Establishes data subject rights (access, rectification, deletion) and obligations on data controllers.
Key provisions
  • Mandatory registration of personal data processing with INPDP
  • Prior authorization for sensitive data (health, biometric, political, religious)
  • Data subject rights: access, rectification, objection, deletion
  • Restrictions on cross-border data transfers
  • Security obligations for data controllers
  • Criminal sanctions for violations: up to 2 years + fines
Digital
Update (2026): A draft organic law to replace this 2004 statute with a GDPR-aligned framework (132 articles, new independent Data Protection Authority) was filed in Parliament in July 2025 and remained under committee review as of July 2026 — see Pending Reforms. Until enacted, Law No. 2004-63 and INPDP remain the operative framework.

Cybercrime Law — Law No. 99-89 of August 2, 1999(as amended)

Enacted: August 2, 1999 | In force: 1999 | Amended: 2004, 2010
Criminalizes unauthorized access to computer systems, interception of electronic communications, introduction of malicious code, and computer fraud. Establishes penalties of 2 to 5 years imprisonment for basic offenses; up to 8 years for attacks on critical infrastructure. Pre-dates the current Digital Code effort.
DigitalCriminal

Telecommunications Code — Law No. 2001-1 of January 15, 2001

Enacted: January 15, 2001 | In force: 2001 | Amended: 2002, 2008, 2013
Liberalizes the telecommunications sector and establishes the independent regulator (INT — Instance Nationale des Télécommunications). Governs licensing of operators, frequency management, interconnection, universal service obligations, and quality standards. Opened mobile and internet markets to competition.
Note: Article 86 (penalizing harm to others via telecom networks) has increasingly been used as an alternative charge to Decree-Law 2022-54 in prosecutions over online speech — see the Zied El Heni case under Press, Media & Freedom of Expression. A long-dormant 2019 draft "Digital Code" intended to fully replace this 2001 law remains stalled in committee — see Pending Reforms.
Digital

Computer Security Law — Law No. 2004-5 of February 3, 2004 [Agency Superseded]

Enacted: February 3, 2004 | In force: 2004 | Agency (Art. 2) superseded: Decree-Law No. 2023-17 of March 11, 2023, in force September 11, 2023
Originally established the National Agency for Computer Security (ANSI — Agence Nationale de la Sécurité Informatique), a public non-administrative entity under the ministry responsible for communication technologies, tasked with mandatory periodic information-security audits for public bodies (excluding defense and national-security systems) and certain private organizations, auditor certification, incident reporting, and Tunisia's national CERT. Law No. 2004-5's audit and certification framework remains in force, but Art. 2's creation of ANSI itself was superseded by Decree-Law No. 2023-17 of March 11, 2023 (entered into force September 11, 2023), which replaced ANSI with the National Cybersecurity Agency (ANCS — Agence Nationale de la Cybersécurité) under Art. 4, transferring all its assets, rights, and obligations, broadening its mandate from computer-system audits to overseeing the security of information and communication systems across the entire national "cyberspace," and giving it authority to issue binding cybersecurity standards and guides for public and private bodies. Confirmed via ANSI.tn's own transition notice, WebManagerCenter, African Manager, and Observatoire de la Sécurité (Marsad), cross-checked against each other; this rename/mandate change had gone undocumented in this artifact for three years despite ANSI.tn being listed among its own sources. A broader, standalone statutory cybersecurity framework — Proposition de Loi n°2024-36 — has been under ARP committee review since January 2024 but not yet enacted; see Pending Reforms.
DigitalSecurityAmended

Postal Code — Law No. 98-38 of June 2, 1998Code des Postes

Enacted: June 2, 1998 | In force: 1998
Regulates postal service licensing and operator obligations, defines prohibited/restricted shipments, and sets infractions and sanctions for unauthorized postal activity. Governs the historical monopoly and later liberalization of courier and postal services alongside the state operator, La Poste Tunisienne.
DigitalAdministrative

National Artificial Intelligence Strategy 2026–2030Non-Binding Policy Framework

Announced/reported: early–mid 2026 | Main axes presented to restricted ministerial council: August 15, 2026 | Legal status: policy document, not enacted law
Tunisia has no dedicated statute or decree regulating artificial intelligence as of August 2026. The National AI Strategy 2026–2030 is a government policy document proposing a "Conseil National de l'IA" (national AI council) and a "Fonds National de l'IA" (national AI fund), alongside a non-binding press-sector AI ethics charter adopted around February 2026. Neither instrument carries the force of law. On August 15, 2026, a restricted ministerial council on digital-transformation project tracking, held at the Kasbah under Head of Government Sarra Zaâfrani Zenzri, heard Communication Technologies Minister Sofiene Hemissi present the strategy's main axes, framed around leveraging AI for economic/social transformation while respecting human dignity, privacy, and constitutional rights, with an explicit aim of strengthening national digital sovereignty and protecting cyberspace against growing risks; the Head of Government stressed a "preventive approach" to AI-linked risks. Tunisia's cited AI-readiness rationale includes a 29th-place global ranking in AI scientific publications and its Startup Act legal framework, but no public budget figures, implementation timeline, or oversight/data-governance mechanism for the strategy itself have been disclosed as of this pass. The only place AI is addressed with any binding intent remains the pending draft Data Protection Organic Law, which contains four articles on profiling/automated decision-making; a freestanding AI law would require separate future legislation not yet drafted.
DigitalDraft

🏥 Health & Pharmaceutical Law

Public Health Code — Law No. 91-63 of July 29, 1991

Enacted: July 29, 1991 | In force: 1992 | Amended: 1994, 2002, 2008, 2019
Framework law for public health in Tunisia. Covers organization of the health system, health facilities (public and private), medical professions, pharmaceutical activities, and preventive health. Establishes obligations for health establishments and practitioners. Governs mandatory vaccination programs and epidemic disease control.
Health

Pharmacy Law — Law No. 85-91 of September 11, 1985

Enacted: September 11, 1985 | In force: 1986 | Amended: 1992, 2004
Governs pharmaceutical activities including manufacturing, import, distribution, and dispensing of medicines. Requires prior authorization for drug marketing (visa d'exploitation). Regulates pharmacy ownership (restricted to licensed pharmacists). Establishes the National Drug Authority (Agence Nationale de Contrôle Sanitaire et Environnemental des Produits — ANCSEP).
Health

Mental Health Law — Law No. 92-83 of August 3, 1992

Enacted: August 3, 1992 | In force: 1992
Governs mental health care and protection of psychiatric patients' rights. Regulates voluntary and involuntary hospitalization, patient consent, and judicial oversight of psychiatric confinement. Establishes conditions for involuntary placement and patients' right to legal representation.
Health

Organ and Tissue Donation Law — Law No. 91-22 of March 25, 1991

Enacted: March 25, 1991 | In force: 1991
Authorizes and regulates human organ and tissue procurement from both living donors and deceased donors (brain-death criteria, with a presumed-consent regime absent recorded family objection). Article 6 prohibits commercialization of organs — donation must be free and anonymous. Establishes medical authorization and reporting requirements for transplant centers.
Health

Medically Assisted Procreation Law — Law No. 2001-93 of August 7, 2001Médecine de la Reproduction

Enacted: August 7, 2001 | Published: JORT No. 63, August 7, 2001
Governs "reproductive medicine," including medically assisted procreation (PMA) to treat infertility. Restricts access to married couples; prohibits third-party sperm and egg donation. Regulates the freezing and storage of gametes and embryos and requires practices that minimize surplus embryos. Administered through authorized public and private fertility centers under Health Ministry oversight.
HealthPersonal Status

Medical & Dental Professions Law — Law No. 91-21 of March 13, 1991Exercice et Organisation des Professions de Médecin et de Médecin Dentiste

Enacted: March 13, 1991 | Amended: Law No. 2018-43 of July 11, 2018 | Implementing text: Decree No. 2018-230 of March 9, 2018 (residents/interns)
The physicians' and dentists' professional statute — the framework beneath the pending aesthetic-medicine regulation bill below, which would build on its unlawful-practice provisions. Art. 1 conditions practice on holding a doctorate in medicine or dental medicine (or recognized equivalent) and registration on the roll of the Ordre des Médecins or Ordre des Médecins Dentistes. Defines the criminal offense of exercice illégal de la médecine: habitually establishing diagnoses or treating illness or surgical conditions by personal acts, consultations, or any other procedure without meeting those conditions. Establishes the Orders' elected councils and roll-keeping function, professional duties, restrictions on advertising and commercial forms of practice, and disciplinary jurisdiction up to striking-off with rights of appeal.
HealthAdministrativeAmended

Pharmaceutical Professions Law — Law No. 73-55 of August 3, 1973Ordre des Pharmaciens de Tunisie (CNOPT)

Enacted: August 3, 1973 | Predecessor: Beylical Decree of July 15, 1947 (founding), reorganized by Decree No. 60-12 of March 16, 1960 | Amended: Law No. 76-31 of February 4, 1976; Law No. 76-62 of July 12, 1976; Law No. 89-24 of February 27, 1989; Law No. 89-101 of December 11, 1989; Law No. 90-105 of 1990; Law No. 92-75 of August 3, 1992; Law No. 2008-32 of May 13, 2008
The pharmacists' professional-order statute, distinct from the Pharmacy Law above, which regulates pharmaceutical activity (manufacturing, import, dispensing) rather than the profession's own governance. Traces to a 1947 Beylical decree first instituting a mandatory Order grouping all pharmacists authorized to practise in Tunisia; post-independence Decree No. 60-12 reserved pharmacy practice to Tunisian nationals across all sectors. The 1973 law abolished the prior collegiate structure and unified all pharmacists into a single body with numerus clausus restrictions on new pharmacy openings; the 1990 reform (Law No. 90-105) created six Regional Councils beneath a National Council (Conseil National de l'Ordre des Pharmaciens de Tunisie, CNOPT) as the Order's governing body, and the 1992 amendment introduced geographically-tiered, degressive numerus clausus zones, abolished the separate Disciplinary Chamber (folding discipline into the Council), and barred simultaneous Order membership and trade-union office. Ethics are separately codified by Decree No. 75-835 of November 14, 1975 (Code de Déontologie Pharmaceutique). CNOPT and private pharmacy-owner representatives were among the stakeholders heard by the ARP's General Legislation Committee during 2026 review of the Narcotics Law reform bill above. Confirmed via CNOPT's own institutional history page and its published text of Law No. 73-55, cross-checked against each other.
HealthAdministrativeAmended2026

Veterinary Profession Law — Law No. 97-47 of July 14, 1997Exercice et Organisation de la Profession de Médecin Vétérinaire

Enacted: July 14, 1997 | Predecessor: Law No. 58-38 of March 15, 1958 (joint physician/dentist/veterinarian statute) | Implementing text: Decree No. 98-1145 of May 18, 1998 (Council of the Order elections and organization)
Carves veterinary practice out of the original 1958 joint statute governing physicians, dentists, and veterinarians together (see the parallel Medical & Dental Professions Law above, itself since re-enacted as Law No. 91-21) into its own dedicated professional-organization law. Establishes an Order of Veterinary Surgeons (Ordre des Médecins Vétérinaires) that mandatorily groups all veterinarians authorized to practise in Tunisia, regardless of whether they work in the public or private sector, governing registration, professional discipline, and ethics through a National Council based in Tunis and regional councils. Decree No. 98-1145 of May 18, 1998 sets the procedure for electing National Council members and its internal organization. Confirmed via the Conseil National de l'Ordre des Médecins Vétérinaires de Tunisie's own published legal-basis pages, cross-checked against independent secondary summaries.
HealthAdministrative2026

Communicable Diseases Law — Law No. 92-71 of July 27, 1992Maladies Transmissibles

Enacted: July 27, 1992 | Amended: Law No. 2007-12 of February 12, 2007
The statutory base on which Tunisia's entire COVID-19 response rested — the epidemic-powers instrument distinct from the state-of-emergency regime. Establishes the list of notifiable communicable diseases and the mandatory-declaration duty on physicians and health establishments. Confers compulsory measures: isolation, quarantine, compulsory treatment or hospitalization, disinfection, and restrictions on gatherings and movement; provides for compulsory vaccination programmes and health control at borders; and empowers the Minister of Health to issue binding implementing orders. Art. 18 punishes by one to three years' imprisonment anyone who deliberately contributes by their conduct to transmitting a disease they carry. Instruments issued under it include Government Decree No. 2020-781 of October 14, 2020 (SARS-CoV-2 prevention procedures) and the Minister of Health Order of August 21, 2020 (mandatory masks in public spaces).
HealthSecurityAmended

Tobacco Control Law — Law No. 98-17 of February 23, 1998Prévention des Méfaits du Tabagisme

Enacted: February 23, 1998 | Implementing texts: Decree No. 98-2248 of November 16, 1998; Minister of Public Health Orders of December 22, 1998 and January 30, 1999
Still the backbone of national tobacco policy in 2026 and an actively debated reform file. Prohibits smoking in lieux à usage collectif — notably public administrations and establishments open to the public — with the covered list fixed by Decree No. 98-2248. Defines "tobacco products" broadly as products intended to be smoked, snuffed, or chewed and constituted even partially of tobacco; regulates and restricts tobacco advertising and promotion; requires health warnings and packaging controls; and sets fines for smoking in prohibited places and for breaches by establishment operators. Tunisian press through mid-2026 reported persistent enforcement gaps in public spaces and the absence of any legal framework covering e-cigarettes.
Health

Ionizing Radiation Protection Law — Law No. 81-51 of June 18, 1981Protection contre les Dangers des Sources des Rayonnements Ionisants

Enacted: June 18, 1981 | Published: JORT No. 42, June 19, 1981 | Implementing text: Decree No. 86-433 of March 28, 1986
Subjects the manufacture, import, holding, use, transport, and disposal of ionizing radiation sources to prior authorization. Establishes dose limits and radiation-protection obligations for workers, patients, and the public; requires designation of radiation-protection officers and dosimetric monitoring of exposed workers; and mandates declaration and registration of radioactive sources and radiological installations across medical, industrial, and research settings. Provides for inspection, suspension of activity, and penalties for unauthorized handling. Operated through the Centre National de Radioprotection under the Ministry of Health, and relied on by the Institut de Santé et de Sécurité au Travail as the occupational-radiation base.
HealthEnvironmentLabor

🎓 Education & Culture

Education Framework Law — Law No. 2002-80 of July 23, 2002

Enacted: July 23, 2002 | In force: 2002 | Replaced: Law No. 91-65
Framework law organizing the Tunisian national education system. Establishes compulsory schooling for 6–16 year olds. Organizes the three-stage system: basic education (9 years), secondary education (4 years), and higher education. Defines national curriculum objectives, teacher qualifications, and school governance.
Education

Higher Education Law — Law No. 2008-19 of February 25, 2008

Enacted: February 25, 2008 | In force: 2008
Governs universities, higher institutes, and research establishments. Introduces the LMD (Licence–Master–Doctorat) system aligned with the Bologna Process. Regulates accreditation, quality assurance, student rights, and university governance. Establishes the framework for private higher education institutions.
Education

Cultural Heritage Protection Law — Law No. 94-35 of February 24, 1994Code du Patrimoine Archéologique, Historique et des Arts Traditionnels

Enacted: February 24, 1994 | In force: 1994
Governs the classification and protection of Tunisia's archaeological, historical, and traditional-arts heritage. Deems heritage sites and objects part of the public state domain absent proven private title. Regulates excavation permits, export and alienation of protected movable cultural objects, and restoration of classified monuments. Administered by the Institut National du Patrimoine (INP).
EducationHeritage

Law on the Artist and Artistic Professions — Law No. 2026-8 of May 13, 2026Statut de l'Artiste et des Métiers Artistiques

Adopted by ARP: May 12, 2026 (76 in favor, 1 abstention, 0 against) | Enacted & published JORT: May 13–14, 2026
A 45-article framework replacing prior legislation deemed obsolete by cultural-sector professionals, creating a formal legal "status of the artist" for the first time. Defines an artist as any person who creates a work, or who through performance or interpretation participates in creating or re-creating an artistic work, where that activity constitutes an essential part of their life. Establishes a mandatory professional card for artists and artistic-trade workers, conditioning access to state cultural-support mechanisms and priority social coverage; mandates a stamped, obligatory artistic contract fixing remuneration, duration, and each party's obligations; strengthens social protection to account for the intermittent, unstable nature of artistic work (salaried or independent); sets minimum broadcast quotas for Tunisian artistic works on audiovisual media to support local production against foreign content; regulates foreign artists' participation in Tunisian cultural events; and, notably, includes provisions addressing artificial intelligence's impact on cultural creation and protecting artists' rights against AI-driven reproduction of their work. Confirmed via La Presse, Business News, Tunisie Numérique, allAfrica, webdo.tn, Kapitalis, and L'Économiste Maghrébin reporting cross-checked against each other; no repeal or amendment found as of this pass.
EducationSocialLabor2026

Decree-Law No. 2024-2 of September 16, 2024Conseil Supérieur de l'Éducation et de l'Enseignement (CSEE)

Enacted: September 16, 2024 | Implements: 2022 Constitution, Art. 135 (Chapter 9) | Operationalized: August 12–17, 2026
Establishes and organizes the Conseil Supérieur de l'Éducation et de l'Enseignement (Higher Council for Education and Instruction), the constitutional advisory body created by Article 135 — the 2022 Constitution's dedicated Chapter 9 — with a mandate to issue opinions on major national orientations spanning education, teaching, scientific research, vocational training, and employment prospects, broader than school policy alone. Placed under the authority of the President of the Republic. Structured around a Secretary General, a seven-member "Haute Instance," a 14-member Commission of Experts (each with 15+ years' experience, including retired pedagogical inspectors), and a 9-member Evaluation Commission (20+ years' experience); its administrative secretariat was further organized by Decree No. 2025-246 of May 8, 2025, with staff falling under the 1983 general civil-service statute. The Council remained dormant for nearly two years after enactment until three presidential decrees — No. 2026-203, No. 2026-204 and No. 2026-205, all of August 12, 2026, published in JORT No. 82 of August 14, 2026 — respectively appointed the seven Haute Instance members (Tijani Gammati, Youssef Ben Othman, Chiraz Stambouli, Rim Aribi, Kamel Hnid, Mohamed Habib Allani and Mohamed Seifallah Ben Abderrazak), named former education minister Mohamed Ali Boughdiri as Secretary General, and convened its inaugural session for August 17, 2026 — attended by President Kaïs Saïed, marking the Council's operational launch. Complements, without repealing, the Education Framework Law and Higher Education Law above.
ConstitutionalEducationAdministrative2026

Vocational Training Law — Law No. 2008-10 of February 11, 2008Formation Professionnelle

Enacted: February 11, 2008 | Published: JORT, February 15, 2008
Sits between the Labor Code and the Education Framework Law, covering ground neither addresses. Establishes the national vocational training system across initial training, continuing training, and apprenticeship, and creates the legal framework for validation des acquis de l'expérience (recognition of prior learning). Defines the national qualifications covered (CAP, BTP, BTS or equivalent, and the certificat de compétence), governs accreditation and supervision of public and private training establishments, sets employer obligations and the financing mechanism for continuing training, and organizes the relationship between training providers and the productive sector. Vocational training also falls within the advisory mandate of the CSEE established above.
EducationLabor

Physical Education & Sport Law — Law No. 94-104 of August 3, 1994Organisation et Développement de l'Éducation Physique et des Activités Sportives

Enacted: August 3, 1994 | Companion: Organic Law No. 95-11 of February 6, 1995 (structures sportives), amended 2004 and 2006 | Replaced: Law No. 84-63 (except Arts. 10–45, 67–70)
The enacted sports framework beneath the two sports bills this artifact tracks in Pending Reforms — previously the reform was documented without the law being reformed. Declares physical education and sport a fundamental right of all persons and an essential factor of physical, mental, and moral development. Vests the State with defining sports policy and with the management, control, and protection of sport against violence, speculation, doping, and abuses contrary to fair competition. Its 54 articles cover school and university sport, sport for all, elite/high-level sport, sports facilities, and sports medicine. The companion Organic Law No. 95-11 on structures sportives is the operative statute for sports associations, clubs, and federations — and is precisely the text the pending Draft Organic Law on Sports Structures is designed to replace.
EducationAdministrative

Anti-Doping Law — Law No. 2007-54 of August 8, 2007Lutte contre le Dopage dans le Sport

Enacted: August 8, 2007 | Implementing text: Decree No. 2008-103 of January 16, 2008 (ANAD organization) | Related: Law No. 2006-61 approving the UNESCO International Convention against Doping in Sport (Paris, October 19, 2005)
Prohibits the use, possession, administration, and trafficking of prohibited substances and methods in sport, aligning Tunisian law with the World Anti-Doping Code. Title II (Arts. 6–7) creates the Agence Nationale Antidopage (ANAD), a public administrative establishment with legal personality and financial autonomy under the ministry responsible for sport. Establishes the in- and out-of-competition testing regime, sample handling, and accredited laboratory analysis; provides disciplinary jurisdiction and sanctions against athletes and support personnel alongside criminal penalties for trafficking; and requires publication of the national prohibited list.
EducationHealth

🤝 Social Security & Protection

CNSS Organic Law — Law No. 60-30 of December 14, 1960 (as amended)

Enacted: 1960 | Extensively amended through 2024
Establishes the National Social Security Fund (Caisse Nationale de Sécurité Sociale — CNSS) for private sector workers. Covers contributory retirement pensions, invalidity, death benefits, occupational hazards, family allowances, and maternity. Employee contribution: 9.18%; Employer contribution: 16.57% of gross wages.
SocialLabor

Social Assistance Program Law — Law No. 2019-49 of June 6, 2019AMEN Social

Enacted: June 6, 2019 | In force: 2020
Creates Tunisia's unified social protection system "AMEN Social" replacing fragmented assistance programs. Provides means-tested direct cash transfers to poor families (National Aid Program — PAN), health coverage for the poor (AMGP), and subsidized access to services. Governed by the Ministry of Social Affairs.
Update (2026): A joint order of the Ministers of Social Affairs and Finance, published in JORT Issue No. 80 of August 7, 2026, raised the monthly AMEN Social cash transfer for the poorest-tier beneficiary families from TND 260 to TND 280 (a TND 20 increase), repealing Article 1 of the prior order of July 10, 2024 that had set the TND 260 level. The increase applies retroactively from January 1, 2026, meaning beneficiaries are owed back-payments covering the January–August 2026 period.
SocialAmended

Disability Law — Law No. 2005-83 of August 15, 2005Promotion and Protection of Persons with Disabilities

Enacted: August 15, 2005 | In force: 2005
Framework law for the rights and inclusion of persons with disabilities. Prohibits discrimination in employment, education, and public services. Requires accessibility in public buildings and transportation. Originally set a disability quota of 1% in public-sector employment; Law No. 2016-41 of May 16, 2016 amended Articles 29–30 to raise the public-sector employment quota to 2%. Creates the National Bureau of Disabled Persons.
SocialAmended

🏠 Land & Property Law

Real Property Registration Law — Law of July 1, 1885 (as reformed)Immatriculation Foncière

Originally: 1885 (protectorate) | Overhauled: Law No. 65-5 of February 12, 1965 | Amended: 1992, 2005
Governs land title registration (immatriculation foncière) through the Property Rights Registry (Conservation de la Propriété Foncière). Once registered, title is conclusive and indefeasible. Unregistered land may be subject to adverse possession claims. Tunisia operates a Torrens-style title system for registered properties.
LandCivil

Urban Planning Code — Law No. 94-122 of November 28, 1994Code de l'Aménagement du Territoire et de l'Urbanisme

Enacted: November 28, 1994 | In force: 1995 | Amended: 2003, 2009, 2016
Governs land use planning, building permits, zoning, and urban development in Tunisia. Requires detailed urban plans (PAD — Plans d'Aménagement de Détail) for urban areas. Establishes building permit requirements, setback rules, and height restrictions. Regulates real estate development and subdivision approvals.
LandAdministrative

Housing Law — Law No. 77-46 of May 30, 1977Rental Relations and Tenant Protections

Enacted: May 30, 1977 | In force: 1977 | Amended: 1996, 2013
Governs residential leases and tenant-landlord relations. Sets minimum lease terms, notice periods for termination, and tenant protection against arbitrary eviction. Regulates rent increases for controlled accommodation. Applies to residential leases; commercial leases are regulated separately under the COC.
LandCivil

Co-ownership Law — Law No. 1977-42 of May 23, 1977Propriété par Étages ou par Appartements

Enacted: May 23, 1977 | In force: 1977 | Amended: 1994
Governs horizontal co-ownership (apartment buildings and condominiums). Defines common areas vs. private lots, establishes the mandatory Syndicate of Co-owners (syndicat des copropriétaires), and regulates management, maintenance costs, and dispute resolution between co-owners.
LandCivil

State Domain Code — Law No. 76-66 of July 2, 1976Code du Domaine de l'État

Enacted: July 2, 1976 | In force: 1976 | Related: Law No. 95-21 of February 13, 1995 (state agricultural land)
Governs the public and private domain of the state: classification of state-owned property, rules for occupation, concession, sale, and exchange of state land, and protection of the public maritime, hydraulic, and road domains against encroachment. Law No. 95-21 separately governs the disposal of state-owned agricultural land.
LandAdministrative

Agricultural Land Protection Law — Law No. 83-87 of November 11, 1983

Enacted: November 11, 1983 | In force: 1983 | Amended: multiple times
Classifies agricultural land into protected zones with varying restrictions on non-agricultural use — banned, protected, or authorization-required — to prevent conversion of arable land to urban or industrial use. Requires special ministerial authorization (Comité Technique) for non-agricultural investment on classified agricultural land.
LandEnvironmental

Expropriation for Public Utility — Law No. 2016-53 of July 11, 2016

Enacted: July 11, 2016 | Superseded: Law No. 76-85 of August 11, 1976 (as amended by Law No. 2003-26) | Amended: Decree-Law No. 2022-65 of October 19, 2022
Governs expropriation of private real property for public-utility projects (infrastructure, urban development, environmental protection, unsafe-structure clearance, natural-disaster-risk zones, archaeological sites). Sets the procedure — expropriation file, government decree identifying the parcels, notice to owners — and compensation rules, resolved by amicable agreement or, failing that, judicial proceedings; possession may be taken after consignment or payment of provisional compensation. Decree-Law No. 2022-65 added governorate-level "recognition and conciliation" commissions and allowed in-kind compensation. A further revision was reported under government review as of December 2024 (La Presse) aimed at accelerating public-project delivery, but no new enacting law had been adopted as of July 2026.
LandAdministrativeAmended

Real Estate Development Law — Law No. 90-17 of February 26, 1990Promotion Immobilière

Enacted: February 26, 1990 | In force: 1990
Regulates professional real estate developers (promoteurs immobiliers) selling residential and commercial units before completion. Requires pre-sale contracts (promesse de vente) specifying land title, price, delivery date, and penalty clauses. Developers may not collect buyer payments before signing the promise of sale, and buyer advances must be secured by a bank guarantee. Aims to protect off-plan property buyers from developer insolvency or non-delivery.
LandCommercial

Architects Profession Law — Law No. 74-46 of May 22, 1974Organisation de la Profession d'Architecte

Enacted: May 22, 1974 | Implementing text: Decree No. 83-1033 of November 1983 (Code des Devoirs Professionnels des Architectes)
The professional statute governing roughly 1,700 practising architects — the natural companion to the Urban Planning Code and Real Estate Development Law above. Bars any person from bearing the title or practising the profession of architect in Tunisia without registration on the Tableau de l'Ordre des Architectes de Tunisie (OAT), and establishes the OAT with its elected Conseil de l'Ordre. Sets qualification, nationality, and registration conditions; defines the architect's exclusive design and supervision missions on building projects; imposes professional duties and disciplinary jurisdiction; and grounds the fee and contracting framework for public and private works.
LandAdministrative

🏢 Associations & Civil Society

Associations Decree — Decree-Law No. 2011-88 of September 24, 2011

Enacted: September 24, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Replaced: Law No. 59-154 (1959)
Post-revolution statute liberalizing civil society formation. Replaced the restrictive 1959 law. Article 12: associations are legally constituted upon submission of a notification letter — no prior government approval required. Prohibits dissolution by executive action without judicial order. Enabled a rapid expansion of civil society: over 20,000 associations registered by 2015.
Update (2025–2026 — Article 45 enforcement wave): Since mid-2025, authorities have increasingly invoked Article 45 of this decree-law — which lets the president of the Tunis Court of First Instance order a 30-day activity suspension, on request of the Secretary General of Government, where an alleged violation is not remedied after a formal warning — against independent civil-society organizations. At least 25 associations received 30-day suspension orders between mid-2025 and mid-2026, including the Tunisian League for Human Rights (LTDH, suspended April 24, 2026), Lawyers Without Borders–Tunisia (ASF, suspended May 5, 2026), the Tunisian Association of Democratic Women (ATFD), the Tunisian Forum for Economic and Social Rights (FTDES), the anti-racism association Mnemty, Nawaat (suspended October 31, 2025), Terre d'Asile Tunisie, the feminist organization Aswat Nissa, and the investigative outlet Al Khatt/Inkyfada (suspension order July 2025). Several organizations that unsuccessfully challenged their suspensions in court now face formal dissolution proceedings initiated by the Prime Minister's office — Al Khatt's dissolution hearing opened May 11, 2026 and continued into June 2026. Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, the ICJ, and Front Line Defenders have characterized the pattern as a systematic narrowing of civic space through a decree-law originally intended to liberalize association formation. See also the related pending Organic Law Bill n°27/2023 below, which would replace this decree-law's notification regime outright with a prior-authorization system.
Key provisions
  • Art. 3: Freedom to form associations guaranteed
  • Art. 10: Founders must submit notification (not seek prior approval) to the Secretary General of Government
  • Art. 12: Association acquires legal personality upon notification
  • Art. 14: Registration certificate issued within 7 days
  • Art. 26: Dissolution only by judicial order on defined grounds
  • Art. 31: Foreign associations may operate with prior authorization
AdministrativeConstitutional

Political Parties Law — Decree-Law No. 2011-87 of September 24, 2011

Enacted: September 24, 2011 | In force: 2011 | Amended: 2014
Governs the formation, registration, financing, and activities of political parties. Requires notification-based registration (not prior authorization). Prohibits parties based on religion, race, gender, or region exclusively. Mandates financial transparency and annual reporting. Political party financing regulated: prohibits foreign funding, limits campaign spending.
AdministrativeConstitutional

Electoral Law — Organic Law No. 2014-16 of May 26, 2014(as amended)

Enacted: May 26, 2014 | In force: 2014 | Amended: 2019, 2022 (x2), 2023, 2024
Governs elections for the President of the Republic and the Assembly of People's Representatives. Establishes the Independent High Authority for Elections (ISIE) as the permanent electoral commission. Sets rules on candidacy, campaigning, vote counting, and dispute resolution. Substantially reshaped since 2022 by the decree-laws below, which moved from party-list to individual candidacy, placed ISIE under presidential appointment, and — most significantly — stripped the Administrative Court of jurisdiction over election disputes shortly before the 2024 presidential vote.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-22 of April 21, 2022ISIE Governance Reform

Enacted: April 21, 2022 | In force: 2022
Amended Organic Law No. 2012-23 governing ISIE. Replaced parliamentary election of ISIE's seven board members (previously requiring a 145-vote supermajority) with a presidential-appointment model: 3 members chosen directly by the President from former ISIE members, 3 magistrates proposed by judicial bodies, and 1 engineer, each confirmed by presidential decree for a non-renewable 4-year term. ISIE's then-president described the authority as having "collapsed" in independence terms.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2022-55 of September 15, 2022Individual Candidacy Electoral Reform

Enacted: September 15, 2022 | In force: 2022
Replaced closed party-list proportional representation for parliamentary elections with an individual candidacy system: candidates run individually and must collect 400 voter signatures (50% women, 25% under-35), may stand in only one of 161 electoral districts (10 reserved for Tunisians abroad), and campaigns are self-financed after the repeal of public campaign financing — removing the gender-parity safeguard that the former list system provided.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

Decree-Law No. 2023-8 of March 8, 2023Municipal/Local Elections Amendment

Enacted: March 8, 2023 | In force: 2023
Amended Organic Law No. 2014-16 for local council elections: candidates must be residents registered in the district where they run; each municipality became an automatic electoral constituency; limited-choice ballot rules applied (voters select up to the council's seat count, with no erasures or additions). Applied to the December 2023 local council elections. A companion Decree-Law No. 2023-10 (same date) organized regional council composition.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

Organic Law No. 2024-45 of September 28, 2024Transfer of Electoral Dispute Jurisdiction

Adopted by ARP: September 27, 2024 (116–12–8) | Published: JORT, September 28, 2024 | In force: immediately, days before the 2024 presidential election
Stripped the Administrative Court of jurisdiction over electoral disputes and transferred it to the Tunis Court of Appeal (with appeal to the Court of Cassation), with a 48-hour filing window. Article 4 applied retroactively to pending disputes — directly reversing an August 2024 Administrative Court ruling that had ordered ISIE to reinstate three disqualified presidential candidates (Mondher Zenaidi, Abdellatif Mekki, Imed Daïmi), a ruling ISIE had refused to implement. Passed and signed within hours, roughly ten days before the vote; condemned by HRW, ICJ, and civil society as unconstitutional, since the 2022 Constitution states Administrative Court rulings are not subject to appeal.
ConstitutionalAdministrative

2024 Presidential Election

Vote: October 6, 2024 | Results announced: October 7, 2024 | Inauguration: October 21, 2024
First presidential election held under the 2022 Constitution and the amended electoral framework above. Only 3 of many aspirants were approved to run — incumbent Kais Saied, Ayachi Zammel (imprisoned during much of the campaign), and Zouhair Maghzaoui — after ISIE disregarded the Administrative Court's reinstatement order for three other candidates. Saied was re-elected with 90.69% of the vote on 28.8% turnout, the lowest of Tunisia's three post-2011 national election cycles; ISIE confirmed no legal challenges were filed.
Constitutional

🛡️ Security & Counter-terrorism

National Security Law — Law No. 2019-73 of December 31, 2019Internal Security Forces Statute

Enacted: December 31, 2019 | In force: 2020
Governs the organization, status, and conduct of Tunisia's internal security forces (National Police and National Guard). Defines missions, hierarchy, disciplinary procedures, and accountability mechanisms. Establishes an independent oversight body for police conduct complaints.
SecurityAdministrative

State of Emergency Decree — Decree No. 78-50 of January 26, 1978

Enacted: January 26, 1978 | In force: 1978
Establishes the legal framework for declaring and managing states of emergency. Grants expanded executive powers including restriction of movement, house arrest, administrative detention, and closure of meeting places. Tunisia declared a state of emergency in November 2015 (after Bardo and Sousse attacks), which has been repeatedly extended ever since without interruption; the latest extension, Decree No. 15 of January 30, 2026, prolongs it through December 31, 2026.
SecurityConstitutional

Military Justice Code — Law No. 86-79 of September 9, 1986

Enacted: September 9, 1986 | In force: 1986 | Amended: 2019
Governs military offenses and the organization of military courts (tribunaux militaires). Military courts have jurisdiction over offenses by military personnel and certain offenses against national defense. A 2019 amendment restricted jurisdiction of military courts over civilians — a key human rights improvement.
SecurityCriminal

Weapons and Ammunition Law — Law No. 69-33 of June 12, 1969

Enacted: June 12, 1969 | In force: 1969 | Amended: multiple times
Regulates the import, manufacture, trade, possession, and carrying of firearms and ammunition, classified into five weapon categories with graduated authorization requirements. Possession and carry permits are issued by the Director General of National Security; unauthorized possession is a criminal offense.
SecurityCriminal

Law No. 91-39 of June 8, 1991Combating Disasters, Prevention & Organization of Relief

Enacted: June 8, 1991 | Companion: Law No. 93-121 of December 27, 1993 (Office National de la Protection Civile); Decree No. 93-942 of April 26, 1993, as amended by Decree No. 2023-159 of February 17, 2023
Establishes the legal regime for declaring and managing disaster situations (calamités) — distinct from the state of emergency above — a live topic given Tunisia's recurrent flood and wildfire exposure. Art. 3 creates a permanent national commission and regional commissions for disaster prevention and relief coordination. Mandates national and regional disaster plans, confers requisition powers over persons, property, and services during relief operations, and allocates prevention duties across ministries and local authorities. Companion Law No. 93-121 created the Office National de la Protection Civile as the operational civil-protection service; Decree No. 93-942 (as amended in 2023) sets the plans' elaboration and activation procedures.
SecurityAdministrative

🛂 Nationality, Immigration & Asylum

Entry and Residence of Foreigners — Law No. 68-7 of March 8, 1968

Enacted: March 8, 1968 | In force: 1968 | Amended: 1975, 2004
Governs the entry, stay, and expulsion of foreign nationals in Tunisia. Requires visa for most non-Maghreb nationals (bilateral visa exemptions apply). Establishes residence permit categories and procedures. Governs administrative detention pending deportation. Tunisia has no comprehensive asylum law; refugee status determined by UNHCR under its mandate.
NationalityAdministrative

Irregular Migration Law — Law No. 2004-6 of February 3, 2004

Enacted: February 3, 2004 | In force: 2004
Criminalizes irregular emigration by Tunisian nationals ("harraga") with penalties of up to 3 years imprisonment. Criticized by human rights organizations as criminalizing movement. Used to prosecute irregular migrants attempting to cross to Europe. Tunisia also receives migrants from sub-Saharan Africa; their status is governed by the 1968 law and UNHCR procedure, which has been suspended since June 2024 (see below). Distinct from the Anti-Trafficking in Persons Law (Organic Law 2016-61), which targets exploitation of victims rather than unauthorized departure itself.
NationalityCriminal

2023–2026 Sub-Saharan Migrant Policy Shift

Presidential statement: February 21, 2023 | UNHCR suspension: June 2024 | Ongoing as of 2026
Following a February 21, 2023 statement by President Saied describing irregular sub-Saharan migration as part of a "criminal plan" to alter Tunisia's demographic composition, authorities enforced existing law (No. 68-7 of 1968 and general criminal law — no new decree was issued) far more aggressively, with reports of mass evictions, arbitrary arrests, and expulsions to the Algerian and Libyan desert borders. In June 2024, authorities ordered UNHCR to suspend refugee registration and status-determination activities — the only functioning avenue for asylum in the absence of a national asylum law — a suspension still in effect as of 2026. A May 2024 bill sharply raising penalties under Law 68-7 (imprisonment from 1–3 years, fines to TND 5,000) remains in parliamentary committee, unconfirmed as adopted. Tunisia has never enacted a comprehensive asylum law.
NationalityAdministrative

EU–Tunisia Memorandum of UnderstandingStrategic and Global Partnership

Signed: July 16, 2023
Non-binding political declaration between the EU and Tunisia covering five pillars: macroeconomic stability, trade/investment, green energy, people-to-people contacts, and — most emphasized — migration and mobility. The EU pledged €105 million for border-control capacity, €150 million in direct budget support, and a conditional €900 million in macro-financial assistance tied to an IMF program Tunisia ultimately declined. Tunisia committed to combating smuggling networks, conducting search-and-rescue in its waters, and accepting returns of third-country nationals. Bypassed parliamentary ratification on both sides. In January 2025 the European Commission overhauled its disbursement mechanism following reports of abuses by EU-funded security forces; Tunisian officials have since pushed to renegotiate the deal toward greater reciprocity.
NationalityAdministrative

Law No. 2016-68 of August 3, 2016National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad (CNTRE)

Enacted: August 3, 2016 | Implementing text: Government Decree No. 2019-820 of September 10, 2019 | Amendment adopted in ARP committee: July 14, 2026 (see Pending Reforms)
Creates the National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad as a consultative body seated in Tunis with legal personality and administrative and financial autonomy, its budget annexed to the state budget, under the ministry responsible for Tunisians abroad. Makes consultation of the Council mandatory on draft legislative and regulatory texts, and on international conventions and treaties to be concluded, relating to Tunisians abroad — with the Council required to render its opinion within one month of receiving a text. Fixes a composition combining elected diaspora representatives, administration officials, and qualified persons, and assigns the Council a role in diaspora economic, social, and cultural policy. This is the base statute that the pending amendment bill below would restructure; it had been referenced only through that bill until now.
NationalityAdministrative

⚖️ Judicial Organization

Judicial Organization Law — Law No. 2013-13 of April 2, 2013

Enacted: April 2, 2013 | In force: 2013 | Amended: 2022
Organizes the court system of Tunisia. Courts of First Instance (Tribunaux de Première Instance) — one per governorate. Courts of Appeal — three (Tunis, Sousse, Sfax). Court of Cassation (Cour de Cassation) — apex civil and criminal court, based in Tunis. Administrative Court — apex administrative jurisdiction. Constitutional Court — established by 2022 Constitution. Cantonal courts handle minor civil and criminal matters.
Court structure
  • Cantonal Courts: minor civil matters, misdemeanor infractions
  • Courts of First Instance (24): civil, commercial, criminal chambers
  • Courts of Appeal (3 — Tunis, Sousse, Sfax): appellate jurisdiction
  • Court of Cassation (Tunis): reviews points of law only
  • Administrative Court: all public law disputes
  • Terrorism Chamber: specialized at TFI Tunis level
  • Constitutional Court: established under 2022 Constitution
AdministrativeConstitutional

Judicial Statute — Law No. 67-29 of July 14, 1967(as amended)

Enacted: July 14, 1967 | In force: 1967 | Amended: 1997, 2013, 2022
Governs the appointment, promotion, transfer, discipline, and removal of judges. Historically implemented through the elected Superior Council of the Judiciary (CSM), replaced in 2022 by the decree-laws below. In May–June 2026, the Tunisian National Bar Association (ONAT) staged an escalating protest campaign — including rallies at courts in Tunis, Bizerte, Béja, Jendouba, Le Kef, and Siliana — culminating in a nationwide lawyers' general strike on June 18, 2026 demanding reform of the legal framework governing the profession and stronger judicial independence guarantees. The strike followed a May 1, 2026 ONAT extraordinary general assembly whose decisions the Tunis public prosecutor sought to have judicially nullified. As of early July 2026 the Justice Ministry had given no formal response and ONAT had not announced further escalation.
AdministrativeConstitutional

Legal Profession Law — Law No. 89-87 of September 7, 1989Organisation de la Profession d'Avocat

Enacted: September 7, 1989 | Amended: Law No. 2006-30 of May 15, 2006; Decree-Law No. 2011-79 of August 20, 2011
Organizes the legal profession, governing registration conditions, rights and duties of lawyers, the bar bodies (Ordre National des Avocats de Tunisie/ONAT and regional sections), professional discipline, and the lawyers' retirement regime. Decree-Law No. 2011-79 reframed the profession's constitutional role post-revolution. ONAT's June 18, 2026 general strike (see the Judicial Statute entry above) centered on reform demands under this framework.
AdministrativeAmended

Notary Profession Law — Law No. 94-60 of May 23, 1994Organisation de la Profession de Notaires

Enacted: May 23, 1994 | In force: 1994
Organizes the notary profession, separating the notarial function from the bailiff (huissier de justice) profession. Governs conditions for appointment as a notary, authentication of legal instruments (particularly real-estate transactions), professional obligations, and disciplinary oversight.
AdministrativeCivil

Organic Law No. 2018-9 of January 30, 2018Organisation de la Profession des Huissiers de Justice

Enacted: January 30, 2018 | Published: JORT No. 17, February 27, 2018 | Replaced: Law No. 95-29 of March 13, 1995
The third pillar of the auxiliaires de justice alongside advocates and notaries — the profession that executes judgments — reframes bailiffs as independent-profession officiers publics rather than civil-servant-adjacent officials. Access requires Tunisian nationality (5+ years), clean record, a law degree, and competitive entry via the Institut Supérieur de la Magistrature (sitting judges and registered lawyers are exempt). Powers include drawing up and serving formal notices, execution of judgments, attachment and seizure of assets, evidentiary findings (constats), and — new in 2018 — arbitration and mediation roles. Creates the Ordre National des Huissiers de Justice with elected national and regional councils and a two-tier disciplinary regime.
AdministrativeCivil

Legal Aid Law — Law No. 2002-52 of June 3, 2002Aide Judiciaire

Enacted: June 3, 2002 | In force: 2002 | Replaced: 1922-era decrees on judicial assistance
Establishes the legal-aid (aide judiciaire) system, allowing individuals of insufficient means to access court proceedings and legal representation without charge. Creates a judicial-aid bureau at each court of first instance, chaired by the public prosecutor and including a Finance Ministry representative, a registered lawyer, and a clerk. Bureau decisions granting full or partial aid are not subject to appeal.
AdministrativeCivil

Decree-Law No. 2022-11 of February 12, 2022Temporary Supreme Judicial Council

Enacted: February 12, 2022 | In force: 2022
Dissolved the elected Conseil Supérieur de la Magistrature (established under the 2014 Constitution and Organic Law No. 2016-34, which it repealed) and replaced it with a provisional council roughly half of whose members are directly appointed by the President. Grants the President power to object to judicial appointments and control postings/promotions/transfers; bans judges from striking. President Saied justified the move by accusing the former CSM of "selling positions." The International Commission of Jurists called the decree "unconstitutional and illegal."
AdministrativeConstitutional

Decree-Law No. 2022-35 of June 1, 2022Dismissal of 57 Judges

Enacted: June 1, 2022 | In force: June 1, 2022
Distinct from Decree-Law No. 2022-11, this instrument empowered the President to dismiss judges directly and was used the same day to fire 57 judges on accusations including obstruction of terrorism investigations and corruption; dismissal automatically triggers criminal prosecution, appealable only after the criminal case concludes. In August 2022, the Tunis Administrative Court ordered reinstatement of 49 of the 57 judges, finding violations of fair-trial rights, presumption of innocence, and rights of defense; the Ministry of Justice did not comply. In January 2023, 37 of the dismissed judges filed criminal complaints against the Justice Minister for non-compliance with the court order.
AdministrativeCriminal

Anas Hmaidi Appeals RulingAssociation of Tunisian Magistrates President — "Obstructing Freedom of Work"

Underlying incident: 2022 magistrates' strike | Appeals ruling: July 1, 2026
The Tunis Court of Appeal's criminal chamber confirmed the conviction of Anas Hmaidi, president of the Association of Tunisian Magistrates (AMT), on charges of "obstructing freedom of work" arising from his role interrupting a 2022 court hearing during the judges' protest movement that followed the mass dismissals under Decree-Law No. 2022-35. The appeals court increased his sentence from a one-year suspended term to one year of firm imprisonment with immediate execution; Hmaidi did not appear at the hearing. The ruling lands amid the broader 2022–2026 pattern of prosecutions of judges, lawyers, and magistrates' representatives connected to the post-2022 restructuring of judicial governance.
CriminalAdministrative2026

Transitional Justice Law — Organic Law No. 2013-53 of December 24, 2013

Enacted: December 24, 2013 | In force: 2014
Establishes Tunisia's transitional justice framework including the Truth and Dignity Commission (IVD — Instance Vérité et Dignité). Covers: truth-seeking, reparations, institutional reform, and accountability for human rights violations committed between July 1, 1955 and December 31, 2013. IVD operated from 2014–2019; issued final report in 2019. The IVD's own former president is now herself a defendant: see the Sihem Bensedrine case below.
AdministrativeConstitutionalCriminal

"State Security Conspiracy" Case — Cour de Cassation Ruling

Arrests from: February 2023 | Referral to specialized court: May 2024 | Cassation ruling: December 25, 2024 | Sentences: April 2025
Mass prosecution of dozens of opposition politicians, lawyers, and activists (including Khayam Turki, Ridha Belhaj, Ghazi Chaouachi, Jaouhar Ben Mbarek, Issam Chebbi, and Abdelhamid Jelassi) on conspiracy-against-state-security charges. On December 25, 2024, the Court of Cassation rejected all cassation appeals and confirmed referral of roughly 40 defendants to a specialized criminal court, which handed down severe sentences in April 2025 — described by Human Rights Watch as a "sham trial." Illustrates the post-2021 pattern of prosecutions against political opponents alongside the judiciary and electoral-authority changes above.
CriminalConstitutional

Sahbi Smara Appeal RulingFabrication of a False "Conspiracy" Dossier

Appeal ruling: July 2, 2026
The Tunis Court of Appeal reduced the sentence of Sahbi Smara — convicted of fabricating a false dossier alleging a conspiracy, used to incriminate opposition figures ahead of the October 2024 presidential election — from 11 years to 9 years and 6 months' imprisonment. The case sits alongside the "State Security Conspiracy" prosecution above as part of the broader 2023–2026 pattern of state-security proceedings against political opponents and related figures.
CriminalConstitutional2026

Mabrouk / Chahed Asset-Unfreezing Case — Appeal RulingCorruption & Money-Laundering Prosecutions

Underlying decision: January 2018 ministerial council | Appeal ruling: July 3, 2026
The Tunis Court of Appeal confirmed roughly 6-year prison sentences and fines totaling 800 million TND against businessman Marouane Mabrouk, former Prime Minister Youssef Chahed, and several former ministers (Mabrouk Korchid, Ridha Chalghoum, Mohamed Anouar Maarouf, Khemaies Jhinaoui, Adel Grar, Lotfi Ben Sassi) over a January 2018 ministerial council decision unfreezing Mabrouk's assets; former minister Mehdi Ben Gharbia was acquitted on appeal. The same day, the court separately confirmed a distinct 14-year money-laundering conviction against Mabrouk. Part of the broader pattern of post-2021 prosecutions of former officials and business figures tied to the pre-2021 political order.
CriminalFinancial2026

Khayam Turki Tax-Fraud & Money-Laundering SentencesSpecialized Financial Chamber Rulings

Money-laundering verdict: April 20–21, 2026 | Tax-fraud verdict: July 3, 2026
Two further proceedings against activist and businessman Khayam Turki, both distinct from his existing 45-year sentence in the "State Security Conspiracy" case above and from each other, illustrating the continuing use of parallel criminal tracks (state-security, financial, and tax offenses) against the same defendants in the post-2023 political-prosecution wave. On April 20–21, 2026, the same specialized financial-corruption chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance sentenced Turki to 14 years' imprisonment for money laundering, fraud, and aggravated breach of trust, arising from a UAE company's complaint over a North Africa land-acquisition deal; several Tunisian outlets noted the contrast with Turki's March 2020 acquittal on related charges by the Geneva Correctional Court in Switzerland. On July 3, 2026, the same chamber separately sentenced Turki to 4 years' imprisonment plus a 200,000 TND fine on tax offenses.
CriminalTaxFinancial2026

Ennahda "Conspiracy 2" CaseMass Prosecution of Party Leadership

First-instance verdict: July 8, 2025 | Appeal verdict: February 2, 2026
A separate, large-scale "conspiracy against state security" prosecution targeting Ennahda party leadership and allies — accused of running a covert network aimed at "changing the structure of the state," based largely on anonymous witness testimony, intercepted communications, and documents from police raids. Distinct from the "State Security Conspiracy" case above, which targets a separate network of secular opposition figures (Turki, Belhaj, Chaouachi, Ben Mbarek, Chebbi, Jelassi) under the same broad charge. At first instance (Tunis, July 8, 2025), 21 of 24 defendants were convicted, with sentences of 12–35 years. On appeal (February 2, 2026), at least 20 defendants were convicted, with sentences of 3–35 years; jailed former Ennahda president Rached Ghannouchi (detained since April 2023) had his sentence in this case increased from 14 to 20 years. Ghannouchi faces numerous additional parallel prosecutions, including the "Secret Apparatus" case (life imprisonment plus 30 years, June 2026) and the Instalingo case (22 years, confirmed on appeal January 14, 2026) below — his defense team's own cumulative tally across all cases now runs to life imprisonment plus roughly 100+ years, though exact totals vary by source and case status. Kamel Guizani, Rafik Abdessalem, Lotfi Zitoun, and Mouadh Ghannouchi received 35-year sentences in absentia; Habib Ellouz was among the senior leaders convicted. Defendants were held in pre-trial detention throughout and compelled to participate in hearings by video link from prison, which rights groups say hampered communication with defense counsel. Amnesty International and other rights groups have condemned the case as part of the broader post-2021 pattern of politically-motivated prosecutions of opposition figures. Update (added v2.58): Ghannouchi's detention conditions drew renewed attention in August 2026: he began a hunger strike and refusal of medical treatment at Mornaguia civil prison on August 6, 2026, and suspended it on August 10, 2026 on the advice of the prison hospital's medical staff, citing his advanced age and chronic illness. Ennahdha welcomed his decision to follow medical advice while calling his detention arbitrary, demanding his release, denouncing what it termed an "isolation imposed by the Justice Minister," reasserting his family/lawyer visitation rights, and announcing it would pursue legal proceedings against those it holds responsible for his condition. Confirmed via Kapitalis (August 11, 2026); no change to the sentence or appeal status of this or his other parallel cases as of this pass.
CriminalConstitutional2026

Ahmed Souab ProsecutionAnti-Terrorism Law & Decree-Law 2022-54

Arrested: April 21, 2025 | Sentenced: October 31, 2025 | Appeal ruling & release: February 23, 2026
Prominent human-rights lawyer and Ebru Timtik Award laureate Ahmed Souab was arrested on April 21, 2025 — two days after publicly criticizing the lack of a fair trial in the "conspiracy case" (see above) — and detained pending trial. On October 31, 2025, the terrorism-specialized criminal chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance sentenced him to 5 years' imprisonment plus 3 years of administrative supervision, on charges of "forming and organizing a terrorist group" and "spreading false information," brought under the Anti-Terrorism Law (No. 2015-26) and Decree-Law No. 2022-54. The hearing reportedly lasted under ten minutes, with the court retiring for roughly seven minutes before delivering its verdict; defense objections that Souab be allowed to appear in person, rather than by video link, were disregarded. Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, the ICJ, and Lawyers for Lawyers all condemned the conviction as retaliation for the exercise of free expression and professional legal duties. On appeal, the Tunis Court of Appeal ruled on February 23, 2026, commuting the sentence to 10 months firm (covered by time already served, on the "threat of a terrorism-linked crime" count) plus 2 years suspended (on the "endangering protected persons" count); Souab was released the same day.
CriminalAdministrative

Sihem Bensedrine CaseFormer Truth and Dignity Commission President

Verdicts: June 26, 2026 (Tunis Court of First Instance)
Sihem Bensedrine, who headed the Truth and Dignity Commission (IVD, see Transitional Justice Law above) from 2014–2018, was sentenced on June 26, 2026 to a combined 25 years' imprisonment (20 years in one case, 5 in a second) plus a fine of roughly 1.8 billion TND (~USD 600 million) held jointly with co-defendants. Charges included using her position for unfair personal or third-party advantage, fraud and forgery relating to the Commission's official report, and using her position in connection with an arbitral reconciliation agreement involving businessman Slim Chiboub. Human Rights Watch (July 7, 2026) noted the Transitional Justice Law grants IVD members immunity for acts performed in that capacity, and characterized the verdict — issued while Bensedrine remains free pending her appeal — as part of an escalating pattern of "crushing" sentences against rights defenders and civil-society figures.
CriminalFinancial2026

Saadia Mosbah / Mnemty CaseIllicit Enrichment & Money Laundering

First-instance: March 19, 2026 | Appeal upheld: June 23, 2026
Saadia Mosbah, president of the antiracism association Mnemty (co-drafter of the Racial Discrimination Law above), had her 8-year prison sentence and 122,000 TND fine — for illicit enrichment, money laundering by an organized group, and inadequate accounting records — upheld by a Tunis appeals court on June 23, 2026. She has been held in what Human Rights Watch calls arbitrary detention since May 6, 2024. Six co-defendants, all Mnemty members, were sentenced the same day: Ghofrane Binous received 3 years in absentia; Zied Rouin and Fares Gueblaoui each received 2-year suspended terms with fines; two others received suspended sentences of 2 years and 1 year; three members were acquitted; and five members were stripped of voting and candidacy rights for 5 years.
CriminalFinancial2026

Khaled Krichi Case

Arrested: June 3, 2026 | Sentenced: June 26, 2026
Khaled Krichi, a lawyer and former Truth and Dignity Commission member, was arrested June 3, 2026 and sentenced by the Tunis Court of First Instance on June 26, 2026 to 10 years' imprisonment, the same day as the Bensedrine verdict above. Reported by Human Rights Watch (July 7, 2026) as part of the same cluster of prosecutions targeting figures associated with the IVD's transitional-justice work.
Criminal2026

Abir Moussi ProsecutionsFree Destourian Party (PDL) Leader — Three Parallel Cases

Detained since: October 3, 2023 | "Bureau d'ordre" first instance: December 12, 2025 | ISIE case 1 first instance: June 12, 2025 | "Bureau d'ordre" appeal: March 13, 2026 | ISIE cases appeal: December 30, 2025 | Cassation review announced: July 28, 2026
Abir Moussi, president of the opposition Free Destourian Party (Parti Destourien Libre, PDL) and a leading critic of President Kaïs Saïed, has been held since October 3, 2023, when she was detained outside the presidential palace while attempting to file legal appeals against presidential decrees. She faces three distinct, parallel prosecutions. (1) The "bureau d'ordre" case: the Tunis Court of First Instance sentenced her on December 12, 2025 to 12 years under Article 72 of the Penal Code for "attempted change in the form of government," incitement of citizens to arm against each other, and provoking disorder; the Tunis Court of Appeal's criminal chamber reduced this on appeal (March 13, 2026) to a combined 10 years, plus a separate conviction for processing personal data without authorization and 6 months for "obstruction of freedom of work." (2) An ISIE (Instance Supérieure Indépendante pour les Elections)-filed case over her public remarks questioning the independence of the 2022 legislative elections: the Tunis Court of First Instance's 44th criminal chamber sentenced her on June 12, 2025 to 2 years for abusive use of information/communication systems and disseminating false information likely to disturb public order; confirmed on appeal December 30, 2025. (3) A second, separate ISIE-filed case, confirmed on appeal to a further 6 months. On July 28, 2026, the Court of Cassation confirmed it will examine appeals against all three rulings — totaling roughly 12.5 years combined — at the opening of the new judicial year (rentrée judiciaire, typically October). Amnesty International has described her as "unjustly sentenced," and the Committee for Justice (CFJ) has called for fair-trial guarantees to be respected, both citing the case as part of the broader post-2021 pattern of prosecutions of political opponents documented elsewhere in this section.
CriminalConstitutional2026

"Flottille Soumoud" Financial-Crimes ProsecutionEight Gaza-Solidarity Activists — Economic & Financial Judicial Pole Case No. 2026/4010

Detention orders issued: March 4–16, 2026 (Boughdiri presented himself March 10, 2026) | Msahli released: April 17, 2026 | Channa released: April 23, 2026 | Bennour released: May 22, 2026 | Boughdiri hunger strike: from July 13, 2026 | Boughdiri release request denied: July 16, 2026 | Nawar/Henchiri/Chennoufi release requests denied: July 21, 2026 | Boughdiri's separate "Maersk protest" appeal postponed: July 28 to August 11, 2026 | Boughdiri's Maersk-protest sentence confirmed on appeal: August 14, 2026 | Humena release appeal (Nawar, Henchiri, Boughdiri, Chennoufi still detained): August 12, 2026 | Nawar hunger strike: from August 16, 2026 | ARP deputies publicly call for detainees' release: August 20, 2026
In March 2026, the Pôle Judiciaire Économique et Financier (Economic and Financial Judicial Pole) of Tunis opened a criminal investigation, case file 2026/4010, into the Tunisian coordination committee of the "Flottille Soumoud" ("Resilience"/Sumud Flotilla) — an international civilian initiative launched in September 2025 that organized from the Tunisian coast (notably Sidi Bou Saïd) to attempt to break Israel's maritime blockade of Gaza and deliver humanitarian aid, two of whose vessels (Familia Madeira and Alma) were reportedly struck by drones in Tunisian waters on September 8–9, 2025 before interception by the Israeli Navy in October 2025. Eight coordination-committee members — Wael Nawar, Jawaher Channa, Ghassen Henchiri, Dr. Mohamed Amine Bel Nour, Ghassen Boughdiri (also transliterated Boughediri), Nabil Chennoufi, Sana Msahli, and Wafa Kechida — were placed under investigation over roughly TND 1.5 million in public donations collected to fund and equip the flotilla, facing charges including forgery and use of forged documents, fraud, aggravated breach of trust, money laundering via an organized network, unauthorized foreign financing, and — in the most severe framing reported — suspected high treason; most were subject to mandats de dépôt (pretrial detention orders) issued March 4–16, 2026, with Boughdiri presenting himself to investigators on March 10, 2026. Defense counsel and the Soumoud coordination committee argue the funds were collected openly in central Tunis and that administrative irregularities (cash payments, incomplete accounting) do not by themselves establish criminal intent, calling the case "targeted repression" of Palestine-solidarity activism; other commentary cited by Inkyfada's investigative account takes the financial-irregularity allegations at face value regardless of the underlying cause. Custody has since diverged sharply among the eight: the investigating judge released Sana Msahli on April 17, 2026, Jawaher Channa on April 23, 2026, and Dr. Mohamed Amine Bel Nour on May 22, 2026 (the latter with all charges against him dropped), while repeatedly renewing detention for Wael Nawar, Ghassen Henchiri, and Nabil Chennoufi, whose release requests were most recently denied on July 21, 2026 (Chennoufi's following a fresh hearing). Boughdiri — separately convicted May 8, 2026 by the Tunis Court of First Instance to seven months' imprisonment (Mornaguia civil prison) for "obstruction of work and violence" over a distinct March 18, 2025 protest outside Maersk's Tunisia offices (over the shipping line's alleged indirect support for Israel's military effort) — began a hunger strike on July 13, 2026 after four months in pretrial detention on the financial-crimes file without, per his committee, having been questioned by an investigating judge; on July 16, 2026 the investigating judge rejected his release request. That unrelated Maersk-protest appeal opened July 28, 2026 before the Tunis Court of Appeal, was immediately postponed to August 11, 2026, and — after a solidarity rally outside the court that day organized by the National Committee for the Defense of Activists of the Resilience Flotilla and Palestinian Rights — the court confirmed the seven-month sentence on August 14, 2026; the defense committee denounced the ruling as "political" and "vindictive." Wafa Kechida's committee separately reported losing contact with her around the March 2026 arrest wave; her subsequent custody status has not been independently confirmed by this pass. On August 12, 2026, rights group Humena publicly appealed for the release of the four coordination-committee members still held on the financial-crimes file — Wael Nawar, Ghassen Henchiri, Ghassen Boughdiri, and Nabil Chennoufi — noting they remained in pretrial detention at Mornaguia prison nearly five months after their March 2026 arrests and flagging deteriorating health conditions and prison overcrowding; this is distinct from Boughdiri's separate, already-concluded Maersk-protest appeal (sentence confirmed August 14, 2026). Wael Nawar began a hunger strike in prison on August 16, 2026, publicized by Business News and Kapitalis, and on August 20, 2026 a group of ARP deputies publicly called for the release of the still-detained flotilla-case defendants (Business News); neither development changes the case's custodial or trial-scheduling status. Confirmed via Kapitalis, webdo.tn, Business News, and African Manager (multiple 2026 reports on each release/denial/appeal outcome), L'Économiste Maghrébin, Secours Rouge, and Inkyfada's dedicated investigative piece (which independently corroborates the case-file number as 2026/4010), cross-checked against each other; no trial date has been confirmed for the separate, still-pending financial-crimes proceeding as of the v2.88 pass (August 25, 2026).
Criminal2026

Chokri Belaïd Assassination TrialBéchir Akremi / Habib Ellouze Prosecution

Assassination: February 6, 2013 | Alleged gunman Kamel Gadhgadhi killed by security forces: February 2014 | Death sentences for 4 defendants (main case): March 27, 2024 | Akremi/Ellouze first-instance verdict: March 4, 2026 | Appeal repeatedly postponed, most recently to July 13, 2026
The direct criminal prosecution over the February 6, 2013 assassination of leftist opposition leader Chokri Belaid, now in its second decade before Tunisian courts. Alleged gunman Kamel Gadhgadhi was killed in a security operation in February 2014; in the main case, the terrorism-specialized chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance sentenced four defendants to death on March 27, 2024. In a related but distinct sub-proceeding, the same specialized chamber ruled on March 4, 2026 against former Republic Prosecutor Béchir Akremi — sentenced to a combined 23 years across two separate matters, on charges including concealment of documents and evidence considered determinative to the investigation — and Ennahda leader Habib Ellouze, sentenced to 13 years, both alleging obstruction of and political complicity in the investigation. The Tunis Court of Appeal's terrorism-specialized chamber has repeatedly postponed the appeal (from June 23 to July 6, then to July 13, 2026). Distinct from, but factually linked to, the "Secret Apparatus" case below, which separately alleges Ennahda ran a clandestine security wing implicated in ordering the Belaid and Mohamed Brahmi assassinations.
CriminalConstitutional2026

"Secret Apparatus" CaseAlleged Ennahda Clandestine Security Wing

Verdict: June 2–3, 2026 (Tunis Court of First Instance, terrorism chamber)
The terrorism-specialized chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance sentenced jailed former Ennahda president Rached Ghannouchi to life imprisonment plus 30 years, in a case alleging the party ran a clandestine armed/security wing ("l'appareil secret") implicated in the 2013 assassinations of leftist politicians Chokri Belaid and Mohamed Brahmi — see the Belaïd assassination trial above for the direct prosecution over that killing. Former Prime Minister and Ennahda official Ali Larayedh was sentenced to 42 years; co-defendant Mustapha Khedher reportedly received the case's heaviest sentence, life plus 96 years. Sources vary on the total number of defendants (Al Jazeera reported 25; several French-language Tunisian outlets reported 35). Distinct from the Ennahda "Conspiracy 2" and "State Security Conspiracy" cases above, and from the Instalingo and "Foreign Financing" cases below — one of at least five parallel prosecutions against Ghannouchi. Human rights organizations including DAWN condemned the verdict as politically motivated.
CriminalConstitutional2026

"Instalingo" CaseDigital-Media Company Prosecution

Raid: September 10, 2021 | Appeal ruling: January 14, 2026 | Related sentences: April 14, 2026
On January 14, 2026, the Tunis Court of Appeal confirmed a 22-year prison sentence against Rached Ghannouchi, alongside sentences for roughly 40 co-defendants (journalists, bloggers, and businesspeople), in a case tied to Instalingo, a Sousse-based digital-media/content-farm company raided on September 10, 2021 amid allegations of state-security offenses, money laundering, and online defamation. In a related sub-proceeding, Soumaya Ghannouchi (Rached Ghannouchi's daughter), Yahia Khlil, and Adel Daâdaâ were sentenced to 10 years' imprisonment on April 14, 2026. Distinct from the "Secret Apparatus" case above and the Ennahda "Conspiracy 2" and "State Security Conspiracy" cases elsewhere in this section.

"Foreign Financing" Case2019 Electoral Campaign — Ennahda

First-instance conviction: earlier proceeding | Appeal confirmation: July 13, 2026 (Tunis Court of Appeal, financial-corruption chamber)
The specialized criminal chamber for financial-corruption cases at the Tunis Court of Appeal confirmed on July 13, 2026 a 3-year prison sentence against Rached Ghannouchi and his son-in-law Rafik Bouchlaka over allegations of illegal foreign financing of Ennahda's 2019 electoral campaign, plus a fine equivalent to the foreign funds received (valued in US dollars). A fifth documented parallel prosecution against Ghannouchi, distinct from the "Secret Apparatus" case, the Instalingo case, and the Ennahda "Conspiracy 2" and "State Security Conspiracy" cases elsewhere in this section — illustrating the continued proliferation of separate criminal proceedings against the same defendant. Confirmed via Kapitalis, cross-checked against other July 13–14, 2026 Tunisian press coverage of the same appeal ruling.
CriminalFinancial2026

Ajmi Lourimi / Moussab Gharbi CaseFailure to Report a Terrorist Offense

Detained since: July 2024 | Verdict: July 3, 2026 (Tunis Court of First Instance, terrorism chamber)
Ajmi Lourimi, Ennahda's secretary-general, and Moussab Gharbi were sentenced on July 3, 2026 by the terrorism-specialized criminal chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance to 3 years' imprisonment each. Both had been detained since July 2024, arrested at Borj El Amri in the company of a third man, Mohamed Ghanoudi, reportedly wanted in a counterterrorism probe. Lourimi was prosecuted for abstaining from reporting facts related to a terrorist offense; Gharbi faced charges of preparatory acts to harbor, conceal, and facilitate the flight of a person linked to terrorist offenses. The Tunisian human-rights NGO CRLDHT and the Tunisian Human Rights League (LTDH) both denounced the verdict — issued the same week as a separate sentence against Elyes Chaouachi — as a further step in the post-2021 pattern of prosecutions of Ennahda figures documented elsewhere in this section. Both defendants retain a right of appeal.
