Samir Abdelli CaseMoney Laundering & Terrorism-Related Offenses

Arrested: 2024 | First-instance verdict: July 7, 2026 (Tunis Court of First Instance, terrorism chamber)
Lawyer and former presidential candidate Samir Abdelli — previously named in reporting on the 2016 "Panama Papers" leak in connection with the Panama-registered Faygate Corp — was sentenced on July 7, 2026 by the terrorism-specialized criminal chamber of the Tunis Court of First Instance to 18 years' imprisonment at first instance, on charges combining money-laundering and terrorism-related offenses. His personal driver received a 3-year sentence and his secretary, tried while at liberty, received 2 years, in the same judgment. Abdelli had been held in pre-trial detention since his 2024 arrest. Part of the broader 2022–2026 pattern of prosecutions of opposition-linked lawyers and public figures before Tunisia's specialized terrorism and financial-crime chambers.
CriminalFinancial2026

Content-Creators Trafficking & Exploitation Case18 Defendants — Decree-Law 2022-54, Anti-Trafficking & Anti-Terrorism/AML Laws

Referral: May 16, 2026 | Verdict: July 7, 2026 (Tunis Court of First Instance)
Eighteen social-media content creators (TikTok/Instagram) were sentenced on July 7, 2026 by the Tunis Court of First Instance to terms of up to 27 years' imprisonment, in a prosecution combining three separate statutes: the Anti-Trafficking in Persons Law (Organic Law No. 2016-61), Decree-Law No. 2022-54 on information- and communication-system offenses, and the Anti-Terrorism Law (Law No. 2015-26). Charges included production, distribution, and download of pornographic images and videos (including content depicting minors and sexual assault), human trafficking, and money laundering. Referred to trial on May 16, 2026 after arrests earlier in the year amid a wider public controversy over unregulated commercial content creation on social platforms. Unlike most other 2026 entries in this section, this case centers on child-exploitation and trafficking allegations rather than political speech or opposition activity, though it marks a rare instance of the Anti-Trafficking Law and Decree-Law 2022-54 being applied jointly in a single prosecution.
CriminalDigital2026

Abdennaceur Aouini CaseAttempted Unlawful Entry — Common-Law Charge

First-instance (by default): 6 months | Opposition ruling: non-lieu | Prosecution appeal ruling: 1 year (Tunis Court of Appeal, correctional chamber, July 15, 2026)
Lawyer Abdennaceur Aouini — a member of the committee defending assassinated politicians Chokri Belaïd and Mohamed Brahmi (see the Belaïd assassination trial above) — was sentenced on July 15, 2026 by the correctional chamber of the Tunis Court of Appeal to one year's imprisonment, after the public prosecution appealed an earlier non-lieu (no-case-to-answer) ruling. The underlying complaint, filed by a fellow lawyer's wife, alleged that Aouini and two colleagues attempted to force entry into her home at night, one allegedly armed, to retrieve client documents; she said she was threatened and insulted. Unlike most other entries in this section, the charge itself (attempted unlawful entry) is an ordinary common-law offense rather than a security or speech-related statute. The Committee for the Defense of Belaïd and Brahmi, whose statement of solidarity with Aouini was reported by Kapitalis, frames the prosecution as retaliation for his public criticism of the judiciary and fair-trial standards at a May 1, 2026 lawyers' consultative assembly and his participation in a May 19, 2026 Tunis-region lawyers' strike. A July 17, 2026 La Presse follow-up, citing a judicial source responding directly to that characterization, pushed back: the source stated the case "relève du droit commun" (is an ordinary common-law matter, not a political or fair-trial-retaliation case) and that "les procédures légales ont été respectées" (legal procedures were properly observed), including that the investigation was opened on the Prosecutor General's own initiative. Confirmed via La Presse and Kapitalis, cross-checked against each other.
Criminal2026

🌍 International Law & Key Treaties

Key International Conventions Ratified by Tunisia

Various dates
Tunisia is party to numerous international conventions that form part of Tunisian law upon ratification (monist approach).
Major conventions
  • UN Charter (1956) — upon independence
  • Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948) — adopted, not treaty
  • International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights — ratified March 18, 1969
  • International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights — ratified March 18, 1969
  • Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) — ratified September 20, 1985 (with reservations; reservations partially lifted 2011)
  • Convention on the Rights of the Child — ratified January 29, 1992
  • UN Convention Against Torture — ratified September 23, 1988
  • African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights — ratified 1983
  • Arab Charter on Human Rights — ratified 2008
  • New York Convention on Arbitral Awards (1958) — ratified July 22, 1967
  • ILO Core Conventions (62 conventions ratified)
  • TRIPS Agreement — WTO member since March 29, 1995
  • WIPO Copyright Treaty — ratified 2012
  • Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT) — member since March 1, 2001
  • UN Convention Against Corruption (UNCAC) — ratified October 23, 2008
  • Association Agreement with the European Union (1998)
Constitutional

Private International Law Code — Law No. 98-97 of November 27, 1998Code de Droit International Privé

Enacted: November 27, 1998 | In force: 3 months after JORT publication | Non-retroactive
Governs cross-border private-law disputes involving Tunisia. Organized in five titles: jurisdiction of Tunisian courts in international disputes, conflict-of-laws rules for determining applicable law, recognition and enforcement of foreign judgments, and judicial and execution immunities. Tunisian courts have jurisdiction whenever the defendant is domiciled in Tunisia, regardless of nationality. A foundational text for international litigation, family-law conflicts (mixed marriages, cross-border custody), and commercial disputes involving foreign parties.
CivilConstitutional

EU-Tunisia Association Agreement — Law No. 97-37 of July 1, 1997

Signed: July 17, 1995 | Ratified by Tunisia: July 1, 1997 | In force: March 1, 1998
Association agreement between Tunisia and the European Union, the first such agreement with a Mediterranean country (Euro-Mediterranean Partnership). Established a free trade area for manufactured goods, progressively eliminating tariffs. Includes provisions on services, capital, intellectual property, and political dialogue. Tunisia achieved compliance with WTO rules under this agreement.
CommercialConstitutional

Arab Maghreb Union Treaty — 1989

Signed: February 17, 1989 (Marrakesh Treaty) | Ratified: 1989
Treaty establishing the Arab Maghreb Union (UMA) among Tunisia, Algeria, Morocco, Libya, and Mauritania. Aims for eventual economic integration. In practice, the union has been largely dormant due to Algeria-Morocco tensions. However, bilateral agreements within the AMU framework remain in force.
CommercialConstitutional

Withdrawal from the African Court on Human and Peoples' RightsArt. 34(6) Declaration Withdrawn

Withdrawal notified: March 7, 2025 | Effective: March 7, 2026
Tunisia notified the African Union Commission of its withdrawal of the Article 34(6) declaration under the Protocol establishing the African Court on Human and Peoples' Rights, which had allowed individuals and NGOs to bring cases directly against Tunisia before the Court. Under the Protocol's one-year notice rule, the withdrawal took effect March 7, 2026, ending that direct-access avenue (cases already filed before that date remain unaffected). Tunisia is the fifth African state to withdraw individual/NGO access, following Rwanda, Tanzania, Benin, and Côte d'Ivoire. Human Rights Watch, the ICJ, and EuroMed Rights condemned the move as a serious setback for human-rights accountability, noting it removes an external avenue for the very rights defenders and opposition figures documented in the Judicial Organization section's political-prosecution cases.
ConstitutionalAdministrative2026

🕒 Pending Reforms & Legislative Drafts

Tracked here for completeness: bills and draft codes that were under active parliamentary or ministerial review as of mid-2026 but had not yet been enacted. Treat these as reforms-in-progress, not current law — cross-referenced from the relevant sections above. Note (added v2.34, updated v2.37): the ARP closed its fourth ordinary session on the evening of July 29, 2026 (Art. 71 of the Constitution / Art. 77 of the ARP's internal rules), so none of the bills below can receive a further plenary vote until the next ordinary session opens; committee-level review may continue in the interim. In his closing address, ARP Speaker Ibrahim Bouderbala said the session had handled 107 bills across economic, health, energy, and social domains and set the next ordinary session to open October 7, 2026. Separately, a July 27–28 emergency plenary on nationwide water and electricity cuts (linked to a heatwave since July 10) saw at least two deputies say a motion of censure against the government was being prepared over the crisis; as of this pass no such motion had been formally filed, so it is noted here as a watch item rather than tracked as a bill.

Draft Organic Law on Personal Data Protection

Filed: July 2025 | Status: in committee review as of July 2026
Would replace Law No. 2004-63 with a GDPR-aligned framework, creating an independent Data Protection Authority (replacing INPDP), mandatory data-protection officers, graduated administrative fines up to TND 200,000, and new rules on biometrics, video surveillance, AI/profiling (four dedicated articles — a future standalone AI law remains a separate, unaddressed question; see the National AI Strategy entry below), and cross-border transfers. The AI/profiling provisions specifically include a right not to be subject to a decision based exclusively on automated processing (including profiling) with legal or similarly significant effects, and a mandatory impact-assessment requirement for high-risk processing operations; a dedicated chapter separately organizes health-data processing. Reviewed by the ARP's Rights and Liberties Committee since a February 27, 2026 hearing; the committee requested expanded consultation with outside experts and civil-society associations and sought further written opinions from ministries and public institutions as of May 2026, with review continuing into mid-2026 and not yet voted. Article-count reporting genuinely conflicts across two credible, dated sources rather than being a simple tracking error: La Presse (August 5, 2025) reported 132 articles across 6 chapters at the bill's initial filing, while WebManagerCenter (February 23, 2026) reported 123 articles across 6 chapters at the committee-hearing stage — the count may have changed between filing and committee-stage text, but no source confirms this explicitly, so both figures are given pending clarification against the official bill text.
DigitalDraft

Draft Organic Law on the Organization of AssociationsBill n°27/2023

Filed: October 10, 2023 | ARP study day: April 15, 2026 | Committee report deadline set by ARP Bureau: June 24, 2026
Would replace the liberal notification-based regime of Decree-Law No. 2011-88 with a prior-authorization system: association formation and, notably, receipt of foreign funding would require advance government approval rather than mere declaration, placing associations "under the control and supervision" of the Ministries of Interior and Foreign Affairs. Framed by supporters, including President Kais Saied, as necessary to prevent foreign interference and combat money laundering/terrorism financing; a Prime Ministerial commission was tasked with revising the text in December 2023. Reviewed by the ARP's Commission on Rights and Freedoms; at an April 10, 2026 hearing, committee president Thabet El Abed said the amended text "institutes a disguised authorization in the guise of declaration" on foreign financing and reinforces administrative control at the expense of associative autonomy. Following the April 15, 2026 parliamentary study day, the ARP Bureau set June 24, 2026 as the deadline for the committee to finalize its report; on May 22, 2026, Deputy Fatma Mseddi wrote to ARP leadership warning of a "blockage" of the bill (pending since October 2023) and pushed for the report and a plenary vote before the summer recess. No evidence was found that the committee report was transmitted to plenary before the ARP's fourth ordinary session closed July 29, 2026; not yet enacted as of this pass. Comparable bills were proposed and abandoned in 2016–2019 and 2022 amid civil-society mobilization. See also the 2025–2026 Article 45 suspension wave above, which is reshaping the same civic space this bill would formalize.
AdministrativeConstitutionalDraft2026

Draft Organic Law Amending the Nationality CodeBill n°17/2026

Filed: February 27–March 2, 2026 (sources give the deposit date differently) | Rights and Liberties Committee hearings: May 7 and May 20, 2026
Would amend and supplement several provisions of the 1963 Nationality Code (Law No. 63-6), tightening general access while opening a targeted fast track for sought-after talent. On naturalization, the bill would require legal entry to Tunisia and at least 7 years of continuous, regular residence before an application (up from the current 5 years in the Code's Art. 12), expressly conditioning eligibility on the applicant's compliance with the entry/residence rules of Law No. 68-7. It would also tighten birth-based acquisition: children born in Tunisia to stateless parents would qualify only if the parents have resided in the country 10+ years, filiation proof requirements would expand to include "scientific, technical and other" evidence, and foundlings would be presumed born on Tunisian territory unless proven otherwise. In parallel, it would create an accelerated naturalization route for foreigners with distinguished scientific, technological, artistic, cultural, sporting, or economic competencies, framed by sponsors as a tool to attract strategic talent and skills that could contribute to Tunisia's growth and international influence. Reviewed by the ARP's Rights and Liberties Committee alongside the Associations bill and Data Protection bill as one of three parallel "sensitive" legislative fronts; at the May 20, 2026 session, committee president Thabet El Abed reported members had proposed continuing hearings before finalizing recommendations, with the committee also raising open legal questions on proportionality, potential retroactive effect, and procedural guarantees. No floor vote or promulgation found as of this pass; not yet enacted. Cross-checked via Business News (May 7, 2026), Tuniscope, WebManagerCenter/Directinfo (May 20, 2026), and webdo (undated committee-status recap), which converge on the substantive provisions and review status despite the filing-date discrepancy noted above.
NationalityConstitutionalDraft2026

Draft Organic Law on the Constitutional Court (No. 100/2025)

Filed: July 2025 (prior attempt collapsed April 2025) | Status: committee hearings through February 2026
Filed July 15, 2025 by 21 deputies to activate Arts. 125–132 of the 2022 Constitution and finally establish the Constitutional Court, mandated since 2014 but never formed; a prior 2025 attempt was withdrawn April 23, 2025. The ARP Bureau examined the proposal on January 31 and again February 18, 2026, and the Venice Commission issued an opinion on a related draft (Opinion No. 817). Would grant the court jurisdiction over constitutionality review of laws and treaties. As of July 2026, no law has been enacted and no judges appointed.
ConstitutionalDraft

Draft Exchange Code Reform (Crypto-Assets)

Filed: October 20, 2025 (bill n°2025/115) | Finance & Budget Committee review opened: February 23, 2026 | CONECT submitted 41 amendments: June 1, 2026
Would comprehensively replace the exchange-control regime dating to Law No. 76-18 of 1976 and become Tunisia's first legal framework addressing digital/crypto assets: crypto-assets would become subject to a declaration regime with the Central Bank above a threshold to be set by decree — superseding the current outright BCT prohibition on cryptocurrency trading, payments, and mining in force since May 2018 — alongside provisions covering start-ups, freelancers, and platforms like PayPal. The bill, filed by 45 deputies, runs to 91 articles and introduces a new statutory definition of "resident" (natural and legal persons) as part of its broader framework. Note: the narrower liberalization letting residents open foreign-currency accounts without prior BCT authorization — long anticipated as this bill's headline feature — was in fact separately enacted ahead of it via Finance Law 2026's supplementary Article 81 (adopted December 2, 2025, in force January 1, 2026; see that entry above); this bill's remaining distinct contribution is the crypto-asset declaration regime, the new "resident" definition, and the start-up/freelancer/payment-platform provisions. As of May 2, 2026, employer groups (patronat) were pushing for a more liberal text while Parliament was reported to be "temporizing"; the Finance Ministry had signaled intent to accelerate adoption before the summer 2026 recess. By late June 2026 the Finance and Budget Committee's article-by-article review was reported roughly 90% complete, with the committee chair weighing whether to proceed to a vote without further delay or to keep awaiting input from the Central Bank (BCT), which had not responded to Parliament's requests. The pre-recess adoption target was missed. On July 23, 2026, Finance and Budget Committee chair Maher Kattari publicly rejected the Finance Minister's request to postpone examination to January 2027 — "we cannot wait another year" — and announced that the committee's first post-recess session, in October 2026, will take up the Code and vote it chapter by chapter even without the BCT's input, the Central Bank having not replied since April 2026 despite repeated official letters. Not yet enacted as of August 2026. See the base Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Code (Law No. 76-18) now documented in the Financial & Banking section above. Distinct from the narrower foreign-exchange violations regularization bill below, which predates this reform and is framed by its sponsors as a preliminary step toward it — the two should not be conflated.
FinancialDigitalDraft

Foreign-Exchange Violations Regularization BillProjet de Loi n°58/2025

Filed: ~May 2025 (Independent National Bloc) | ARP plenary vote to refer back to Finance Committee: April 14, 2026 (54–3–2)
A narrower amnesty measure, distinct from the broader Exchange Code reform above, that would let individuals holding undeclared foreign currency or assets abroad regularize them through repatriation and a declaration on honor. Establishes a "liberatory contribution" — set at 15% of the acquisition value for foreign real estate or financial assets — whose payment grants amnesty from exchange-control violations and related tax infractions on the regularized amounts; aimed particularly at Tunisian students and long-term workers abroad, and framed by supporters as a tool to reduce parallel-market foreign-currency circulation and support dinar stability. On April 14, 2026, the ARP plenary voted 54 in favor, 3 against, and 2 abstentions to refer the bill back to the Finance Committee at the initiating bloc's own request, citing a large volume of proposed amendments requiring fuller comparison with the original text before any new vote. Framed by its own sponsors as a preliminary phase ahead of the full Exchange Code revision, rather than a substitute for it. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
FinancialDraft2026

Mining Code Reform

Under revision since ~2023 | Ministerial call to accelerate: July 2025
Revision of the 2003 Mining Code to align with the 2022 Constitution's requirement that natural-resource agreements be approved by both chambers of Parliament. Affects a sector representing roughly 3% of GDP and 25,000+ jobs. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
AdministrativeDraft

Hydrocarbons Code Reform

Examined in restricted ministerial council: February 7, 2025 | National Council of Regions and Districts hearings: June 25–26, 2026
Would simplify exploitation-permit procedures, modernize the 1999 Hydrocarbons Code's provisions dating to 2002–2008 amendments, and strengthen environmental safeguards amid a sustained production decline since 2010, an absence of major new discoveries, and concessions set to expire 2027–2031. A restricted ministerial council (Conseil Ministériel Restreint) examined a bill to amend the Hydrocarbons Code; the productive-sectors committee of the National Council of Regions and Districts separately held hearings with Energy and Mines Ministry officials on June 25–26, 2026 pressing for urgent revision and greater sector transparency. A July 3, 2026 IACE (Institut Arabe des Chefs d'Entreprises) report corroborates that this draft, like the codes in the entry below, has been "technically finalized and validated in Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP." Sector observers continue to call the revision "urgent" — not yet enacted as of July 2026.
EnvironmentalDraft

Draft Investment, Water & Environmental Framework Code RevisionsStalled Pre-Parliament

Reported: IACE (Institut Arabe des Chefs d'Entreprises), July 3, 2026, via Tunisie Tribune; Water Code status updated per La Presse de Tunisie, July 30 and August 9, 2026
A July 3, 2026 IACE report identified three further draft code revisions — a new Investment Code (replacing Law No. 2016-71), a revised Water Code (replacing the 1975 Water Code, Law No. 75-16), and a revised Environmental Framework Code (replacing Law No. 88-91) — as "technically finalized and validated in Council of Ministers but never transmitted to the ARP," a status the same report says also applies to the Hydrocarbons Code reform above. Unlike the Constitutional Court, data-protection, or electoral-code bills tracked elsewhere in this section, none of these three drafts appear to have reached parliamentary committee stage at all — the bottleneck sits at the executive-to-legislature handoff. Independent corroboration and a procedural update for the Water Code specifically: following a February 25, 2025 restricted ministerial council that tasked the Ministry of Agriculture and the Prime Minister's office with finalizing the text (a project first initiated in 2019, stalled by post-2022 political and institutional restructuring, and rewritten to align with the 2022 Constitution), an amended version incorporating stakeholder observations — with an explanatory table of integrated comments, a preliminary set of implementing texts, and a reform note — was transmitted to the competent services on June 30, 2026; as of an August 9, 2026 report responding to Deputy Awatef Cheniti's public concern over the delay, the text remained on track for imminent submission to the Council of Ministers, ahead of formal transmission to the ARP, but had not yet reached either body. A June 2026 restricted ministerial council additionally linked the reform to a water strategy through 2050 tying water-sector energy use to renewable-energy targets (photovoltaic-powered pumping stations by 2030), per the Tunisian Water Observatory's advocacy. No enacted text for any of the three codes as of this pass.
CommercialEnvironmentalDraft

Startup Act 2.0 Amendments

Reported under discussion: March 20, 2026 (earliest identified report) and April 17, 2026 (La Presse)
A package of amendments to the 2018 Startup Act (Law No. 2018-20) — informally dubbed "Startup Act 2.0" — was reported under discussion between the relevant ministries and Parliament since at least March 2026, aimed at updating the "Startup" label's tax and social-security incentives and eligibility criteria for the ecosystem's next phase. Reported proposals include: deferred taxation of stock-options at sale rather than at attribution, with discounts of up to 20% on market value, and extended eligibility to contractors/advisors beyond employees; extending the maximum label duration from 8 to 10 years (with an intermediate 5-year threshold for deep-tech/hardware ventures); replacing the current benefits "cliff" at label expiration with a progressive phase-out; a single-window, 30-day-target process for foreign hires' work/residence permits in place of existing recruitment quotas; and higher tax-relief caps for startup investors, with clearer treatment for corporate venture capital/family offices and loss recognition for unrecovered investments. Unresolved points as of mid-2026 include the labeling committee's criticized lack of transparency, alignment with the pending Exchange Code reform, and treatment of foreign startups opening Tunisian subsidiaries. No bill text, filing number, or committee referral date was independently confirmed, and no vote has occurred as of July 2026.
CommercialDigitalDraft

Single-Use Plastic Bags Restriction Bill

Widely reported: July 5–6, 2026 (La Presse, African Manager, WebManagerCenter, Tunisie Tribune); sponsor/article detail: Tekiano, July 6, 2026
A 23-article proposition de loi carried by 25 ARP deputies, framed as establishing a circular-economy approach to plastic pollution (Tunisia consumes an estimated 4.2 billion single-use plastic bags per year, roughly 400 per inhabitant). Would ban single-use plastic bags at retail checkouts outright upon entry into force, with a 12-month grace period for industrialists and importers to cease production, import, or distribution; only reusable bags (minimum 50-micron thickness, rated for 20+ uses) or biodegradable bags would remain permitted. Violators face fines of TND 5,000 to 50,000 plus product seizure, escalating to imprisonment for repeat offenders, alongside a proposed national industrial-reconversion program of subsidized loans, tax incentives, and innovation aid for businesses developing alternatives. Widely covered in Tunisian press in early-to-mid July 2026, but remains a bill under ARP review, not an enacted law — a July 6, 2026 Business News report separately debunked a circulating rumor that a related Road Code change had already taken effect on July 1, 2026 (the Road Traffic Code in force remains unchanged, per Law No. 99-71). Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
EnvironmentalDraft

Draft Organic Law on Sport (Sports Structures)

Reform launched: June 2025 | Status: reported stalled, May 2025 press ("pourquoi ça traîne encore")
Would replace the 1995 sports law with a 211-article, 7-chapter framework covering federation/club governance, democratization and financial transparency of sports associations, the Olympic and Paralympic Committees, and new independent "sports enterprise" structures. Distinct from the narrower stadium-violence bill below, which is moving faster through committee. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
AdministrativeDraft

Draft Law No. 83/2025Combating Violence in and Around Sports Stadiums

Filed: 2025 | Education, Youth & Sports Committee review: 2025–2026
A narrower, faster-moving bill (distinct from the broader Sports Code above) that would set 6 months–5 years' imprisonment for stadium violence, minimum TND 5,000 fines for throwing projectiles or setting off smoke/pyrotechnic devices, stadium bans of 5 years (life for repeat offenders), 5–10 years' imprisonment for bringing weapons to sporting events, and club fines starting at TND 50,000 for repeated supporter violence. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
CriminalAdministrativeDraft

Tourism Sector Bills (Three Separate Proposals)

In ARP committee discussion since March 2026, targeting the longer-term "Tourism 2035" strategy
Rather than a single unified code, three distinct bills are advancing: (1) reform of tour-guide regulation, replacing rules dating to the 1970s; (2) a national "social tourism" system reserving discounted hotel quotas for lower-income families; and (3) regulation of short-term furnished-apartment rentals (price control, mandatory registry, and a crackdown on undeclared rentals). A unified "Code du Tourisme" remains a longer-term objective, not yet drafted as a single text. In the meantime, tourist-tax and entry-requirement changes have been implemented through the 2024–2025 Finance Laws (quadrupled stay tax, new per-night tariff grid, 3-month passport-validity rule from January 2025). None of the three bills had been enacted as of July 2026.
AdministrativeDraft

Competition and Prices Law Reform

Ministry of Commerce reform announced: April 2025 | Referred to Industry/Commerce and Agriculture committees: February 2025
Would revise Law No. 2015-36 on competition and prices; accompanied by a memorandum of understanding with the COMESA Competition Commission to align Tunisian competition law with regional standards. The Observatoire Tunisien de l'Économie has criticized the draft as a "missed opportunity" for deeper reform. The existing Competition Council remains active in the interim — it issued a TND 4.2 million fine in January 2026. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
CommercialDraft

Draft Organic Law Reorganizing HAICA

Filed by 16 deputies: ~late February 2025 | Referred to Commission of General Legislation: April 11, 2025 | Committee jurisdictional review: May 20, 2026
A 99-article, 5-chapter proposition de loi organique that would repeal Decree-Law No. 2011-116 (which created HAICA) and replace the audiovisual regulator — whose board has been vacant at the chair level since January 2023 — with a new permanent 9-member authority, barring politicians and association heads from owning broadcast media. As of the ARP Rights and Freedoms Committee's May 20, 2026 session, Parliament was still resolving which committee has jurisdiction to lead review; no substantive vote had occurred. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
PressDraft

Draft Digital Code (Dormant) — Projet de Loi n°80/2019

Filed/approved by Council of Ministers: December 2019 | Last recorded committee activity: March 2020
A draft code (reported as 251 articles per the ARP's official Marsad/Al Bawsala legislative tracker, though press coverage cites 258) developed with input from UGTT and employer organizations, intended to fully replace the 2001 Telecommunications Code. Approved by the Council of Ministers and transmitted to Parliament in December 2019, it has sat in "discussion en commission" since March 2020 with only one recorded committee session; no evidence of revival has surfaced through the 2021–2022 parliamentary dissolution or the 2023–2026 Assembly. Effectively dormant, not actively advancing, as of July 2026.
DigitalDraft

Draft Cybersecurity Law — Proposition de Loi n°2024-36

General Legislation Commission hearing (Minister Nizar Ben Neji): January 25 & 29, 2024 | Filed as Proposition de Loi n°2024-36: April 30, 2024 | Administrative Organization, Digitalization, Governance & Anti-Corruption Commission hearing: June 7, 2024 | Status: last confirmed committee activity June 2024; no 2025–2026 movement located
A standalone 54-article cybersecurity bill, distinct from the narrower institutional rename already documented in the ANSI/ANCS entry above (Decree-Law No. 2023-17 of March 11, 2023, which replaced ANSI with the National Cybersecurity Agency but did not itself create a comprehensive statutory cybersecurity framework). Would define "cybersecurity" in law for the first time, require public entities, local authorities, and operators of "vital importance" critical infrastructure to adopt security policies, classify sensitive information assets, undergo regular audits, and report incidents to a national authority; create a Strategic Cybersecurity Committee alongside the existing National Cybersecurity Agency (ANCS) for governance and critical-infrastructure oversight; mandate data localization for sensitive data ("l'hébergement des données sensibles... limité au territoire national"); require business-continuity/disaster-recovery planning; and set penalties reported at TND 20,000–60,000, alongside training and international-cooperation provisions, framed partly as implementing Tunisia's obligations under the Council of Europe's Budapest Convention on Cybercrime. The ARP's General Legislation Commission first heard Minister of Communication Technologies Nizar Ben Neji on the bill January 25 and 29, 2024 (with Foreign Affairs and Interior ministry officials also heard); it was formally filed/numbered as Proposition de Loi n°2024-36 on April 30, 2024, and was separately reviewed by the Administrative Organization, Digitalization, Governance and Anti-Corruption Commission on June 7, 2024, which agreed to hold further stakeholder-consultation sessions. No subsequent hearing, article-by-article vote, or floor referral was located despite a dedicated search of 2025–2026 coverage; distinct from the unrelated, separately-stalled "cybercriminalité" (cybercrime-penalties) bill reported blocked by the Interior Ministry in early 2024, which this artifact does not track as a separate item pending further confirmation of its status. Confirmed via legislation-securite.tn (bill text and April 30, 2024 filing date), WebManagerCenter (June 7, 2024), and legal-doctrine.com, cross-checked against each other; effectively dormant for roughly two years, similar in pattern to the Digital Code above, as of this pass. A related but distinct executive-branch development: L'Économiste Maghrébin reported August 7, 2026 that a "Stratégie Nationale de Cybersécurité 2026-2030" had been finalized, imposing 12-month security-audit obligations on cloud providers, public-network operators, and critical digital infrastructure — a policy strategy document, not a statute, and not confirmation this bill itself has advanced; the bill's own parliamentary status remains unchanged as of the v2.88 pass (August 25, 2026).
DigitalSecurityDraft

Status: ARP committee review accelerated, reported June 1, 2026
A narrower, actively-moving bill (distinct from the dormant Digital Code above) updating regulation of online commerce, reported by La Presse de Tunisie as advancing through ARP committee review in June 2026. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.

Draft Unified Electoral Code — "Code Électoral et du Référendum"

Filed by 29 deputies | ARP bureau referral: Thursday, May 7, 2026 | Committee hearing: May 19, 2026
A 261-article organic-law proposal in three parts (general elections/referendums; regional and district council elections; transitional provisions) that would consolidate Tunisia's scattered electoral legislation — Organic Law No. 2014-16 and its amendments, Decree-Law No. 2011-35, and related texts — into a single code. Referred by the ARP bureau to the Commission on Internal Regulations, Electoral Laws, Parliamentary Laws and Electoral Function; that committee opened hearings with the initiating deputies on May 19, 2026, debating provisions including a proposed 5,000 TND candidacy deposit and candidate education-level requirements. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
ConstitutionalAdministrativeDraft

Informal-Housing Land Regularization Bill

Transmitted to committee: July 2025 | Committee hearings: February 2026 | Committee approved amended text: June 22, 2026 | Awaiting plenary vote
A 12-article bill that would regularize buildings constructed without authorization, or in breach of urban-planning rules, before December 31, 2025 — affecting an estimated 800,000 to 1 million residents across more than 1,400 informal neighborhoods. Article 12 would penalize future unauthorized construction with demolition or a fine equal to three times the land's value. The ARP's Strategic Planning, Sustainable Development, Transport, Infrastructure and Territorial Planning Committee approved an amended version on June 22, 2026; the bill next requires a committee report and a plenary vote, which had not occurred as of early July 2026. Distinct from the separate "second-generation" national urban-renewal program covering roughly 160 neighborhoods.
LandAdministrativeDraft

Draft Organic Law to Designate Ennahda a "Terrorist Organization"

Filed by MP Fatma Mseddi: July 9, 2026 | Signature-gathering announced: March 2026 | Status: newly filed, not yet referred to committee
A 6-article organic-law proposal filed with the ARP on July 9, 2026 that would declare Ennahda a political and organizational "continuation" of the Muslim Brotherhood in Tunisia and formally classify it, and any association or structure shown to have an organizational, financial, or functional link to it, as a "terrorist organization." Would trigger immediate dissolution of the targeted organizations, strip office-holders found to belong to them of their representative/parliamentary status, and bar those individuals from standing for election or holding public office for 10 years. Framed by its sponsor as protecting "national security, state sovereignty, and the republican system" and preventing parties/associations from serving as fronts for terrorist organizations; MP Mseddi had announced she was collecting the required co-sponsor signatures since March 2026. Distinct from, but arising against the backdrop of, the mass criminal prosecutions of Ennahda leadership already documented in the Judicial Organization section (the "Conspiracy 2", "Secret Apparatus", and Instalingo cases). Not yet referred to an ARP committee as of this pass; no vote scheduled.

Bill Amending Law No. 2016-68 on the National Council of Tunisians Residing Abroad

Under ARP committee review as of July 2026 | Foreign Relations Committee adopted text in full: July 14, 2026 | Status: report to be transmitted to ARP Bureau, pending scheduling of a plenary session
A bill amending Law No. 2016-68 of August 3, 2016 (which created the National Council for Tunisians Residing Abroad and set its powers, composition, and operating rules), restructuring the diaspora advisory body. The ARP's Committee on External Relations, International Cooperation, Affairs of Tunisians Abroad and Migration adopted the amended text in its entirety on July 14, 2026. Key changes: the Council would be attached to the Presidency of Government (strengthening diaspora-policy coordination); its permanent membership would combine the deputies elected from overseas constituencies with sixty members representing Tunisians abroad, plus non-voting observer members from relevant ministries and public bodies; membership would be unpaid (no salary or allowance); and the reform introduces individual voting (allowing the diaspora to directly choose its Council representatives) and electronic/internet voting to widen participation. Not yet put to an ARP plenary vote as of this pass.
AdministrativeDraft2026

Possible Complementary Finance Law for 2026Loi de Finances Complémentaire

Publicly floated: July 25, 2026 (Maher El Kattari, ARP Finance and Budget Committee chair) | Possible submission to Parliament: October 2026 | Status: no bill filed
Not yet a filed bill: on July 25, 2026, the chair of the ARP's Finance and Budget Committee publicly stated that Tunisia "could" adopt a complementary/supplementary finance law for 2026, since the enacted Finance Law 2026 (Law No. 2025-17 of December 12, 2025) was built on an assumed oil price of USD 63/barrel while actual prices have since exceeded USD 100/barrel — each USD 50/barrel increase adding an estimated TND 500 million in monthly fiscal cost. He indicated any resulting bill could be submitted to Parliament in October 2026, but as of this pass no draft text, filing number, or Council of Ministers examination had occurred. Tracked here as an early-stage possibility rather than an active reform, consistent with this artifact's practice for the Startup Act 2.0 entry at an equivalent pre-filing stage; single-sourced as of this pass (La Presse de Tunisie, July 25, 2026) pending independent corroboration.
FinancialDraft2026

Draft Finance Law 2027Projet de Loi de Finances 2027 — Orientation-Setting Stage

Restricted ministerial council on the bill's broad orientations: August 4, 2026 (chaired by Head of Government Sarra Zaâfrani Zenzri) | Status: pre-bill — orientations only, no draft text filed with the ARP
Not yet a filed bill: on August 4, 2026, Head of Government Sarra Zaâfrani Zenzri chaired a restricted ministerial council ("Conseil ministériel restreint") setting the broad orientations for the 2027 Finance Law, the first formal step in each year's budget-drafting cycle. Finance Minister Méchket Slama Khaldi presented an assessment of 2026 budget execution and the macroeconomic assumptions underpinning the 2027 draft. The stated orientations — framed as continuing the 2026–2030 Development Plan's "bottom-up," participatory approach — center on stimulating wealth creation by valuing national resources and high-value-added sectors rather than prior public-resource-management models; a "self-reliance" (al-taʿwil ʿala al-dhat) principle aimed at strengthening economic sovereignty; maintaining fiscal resilience amid global crises and fallout from the 2026 conflict through economic diversification and stronger production systems; protecting vulnerable groups' purchasing power; prioritizing food, water, and energy security; reforming public enterprises and the healthcare system; advancing digital transformation and balanced regional development; prioritizing youth employment; and strengthening tax equity, including further integration of the informal economy into the tax base. No specific tax measures, expenditure figures, or article-level detail have been disclosed as of this pass — distinct in both subject and procedural stage from the separate, oil-price-driven possible complementary finance law for 2026 above, which would amend the already-enacted Finance Law 2026 rather than set terms for the following year's budget. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie, Business News, African Manager, Managers.tn, RTCI, Le Temps News, and Tunisie Tribune, cross-checked against each other for the August 4, 2026 date and orientations. This entry closes a standing gap: the item had been referenced and re-verified as "unchanged" in this artifact's own v2.49–v2.53 changelog entries without ever having been given its own Pending Reforms card — an oversight corrected in this pass. Update (August 19, 2026): a further Conseil ministériel chaired by the Head of Government adopted the budget économique 2027 — the macroeconomic framework that feeds the eventual finance bill — reporting H1-2026 growth of 2.4% and unemployment easing to 14.9% (from 15.3%), and setting priorities on the business climate, investment, food and water sovereignty, energy and digital transition, artificial intelligence, and public-enterprise reform. Still a pre-bill stage: no draft text has been filed with the ARP, which does not reconvene in ordinary session until October 7, 2026.

Draft Law on Aesthetic Medicine Regulation

Submitted to ARP Health Commission: July 23, 2026 (National Syndicate of Aesthetic Medicine Doctors) | Status: heard in committee, not yet referred to a vote
A draft law proposed by the National Syndicate of Aesthetic Medicine Doctors, presented to the ARP's Health Commission on July 23, 2026 by syndicate president Dr. Iman Ben Fredj, in response to a documented rise in unlicensed cosmetic procedures causing severe complications. Would criminalize the illegal practice of aesthetic medicine by unlicensed/clandestine practitioners, impose strict controls on the importation and traceability of injectable products (syndicate testimony described products of "unknown origin" with no Ministry of Health validation circulating via social-media-advertised services), set standards for facilities authorized to perform such procedures, regulate advertising of aesthetic services on media and digital platforms, and reserve aesthetic procedures to qualified physicians. The Ministry of Health has separately committed to publishing detailed operating specifications ("cahier des charges") for the sector. Earlier-stage than the Startup Act 2.0 or complementary-finance-law entries above — a syndicate-authored proposal heard once in committee, not yet a bill formally adopted by it — confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie and allAfrica's July 23, 2026 coverage of the same hearing. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.
HealthDraft2026

Draft Law on Sign Language RecognitionProposition de Loi n°71/2024

Filed: 2024 | Health Commission hearing (OTDDPH, Ibsar): February 9, 2026 | Article-by-article review continuing: May 20, 2026
A 14-chapter proposition de loi that would formally organize the adoption of sign language for deaf and hard-of-hearing persons, later expanded during committee review to also cover people with total or partial speech loss, requiring revisions to the bill's title and definitions. Would require the state to unify sign language at the national level and mandate its integration into education, public services, legislation and parliamentary work, information and communication, litigation, and public spaces. The ARP's Health, Women, Family, Social Affairs and Disabled Persons Commission held a dedicated hearing on February 9, 2026 with the Tunisian Organization for the Defense of the Rights of People with Disabilities (OTDDPH) and the association Ibsar, and continued article-by-article debate on May 20, 2026 (the same session that examined the retiree-benefits bill below). Not yet put to a plenary vote as of July 2026.
HealthSocialDraft

Proposition de Loi n°30/2026Improving Retirees' Living Conditions (Cumul Emploi-Retraite)

Reported under committee discussion: May 8, 2026 | Health Commission article-by-article debate: May 20, 2026
A bill before the ARP's Health, Women, Family, Social Affairs and Disabled Persons Commission that would lift the current legal prohibition on combining a retirement pension with income from another paid professional activity ("cumul emploi-retraite"), framed by sponsors as preserving retirees' dignity and making use of their professional expertise; Article 5, authorizing the cumul, drew the most committee debate, with members weighing whether conditions or safeguards should attach. Would also grant employers a 30% social-security-contribution exemption for hiring experienced retirees for consulting, mentoring, or training roles, and would extend new social benefits to public- and private-sector retirees (including those residing abroad): at least a 30% reduction on urban and rail public-transport fares via a dedicated "retiree transport card," discounts on tourism and free access to museums, and priority access to healthcare. Debated article-by-article on May 20, 2026 (the same session covering the sign-language bill above); not yet referred to a plenary vote as of July 2026.
LaborSocialDraft2026

Proposition de Loi n°29/2026Organization of Nurseries and Kindergartens (Petite Enfance)

Health Commission hearings: May 10 and June 10, 2026 | Sponsor: Raouf Fekiri (Health Commission rapporteur) and co-initiators
A 30-article proposition de loi that would replace the sector's current reliance on administrative "cahiers des charges" (regulatory specification documents) with a formal legislative framework governing crèches and jardins d'enfants (nurseries and kindergartens), aiming to combat unregulated/"anarchic" facility proliferation, strengthen child-protection and educational/psychological safeguards, and modernize registration and oversight procedures (including digitalization) while granting facilities greater management flexibility. The ARP's Health Commission has held a series of hearings with sector specialists, professionals, associations, and the Ministry of Family, Women, Children and Elderly Affairs, including sessions on May 10 and June 10, 2026; rapporteur Raouf Fekiri voiced hope for passage before the start of the next school year, though this remains a stated aspiration rather than a confirmed timeline. Distinct from, but touching a similar population as, the earlier February 2026 committee discussion pairing this bill with a separate hearing-disability code proposal. Not yet enacted as of July 2026.

Draft General Civilian Drone Regulation Law

First disclosed: November 12, 2025 (Defense Minister Khaled Shili, ARP joint plenary budget session, Bardo Palace) | Re-confirmed: December 4, 2025 (Finance Minister Méchket Salama, during Finance Law 2026 debate) | Status: draft text finalized and circulated for stakeholder observations; not yet filed as an ARP bill
Distinct from the narrow agricultural exemption enacted via Article 135 of the Finance Law 2026 (which authorizes drone use only for farming/pesticide-spraying purposes), a comprehensive, standalone civilian drone-regulation text has been under inter-ministerial development since at least November 2025: at a November 12, 2025 joint plenary budget session, Defense Minister Khaled Shili told the ARP that his ministry had coordinated with the Transport Ministry to form a commission — under the General Directorate of Civil Aviation — that had by then finalized a regulatory draft and submitted it for observations to concerned stakeholders, while stressing continued Defense/Interior/Transport coordination on airspace-security risks. Finance Minister Méchket Salama separately referenced the same general-regulation effort during the December 4, 2025 Finance Law debate. Tunisia's default civilian drone posture otherwise remains prohibitive — use outside the new agricultural exemption requires exceptional authorization, historically issued by military/security authorities on a case-by-case basis. No bill number, ARP filing date, or committee referral had been identified as of this pass, so despite the draft text reportedly being complete, this is tracked as a pre-filing item, consistent with this artifact's practice for similarly-staged entries such as the Startup Act 2.0 and complementary finance law.
AdministrativeSecurityDraft

Draft Framework Law on Tunisian Engineers AbroadLoi-cadre pour la Diaspora Ingénieure

Transmitted to ARP President: July 16, 2026 | Sponsor: Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens (OIT)
A framework bill drafted by the Tunisian Order of Engineers (Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens) and transmitted to the President of the ARP on July 16, 2026, aimed at creating an incentive-based legal framework to reconnect Tunisia with its engineering diaspora and value its expertise, reframing outward emigration ("brain drain") as a strategic asset for economic development, innovation, and knowledge transfer rather than a pure loss. Would establish mechanisms for the diaspora's contribution to national development through voluntary return, knowledge and know-how transfer, remote mentoring/training, and technological partnerships and investment. Follows a June 22, 2026 meeting of the ARP's Committee on External Relations, International Cooperation, Affairs of Tunisians Abroad and Migration on strengthening ties with expatriate skills, and is distinct from the separate bill restructuring the National Council of Tunisians Residing Abroad. OIT dean Mohsen Gharsi publicly renewed the call for urgent promulgation on July 27, 2026 (national radio, "Yaoum Saïd"). Not yet formally registered as a numbered ARP bill or referred to committee as of this pass.
LaborNationalityDraft2026

Proposition de Loi on Natural Resources Governance and Sovereignty

Filed: late January 2026 (10 ARP deputies) | Hearing before Industry, Commerce, Natural Resources, Energy & Environment Committee: April 2026 | Status: committee requested clarification, drafting continuing
A 17-article proposition de loi covering oil, gas, phosphate, minerals, water, forests (including roughly 1.25 million hectares of underused forest land), and marine resources — distinct from, and broader than, the sector-specific Mining Code and Hydrocarbons Code reforms above. Would require ARP approval and public disclosure for all resource-exploitation contracts; create a Sovereign Natural Resources Fund intended to preserve rights for future generations, with revenues split 60% to the state (25% of that share earmarked for producing regions) and spending restricted to education, health, infrastructure, and strategic projects; establish a High Independent Instance for Natural Resources with oversight powers, including requiring major state enterprises (ETAP, CPG) to publish detailed annual activity, finance, and contract reports on a public electronic platform; and introduce criminal penalties for falsifying resource data. At its April 2026 committee hearing, deputies endorsed the transparency and sovereignty goals but asked sponsors to clarify the underlying legal concepts of state ownership/management and to precisely define which contract types would require parliamentary approval. Not yet enacted or referred to a floor vote as of this pass.
EnvironmentalFinancialDraft2026

Draft Organic Law on the Right to Asylum

Filed: 2016 | Status: dormant — no recorded committee activity located; UNHCR asylum processing in Tunisia suspended since June 2024
A draft organic law, pending before the ARP since 2016 and never adopted, that would give effect to Art. 26 of the 2014/2022 Constitution (guaranteeing the right to political asylum and prohibiting extradition of political refugees) and to Tunisia's obligations under the 1951 Refugee Convention (ratified October 24, 1957) and its 1967 Protocol. Article 27 of the draft would create the Instance Nationale de Protection des Réfugiés (INPR), an autonomous body with its own budget and decision-making power, as the competent authority for refugee-status determination — a function currently exercised, in the law's continued absence, by UNHCR under its own mandate per the Entry and Residence of Foreigners Law above. Tunisian authorities suspended UNHCR's asylum-registration and status-determination activities in the country in June 2024, and the EU's February 2026 addition of Tunisia to its first common list of "safe countries of origin" has drawn renewed rights-group criticism of the domestic legal vacuum. No bill number, revival, or committee referral located as of this pass; among the longest-dormant items tracked in this section, alongside the Digital Code above.
NationalityAdministrativeDraft

Proposition de Loi Revising the Narcotics Law (Law No. 92-52)

Transmitted to the General Legislation Committee: April 2025 | Committee hearings: February 26, May 7–8, May 16, May 18, and May 21, 2026 | Status: in committee, no floor vote
A 40-article proposition de loi to substantially rework the 1992 Narcotics and Psychotropic Substances Law, seeking a balance between harsher penalties for organized trafficking networks and reduced, care-oriented sanctions for personal consumption — the same "repression vs. treatment" tension that prompted the law's more limited 2017 sentencing-discretion amendment. Would repeal and replace Articles 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 11, and 18 of the 1992 law, raise fines, introduce new investigative techniques against trafficking networks, and add protections for security personnel involved in narcotics enforcement. The General Legislation Committee opened examination February 26, 2026 and has since heard criminal-law academics Béchir Mannoubi Ferchichi and Sihem Achour (May 16 and 18, 2026) and representatives of the National Council of the Order of Pharmacists and the Union of Private Pharmacy Owners (May 7–8, 2026), who argued for reduced custodial sentences for consumers seeking treatment in exchange for tougher penalties on traffickers, and separately flagged the unrelated 1969 Toxic Substances Law as failing to protect pharmacists in dispensing controlled medication. No consolidated committee text, article-by-article vote, or floor referral located as of this pass. Confirmed via African Manager, webdo.tn, La Presse de Tunisie, Kapitalis, Business News, and Le Temps News, cross-checked against each other for the April 2025 filing date and 40-article count.
CriminalHealthDraft2026

New Labor Code & Twin Bills on Telework and the Platform Economy

First disclosed: July 20–21, 2026 (8th Arab Conference on Pensions and Social Insurance, Tunis) | Broader "new Labor Code" framing announced: August 6, 2026 | Sponsor: Minister of Social Affairs Issam Lahmar | Status: two draft laws reported "practically finalized," not yet published or filed as ARP bills
Distinct from, and considerably broader than, the 2025 subcontracting reform and 2026 minimum-wage decrees already documented in the enacted Labor Code and subcontracting-ban entries above. At the 8th Arab Conference on Pensions and Social Insurance (Tunis, July 20–22, 2026), Minister of Social Affairs Issam Lahmar first disclosed two companion draft laws — one regulating telework in the private sector, the other creating a legal framework for the digital platform economy (delivery, ride-hailing, and service-app workers) — describing both as "practically finalized," though as of this pass their text has not been published. On August 6, 2026, Lahmar broadened the framing, stating Tunisia is moving toward drafting an entirely new Labor Code to replace provisions dating back decades, incorporating "unprecedented" telework provisions (working hours, data protection, employer-provided equipment, and a right to disconnect — areas the minister said remain "insufficiently regulated" under current law), a legal status and social-protection framework for platform workers (whose status "often remains unclear"), updated occupational health and safety standards, and closer alignment with ILO norms. The two draft laws' stated objectives include extending social-security coverage to previously uncovered forms of work, a progressive rollout prioritizing vulnerable worker categories, and strengthened contribution-collection and anti-fraud controls — themes echoed in a related, but more diffuse, late-July ministerial announcement of a broader pension/social-security "philosophy shift" (including possible easing of restrictions on pension recipients holding paid work) that has not itself produced a filed bill and is not separately tracked here. No bill number, ARP filing date, or committee referral had been identified as of this pass. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie (July 20, 2026), Managers.tn (July 21, 2026), and webdo.tn (August 7, 2026), cross-checked against each other for the sponsor, the two-bill structure, and the July/August timeline. Update (August 18, 2026): a Conseil ministériel chaired by Prime Minister Sarra Zaâfrani Zenzri at the Kasbah, at which Minister Lahmar presented the sector diagnostic, formally ordered the immediate drafting of the legislative texts for a structural social-security reform — moving this file from ministerial intent to an active drafting mandate. Announced scope: revision of pension regimes; extension of coverage to informal workers, farmers, fishermen, the self-employed, and platform-economy workers and new professions; integration of the social funds with the health system; digitalisation of health and affiliation files; and local pharmaceutical and vaccine production. No figures were released on retirement age or contribution periods, and no text has been published or filed. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie, Business News, Managers.tn, and Tunisie Numérique (all August 18–19, 2026).
LaborDigitalSocial SecurityDraft2026

Proposition de Loi n°36/2026 on Freelancer Status & International Digital Payments

Filed: August 10, 2026 (approx. 11 ARP deputies) | Status: deposited, awaiting committee referral
A proposition de loi aimed at formalizing independent digital work and easing cross-border payments for Tunisian freelancers working with foreign clients — IT development, design, translation, consulting, digital marketing, and content creation are cited as the primary target occupations. Distinct from, and narrower than, the broader Exchange Code reform above (which also touches freelancer foreign-currency accounts and platforms like PayPal as part of a wider 91-article overhaul of the 1976 exchange-control regime): this bill instead proposes (1) a dedicated legal/administrative status for freelancers, (2) formal access to international payment platforms including PayPal, (3) a flat 15% levy on freelance earnings, (4) social-security and other administrative protections for freelancers, and (5) a phased implementation timeline. Positioned by its sponsors as more comprehensive than the existing November 2024 self-employment (auto-entrepreneur) framework specifically for digital/international freelance work. No committee referral, hearing date, or floor vote located as of this pass; confirmed via Le Courrier de l'Atlas (August 10, 2026). Not yet enacted.

Draft Reform of Private Overseas Employment Placement

Ministry finalization reported: April 2025 (Ministry of Employment and Vocational Training, with ILO) | Planned submission to Government Presidency: May 2025 | Status: last confirmed at ministry-drafting stage; no ARP filing or bill number located as of this pass
A ministry-led overhaul of the 2010 private overseas-placement framework (Law No. 2010-49) above, developed following an April 2025 ministry–ILO workshop and press briefings confirming the text was being finalized for submission to the Government Presidency in May 2025. Would replace the current prior-authorization regime with a specifications-based system (cahier des charges) for private placement establishments, while sharply strengthening enforcement: prison sentences of up to 5 years for exercising the placement activity without authorization (explicitly including through shell/fictitious companies or via social media), fines of TND 10,000–20,000, fraud liability for authorized-company managers who abuse the framework, ministerial power to order immediate closure of illegal operations and investigate suspicious online platforms, and a mandatory 6-month post-arrival follow-up obligation on authorized placement companies for candidates newly arrived in the host country. Confirmed via La Presse de Tunisie, webdo.tn, L'Économiste Maghrébin, African Manager, and Tuniscope (all April 2025). No subsequent press coverage, ARP bill number, or committee referral was located as of this pass — the reform's status between its reported May 2025 submission target and August 2026 could not be independently verified; tracked here as an open item pending confirmation of either enactment or continued draft status.

Draft Organic Law on Sports Structures

Committee examination opened: March 6, 2026 (ARP Education, Vocational Training, Scientific Research, Youth & Sports Committee) | Council of Ministers presentation: reported 2026 | Status: article-by-article committee review ongoing through at least May 2026
A comprehensive overhaul of the legal and regulatory framework governing Tunisian sports institutions, aimed at replacing rules the committee's chair described as no longer adapted to the sector's rapid evolution. Core provisions reported across multiple 2026 hearings: term limits capping how many mandates a president of a sports structure (club, federation, or league) may serve, framed by sponsors as aligning Tunisia with international sports-governance norms; authorization for the creation of professional sports companies (sociétés sportives professionnelles), opening the door to public-private partnership financing intended to diversify and stabilize the sector's funding beyond state subsidies; and provisions aimed at insulating sports-body decision-making from political interference ("aléas politiques"). In May 2026, experts and commentators (via allAfrica) warned the draft could paradoxically threaten the independence of Tunisian sport if not carefully calibrated, given international federations' (FIFA, IOC) own rules against government interference in national sports-body governance. No floor vote, promulgation, or JORT publication located as of this pass; not yet enacted. Confirmed via African Manager and Tunisie Tribune (both citing the March 6, 2026 committee session), Business News (April 30, 2026, "transparence, financement et inclusion"), and Espace Manager, cross-checked against each other.
EducationAdministrativeDraft2026

Draft Law Replacing Decree-Law No. 82-12 (Engineering Profession)

National Council statement: May 4, 2026 (following the OIT's May 1–3, 2026 Sousse council session) | Status: pre-filing advocacy stage — no bill number located
A long-sought replacement for the 1982 decree-law governing the engineering profession, which the Ordre des Ingénieurs Tunisiens (OIT) considers obsolete. Following its May 1–3, 2026 national council session in Sousse ("The Engineer, Pillar of Sovereignty and Development"), the OIT publicly called on the legislative and executive branches to accelerate adoption of the new text, citing deteriorating working conditions and an accelerating "brain drain" of engineering talent as the immediate context for the push. Distinct from the separate, already-transmitted diaspora framework bill the OIT sent to the ARP President on July 16, 2026 — that bill addresses expatriate engineers specifically, while this one would replace the profession's core governing statute in its entirety (title protection, Order structure, registration, and disciplinary regime). No draft text, bill number, or committee referral had been identified as of this pass; confirmed via Business News, L'Économiste Maghrébin, webdo.tn, and Gnet News coverage of the May 4, 2026 statement, cross-checked against each other.
CommercialAdministrativeDraft2026

Draft Law on Games of Chance, Money Games and Sports Betting

Sponsor: Minister of Youth and Sports Sadok Mourali | Status: revised draft presented; not yet filed as a numbered ARP bill as of this pass
A comprehensive overhaul of the 1974 casino/Promosport framework, presented by Minister of Youth and Sports Sadok Mourali as a revised draft law covering games of chance, money games, and sports betting. Framed as adapting Tunisia's gambling regime to international standards, with a particular focus on combating money laundering and safeguarding sports integrity against match-fixing risks tied to betting markets; press coverage describes the overhaul as aiming to actively criminalize unlicensed online gambling outright, addressing a regulatory gap press and legal commentary (including Nawaat's 2023 analysis of the "flou dangereux" — dangerous legal vagueness — around online sports betting) have long flagged around unlicensed offshore operators competing with the state-monopoly Promosport platform. No bill number, ARP filing date, or committee referral located as of this pass. Confirmed via FocusGN and African Manager coverage of the ministerial presentation, cross-checked against each other.
